ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u8	The next item is the debate on draft constitutional law Nos. 1429, 7, 12, 35, 67, 68, 125, 127, 143, 196, 238, 253, 261, 279, 305, 332, 339, 414, 436, 543, 574, 702, 732, 736, 737, 877, 878, 879, 907, 1038, 1057, 1193, 1195, 1264, 1265, 1273, 1274, 1280, 1280, 1281, 1355, 1368, 1392, 1395, 1397, 1406, 1408, 1415, 1416, 1420, 1426, 1427 and 1454. I therefore call the rapporteur, Senator Finocchiaro.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u9	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the draft reform law for Part Two of the Constitution arrives today at the Senate House in a text which is profoundly and - in my opinion - very well engraved on the Commission's examination. After a very in-depth debate, which has also benefited from the technical and doctrinal ideas of the cognitive investigation that preceded it, a text has been reached that, while remaining fully adherent to the cornerstones of the reform, must be considered to be significantly enriched and specified in its overall system. The Commission has worked, in particular, on three lines of action, each of which aims to strengthen, better specifying them, the fundamental objectives of the reform. The first is the redesign of the nature and functions of the Senate, in the framework of a reform which, while placing the second Chamber outside the Government-Parliament Trust Circuit, places it at the centre of the national and supranational constitutional order, in a position of link between the institutional bodies of the European Union, the State and the territorial authorities and between the State and the Community legislature. As a Chamber expressly voted for the competition, training and implementation of Union law, the Senate is also seeking to become - with an absolutely innovative formula on a continental scale - the special place for the exercise of those new and wider prerogatives of participation in the Community decision which the Treaty of Lisbon recognizes as "each of the national parliaments or in each House of one of these parliaments." It will not escape anyone that this structure can be a powerful factor of European integration for our country. The second front on which the Commission has measured itself is the overcoming of equal bicameralism according to a model of constitutionalism that incorporates, adapting it, also the system of guarantees provided by the Constitution in 1948. The effort, in this case, was to address the overall constitutional structure resulting from the reform, having regard to a careful recalibration of all the provisions and institutions placed to guarantee and balance the institutional system. Finally, the Commission's examination focused on the third qualifying aspect of the reform: the revision of Title V of Part II of the Constitution and the reorganization of the system of territorial autonomy, in terms of the division of legislative powers and the respective margins of financial autonomy. In this case, the effort has been to combine, even with the commitments that our country has assumed in supranational context, the valorization of territorial autonomy with the needs of unitary government of public finance. The result was, in the end, a text which, while maintaining its original features, has, after its first parliamentary scrutiny in the Commission, been a port point, already very solid and advanced, for the benefit of the speed and effectiveness of the process of constitutional reform that has begun here. I will take into account, of course, in a schematic form, but I also hope on time, the substance of the text. One of the qualifying aspects of the reform is the overcoming of equal bicameralism, through the definition of a new differentiated bicameral system, which sees the Titular Chamber exclusively as the trusting relationship with the Government and the Senate, as the representative body of the territorial institutions. To this end, Article 55 of the Constitution has been amended, introducing an ex novo list of the functions of each of the two chambers, as differentiated in relation to the new bicameral structure, and a statement on the nature of their representation. If the House is maintained the representation of the nation, as it is linked to the political nature of its mandate, the Senate is reserved for the representation of territorial institutions. With regard to functions, not only the legislative function, but also the function of political direction and control over the work of the Government are expressly attributed to the Chamber, according to an innovative formula compared to the current Constitution, which to date mentions only "the political direction" in Article 95, with reference to the functions and prerogatives of the President of the Council of Ministers. Compared to the original proposal for a law, the text approved in the Commission restores the name of "Senato della Repubblica" instead of "Senato delle Autonomie" and amends, supplementing, the functions assigned to it. In addition to the role of liaison between the State and the other constituent bodies of the Republic (Regions, Metropolitan Cities and Municipalities), participation in the ascending and descending phases of European Union law and the function of verifying the implementation of laws - already provided for in the original text - the Commission's text also assigns to the Senate the functions of evaluating the activities of public administrations and, in general, of monitoring public policies, of strengthening the prerogatives of guarantee and union over the work of the Government recognized in the Second Chamber. In this key, the Senate is recognized as a further and peculiar prerogative of control, in the form of the competition to the expression of opinions on appointments of government competence. With regard to the composition and election of the Senate, the examination carried out in the Commission led to a significant modification of the original plant, starting from the number of members of the Senate that was further reduced. The total number of senators is therefore fixed in 100, of which 74 are elected by the Regional Councils and the Councils of the Autonomous Provinces of Trento and Bolzano among their members, 21 are elected by the same Regional Councils among the mayors of the Municipalities of the Region to the extent of one for each; and finally five senators (in place of the originally planned 21), can be appointed by the President of the Republic among the citizens who have illustrated the homeland for the highest merits in the social, scientific, artistic and literary fields. The latter last seven years and cannot be renamed again. By a standard approved in the Commission under the final provisions, it has also been specified that they cannot in any case exceed the total number of five, taking into account the senators of law and life and the remaining office of life senators already appointed on the date of entry into force of the constitutional law. The same norm also crystallizes in the Constitution the general system of election of senators by the Regional Councils, providing that the seats are allocated by the proportional method. These criteria are in turn identified, for the purposes of the first application of the new framework, as part of the final provisions of the constitutional bill, in order to make the electoral mechanism immediately operational, without the need for subsequent legislative acts. As regards the identification of the procedures for carrying out the electoral procedure, it is subject to a law approved by both Chambers, which also regulates the procedures for replacing senators in the event of termination of the regional or territorial elective office. Finally, it is expected that the distribution of seats between the Regions will be carried out in proportion to their population, as a result of the last census, since no region can have a number of senators less than two. On an amending proposal from the rapporteurs, the Commission has introduced a provision on the incompatibility of senators accessing offices in the Senate bodies. With a novella in Article 63 of the Constitution, the Senate's Rules of Procedure have been put in place to identify cases in which election or appointment to office in the Senate bodies may be limited due to the exercise of governmental, regional or local functions. The intention is to avoid the accumulation of the representation of monocratic institutional bodies of different nature and extraction in the same subject. At the same time, on a proposal from the rapporteurs, a provision has been introduced in the Commission which puts the guarantee of the rights of parliamentary minorities to the Rules of Procedure of the House of Deputies. It is therefore ensured constitutional coverage of regulatory principles that, in various ways, qualify as "status of oppositions." Another innovation introduced in the Commission concerns the duties of Members of Parliament to participate in the meetings of the Assembly and in the work of the Commissions. Again, the constitutional constraint is aimed, firstly, at strengthening the requirements of the parliamentary regulations in the light of a stronger and more direct accountability of the individual Member of Parliament in relation to his institutional duties. With reference to the verification of the powers of the members of the Senate, the examination in the Commission led to the restoration of the judicial function of the Senate in the matter of admission certificates and cases of ineligibility and incompatibility, in place of the mere audit provided for by the Government's text. Article 66 is the basis of the present self-report on the verification of powers, i.e. the conferral on the Chambers of their competence to exercise their judicial function definitively. The subtraction of this competence from the Senate would therefore constitute the denial of one of the forms in which the self-dedication of the Chambers is expressed, as organs today fall - as the Constitutional Court recalls - among those directly involved in the sovereign power of the State, and therefore located at the top of the order, in a position of absolute independence and mutual equality. In the same spirit, the Commission has also provided for the reinstatement of Article 68 of the Constitution in the current text, with recognition to the senators of the same immunity regime already provided for Members. In this regard, the Commission's decision had no merit whatsoever in relation to the appropriateness and adequacy of the scope of parliamentary immunity applicable to senators and Members respectively, but focused solely on the systemic coherence of a differentiation, such as that of the Government's text, which would not appear and appears to be adequately justified. Therefore, an additional reflection may be necessary with regard to the contents and perimeter of immunity for the components of each Chamber. As it is entirely consistent with the new configuration and composition of the Senate, the differentiation between the members of the two Chambers, already provided for by the government bill, in the matter of parliamentary allowances remains unchanged: their payment is limited to members of the Chamber of Deputies. As regards the legislative procedure, the Commission's reading has led to some specific but significant changes in relation to the original plant with regard to Articles 70 and 72. Firstly, the law for which the legislative function is exercised collectively by the two chambers has been extended. The laws of revision of the Constitution and other constitutional laws, already provided for in the text of the Government, have been added to the laws of implementation of the constitutional provisions in the matter of popular referendum; the laws authorising the ratification of the treaties relating to the belonging of Italy to the European Union; the laws in the matter of the organization, electoral legislation, governing bodies and fundamental functions of Municipalities and Metropolitan Cities and the laws with provisions of principle on the associative forms of the Municipalities (for the implementation of Article 117, paragraph 2, letter p) of the Constitution; the law on the fundamental principles of the electoral legislation of the Regions (Article 122 of the Constitution); and finally all the laws for which a reservation of approval by both Chambers is provided in the Constitution. In the light of this enlargement, the Commission has therefore also recalibrated the scope of the subjects for which the text of the reform provides for an enhanced approval procedure - in the terms of a final vote by absolute majority - in the event that the House does not comply with any proposed amendments to the Senate. In the text approved by the Commission, therefore, the draft laws on the following subjects are subject to the enhanced procedure: Rome Capital Law; the government of the territory and civil protection; the exercise by the State of the supremacy clause; the delegation of regulatory power to the Regions; agreements between Regions and foreign States; coordination between the State and the Regions in matters of immigration, public order and security, the protection of cultural goods, financial relations with territorial authorities; the exercise of the government's replacement power, the posting of Municipalities from the Regions. With regard to the draft budget law, the provision of the original text, which provides for the examination of the Senate within fifteen days of the approval of the Chamber, has remained unchanged, without the need to activate the call through the prescribed number of senators. In such a case, the House may not comply with the Senate's proposed amendments, re-approving the text by an absolute majority, but only on condition that the Senate has in turn resolved such proposals by an absolute majority. In the course of the examination, the profile of the politically straightforward nature of the draft budget law and its full relevance to the political responsibility of the Government and its majority can be assessed more punctually. With regard to the parliamentary examination procedure, the amendments made by the Commission focused on the so-called priority examination procedure - introduced ex-novo by the reform - according to which the Government may request the Chamber of Deputies to decide that a draft law be placed with priority on the agenda and put to final vote within 60 days of the request. The Commission, in the first place, has foreseen that these draft laws should be designated by the Government as essential for the implementation of the Government's programme. But above all it has introduced a reserve of normal procedure for the draft laws on constitutional and electoral matters, in the matter of legislative delegation and the conversion of decree-laws, as well as for the laws authorising the ratification of international treaties and the approval of budgets and accounts. These laws therefore preclude access to the priority examination procedure. In this respect, I would like to refer to the comments I made earlier on the politically straightforward nature of the draft budget law. The rapporteur's amendment also introduced an amendment to the provisions of Article 71 concerning the popular legislative initiative aimed at revitalising and strengthening a tool for direct democracy which, although provided for in 1948, was not sufficiently exploited. To this end, if on the one hand the number of subscriptions required for the presentation of the bill of popular initiative has risen to 250,000, on the other hand, it has been introduced, a provision which obliges the Parliamentary Regulations to provide for these measures certain times of examination and final vote. With the same aim of strengthening and enhancing the constitutional instruments of direct democracy, the Commission has also introduced in the text a reform of Article 75 of the Constitution, on the subject of a repealing popular referendum. The main innovation relates to the quorum for the validity of the referendum, which has been lowered and made mobile, in the sense that it is linked to the participation in the last elections of the Chamber, i.e. the number of voters during the last election of the Chamber. In particular, according to the new provision, the proposal subject to a referendum is approved if the majority of the voters who participated in the last election of the Chamber of Deputies participated in the vote and if the majority of the votes cast validly are reached. Against the background of the lowering of the quorum, the number of signatures required for the referendum is raised to 800,000, but the deadline is doubled to six months. Finally, in order to prevent such a broad popular mobilization on questions which may prove inadmissible, it was envisaged that the Constitutional Court's eligibility examination should be carried out within three months of the beginning of the collection, when the referendum request is signed by at least 400,000 citizens. Another qualifying innovation introduced during the examination in the Commission is the possibility of submitting the draft law on the election of the Chambers to a preliminary judgment of legitimacy by the Constitutional Court. The aim of the rule is evidently to avoid the eventuality - which occurred with the 2005 electoral reform - of the repeated exercise of the electoral function on the basis of a discipline burdened by profiles of constitutional illegitimacy, otherwise sanctionable only ex post in a incidental way, with what follows in terms of the alleged loss of authority of the elected Chamber with an illegitimate system. To this end, the text proposed by the Commission provides that the laws governing the election of members of the Chamber and of the Senate may be submitted, before their promulgation, to the Court's prior judgment of constitutional legitimacy, on a reasoned appeal lodged by at least one third of the members of a Chamber and that, in the event of a declaration of constitutional illegitimacy, the law cannot be promulgated. The reform also intervenes on Article 77 on the matter of urgent decrees, with the aim of giving importance as a constitutional rule not only to the constraints imposed by Article 15 of Law No 400 of 1988, but also to the principles now crystallized of constitutional jurisprudence. To this end, it is expected that decree-laws may not regulate constitutional and electoral matters, legislative delegation, authorization to ratify international treaties, approval of budgets and conclusions, nor may they repeat provisions adopted by unconverted decrees or regulate legal relations arising on the basis of them, restore the effectiveness of rules which the Constitutional Court has declared illegitimate for defects not related to the proceedings. It is also provided that the decrees must bear measures of immediate application and specific content, homogeneous and corresponding to the title. The Commission has introduced two significant amendments in the text. The first concerns the presidential postponement of the conversion laws referred to in Article 74 of the Constitution. It is expected that the President of the Republic, before promulgating the law, may ask the Chambers for a new resolution even limited to specific provisions and that, when the request concerns the law of conversion of a decree, the deadline for conversion to law will be deferred for a further thirty days. In this way, the presidential power of referral can be activated in a targeted and selective way, avoiding it being conditioned by the complexity and heterogeneity of the draft laws approved by Parliament or by the requirements for the conversion of decree-laws within the time limits laid down. The other amendment, introduced by the Commission, concerns the admissibility of the amending proposals when converting the decree-laws. It has been foreseen, in particular, that in the course of the examination of the draft law of conversion of decrees-laws, provisions outside the object or purpose of the decree, therefore not homogeneous, cannot be approved. With regard to the prerogatives of the Chambers, as a whole recalibrated in the text in relation to the new bicameral structure, one of the changes introduced by the Commission concerned the power of parliamentary inquiry, which the original proposal reserved only to the Chamber of Deputies. The Commission has decided to restore the right of inquiry to the Senate, albeit limited to matters of public interest relating to territorial autonomy, thus further strengthening the profile of the supervisory body and the guarantee outlined for the second Chamber. The requirements for guarantee and overall rebalancing of the institutional system have also inspired the changes made by the Commission regarding the election of the President of the Republic and the exercise of its role of dissolution of Parliament. With regard to the election, the quorums provided for in Article 83 for the first eight ballots have been raised, providing that only from the ninth ballot - and no longer from the third - is the absolute majority sufficient. The quorum of two thirds, which today is applicable to the first three ballots, remains in place for the first four ballots, while the majority of the three fifths of the Assembly is prepared for the ballots between the fifth and eighth. With regard to the functions of the President of the Republic, in conjunction with the new structure of the Chambers, the text provides that, in the event of his temporary impediment to fulfilling them, he is the President of the Chamber; while in the event of permanent impediment, death or resignation, the President of the Senate is to call the election of the new President and to convene and preside over the Parliament in a joint sitting. I will now outline the contents of the reform of Title V of Part II of the Constitution, on which Senator Calderoli - on whose heroism I do not have to spread given the obvious conditions under which he fulfils his role as rapporteur - will carry out his report (and, I imagine, not only on that part). The composition and functions of the new Senate are closely linked to the new division of legislative power between the State and the Regions, according to a logic that leads to the structural integration of the autonomous bodies in the circuit of Parliament's legislative decision. In fact, the reform of Title V is structured in the bill in a complementary way with that of bicameralism, being directed to make more efficient the relations between the State, the Regions and the local authorities and more functional the criteria of division of legislative and regulatory competences. The fundamental prerequisite on which the reform of Title V starts is the integration of territorial autonomy into legislative policies, made possible by the new composition of the Senate, by the reconfiguration of its role and, in particular, by the new constitutional discipline of the legislative procedure that recognizes the Senate the possibility of affecting the draft laws approved by the Chamber in a strengthened form when they adhere to the areas of greater interest for the territorial authorities. One of the cornerstones of the intervention of reorganization of the territorial levels of government provided by the new reform of Title V is the elimination of the Provinces from the number of bodies of which the Republic is composed. The abolition of the Provinces as constitutionally necessary bodies with their own fundamental functions is thus finally carried out. With reference to the allocation to the Regions of further forms and conditions of autonomy in matters of state competence (now governed by Article 116 of the Constitution), the work of the Commission has profoundly modified the initial approach of the Government which had the complete abolition of that discipline and its replacement by a functioning legislative delegation mechanism as a mere instrument of flexibility of the relationship of competence between the State and the Regions. The Commission, on the other hand, considered it essential to preserve the current forecast of important forms of differentiated regionalism, restoring the third paragraph of Article 116 with the appropriate additions. In particular, the new text of article 116, third paragraph, allows the Regions - excluding those with special autonomy - to be granted further forms and special conditions of autonomy with regard to certain matters of exclusive competence of the State, such as: the organization of justice of peace; general and common provisions on education, education, university education and strategic programming of scientific and technological research; the protection of cultural and landscape goods, general and common provisions on the environment and ecosystem, on cultural activities and on tourism and sports organisation. This allocation, as in the current text, is carried out by the law of the State, at the initiative of the Region concerned, heard the local authorities and in compliance with the principles set out in Article 119, but, according to the Commission, is limited to the regions which have conditions of balance between revenue and expenditure within the framework of their own budget. Finally, it is expected that the law that grants these additional forms of autonomy will be approved by both Chambers, on the basis of a special agreement between the State and the Region concerned. The draft law also provides for a broad revision and rationalization of the legislative powers aimed at removing the uncertainties, overlaps and excesses of conflict that have emerged following the reform of 2001 and which have had significant repercussions both on the relationship between the levels of government that make up the Republic, both from the point of view of the huge constitutional litigation in which they have resulted, and on that of the competitiveness of the country system. Therefore, in the Commission we have tried to overcome - I think the attempt has been rewarded by success - the rigidity and ambiguity of the current structure, characterised by the centrality of the subject matter of concurrent legislation, with a restart that makes the exercise of the legislative power of the State more flexible and more clear and guaranteed the areas within which the regional legislative power can be deployed. The main innovation is the elimination of shared legislative competence and the consequent reorganization of matters of exclusive competence, respectively state and regional. Finally, we believe that the supremacy clause which closes the system has also been improved in the text proposed by the Commission. According to it, state law, on a proposal from the Government which assumes responsibility for it, may intervene in particular cases on matters which are not the exclusive legislative competence of the State and the approved text places as a requirement of its exercise, alongside the protection of the legal or economic unity of the Republic, also the protection of the national interest. Allowing Senator Calderoli to illustrate in particular this part of the reform, I would add that a further innovation has been introduced by the Commission, which has in fact constitutionalized the reference to the so-called standard costs and requirements, already provided for in Law No 42 of 2009, providing that the financing of public functions by regions and local authorities must take place on the basis of cost reference indicators and requirements that promote efficiency conditions. These indicators will therefore constitute, by constitutional constraint, the parameters against which to compare and evaluate the public action of all territorial authorities. Another innovation concerns the exercise of the replacement power referred to in the second paragraph of Article 120 of the Constitution. In order to exercise this power, the obligation to obtain the opinion of the Senate was laid down, which must be rendered within fifteen days; moreover, a similar obligation to obtain the opinion of the Senate prior to the adoption of the decree for the dissolution of the Regional Council and for the removal of the President of the Junta. Finally, the reform provides that, within the framework of the State law laying down the fundamental principles for the election of regional bodies, a limit is to be established on the amounts due to the President of the Regional Council and the other members of the regional bodies, the amount of which may not exceed that of the amounts due to the mayors of the municipal capital of the Region. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, none of us is unaware of the importance and the modifi cation of this reform, which has undoubtedly been the most significant since the beginning of republican history as far as Parliament is concerned. Far beyond the mere reduction in the number of Members, it draws significantly, and in my opinion, the perfect system of bicameralism. Nor does any of us escape the responsibility we are taking in redefining the constitutional structure so deeply. Many times I have wondered what was the right spirit to deal with this work, which is to fulfil the duty to place reform consistently and fully in the republican constitutional tradition and, at the same time, to introduce positive innovation, useful to give impetus to the action of the parliamentary institution. We started from the need to focus on the effectiveness and promptness of the political decision and on the differentiation of the tasks of the two Chambers in order to adapt them to the needs of a much changed country: because of the membership of the Union, and the consequent importance assumed, for the Italian order, by the Community standardization in its various sources; for the role, also legislative, political and institutional assumed by the Regions; for the weight assumed by the Municipalities. We felt the need to respond to the demand for instruments of direct democracy, which rises so significantly from the country, and also to the positive demand for control and verification of the action of powers, beginning with that of the Government. We knew that we had to maintain the system of guarantees that is proper to our constitutional tradition, even in the face of the competing need to assure Italy of stable and lasting governments. Great comfort came to me personally of the rereading of the Acts of the Constituent. I have found the same troubles, sometimes the same conflicts that have crossed and crossed our discussion, identical suggestions and the awareness of many, who also voted in the affirmative, that the perfect bicameral system was, in the given conditions, a "transaction". I use the term not by chance; I found it in a speech by Mr Perassi, who, quoting Cattaneo, defines the law "a transaction" where this means not betraying the reason of one of the parties, but composing it with the others. At the end of the Commission's work and on the eve of this House's work, I answer as I know the initial question: what is the right spirit for tackling the reform? Now I think I know him because he has lived in the commitment with which all his colleagues have worked (and I stress the word 'all'). It is to experience the constitutional duty to innovate when this is indispensable to give strength and effectiveness to the institutions and, therefore, to the country; that of not considering the new what remains from a work of clipping of the existing, which therefore reduces, diminishes, impoverishes, but, on the contrary, an innovation that specifies new functions and new powers, recognizes new guarantees, succumbs to malfunctions, is attentive to always seeking a new, advanced, equilibrium of the system. In this sense, the new vision of the Senate of the Republic, the model with which we overcome the perfect bicameralism that we propose to the House today, is the result of a work of enrichment, clarification and definition of the basic text. A result that is immediately grasped by reading the Commission's text; that is the son - I mean - of the strength and responsibility of parliamentarism. Finally, I would like to thank my colleagues who have worked so actively with Senator Calderoli and with me over the last few months. And I especially thank Dr. Goracci and the Senate Offices. [[Applause]] Without their work and professionalism we would simply not be here this morning. [[Applause from the PD, FI-PdL XVII, NCD, LN-Aut, Aut (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT)-PSI-MAIE and Mixed and Senator Mussini.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u10	I call the rapporteur, Senator Calderoli.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u11	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I am going to make an intervention on my arm, not in the sense of not having a written text, but in the sense of being able to use one arm, having the other blocked. [[Applause from the M5S and FI-PdL XVII Group.]] Therefore, I apologise to you, Mr President, and to my fellow Members for any errors contained in the report and for any difficulties I may find in your reading, but I cannot write, having only one hand available and having had little time to prepare it, since last Saturday they even made me go to the hospital for a cut-out, it was quite complicated, especially for an untechnological one like myself. I was also in doubt whether to make the report or to go back to the broad and comprehensive report by my colleague Mr Finocchiaro, whom I congratulate on the completeness of the report. In the end, however, I felt obliged to do so and report on everything that, like it or not, happened in the Commission, from the beginning to the term of office of the rapporteurs, partly because nobody can say that they did not help reform of the Constitution; only that beyond the hand, I put two vertebrae in it and that I was sorry for more. I felt honored to have been appointed as a majority co-rapporteur, although I belong to the opposition, because this is the fruit of the long time I have spent in my life on reforms. For this appointment, I thank President Finocchiaro and those who shared this choice with you for their trust and courage. And it took a lot of courage to appoint me as a majority rapporteur, because it was a bit like giving a loaded gun to a serial killer and hoping he wouldn't do a massacre. But with pride today I want to remember and reiterate that the first and only overall reform of the entire second part of the Constitution, voted four times by Parliament in 2005, bore my signature as Minister of Reform. The reform was rejected in 2006 by a popular referendum for two reasons: first, because of a political exploitation by the left, which falsely attributed to the reform secessionist aims [[Applause from Senator Malan]] ; secondly, due to a resounding and erroneous tactical choice of the premier and vice-premier of that time, Silvio Berlusconi and Gianfranco Fini, who wanted to have the referendum held after the 2006 general election because they feared that the referendum could have a negative impact on the political elections themselves. This choice had seen myself as an opponent, as Minister, and Professor D'Onofrio, who was then the rapporteur for the reform. Unfortunately, we had seen right both me and Professor D'Onofrio: in fact, the coalition of the center-right lost the political elections of spring 2006 and two months later, consequently, the reform was also rejected in the referendum. I am more than ever convinced that, if we had voted first for the referendum and then for the general elections, today the constitutional reform of 2005 would already be in force and we would have won the general elections of 2006. Today we are talking about a reduction in the number of parliamentarians, an end to bicameralism, a revision of Title V and many other things, but it is good to reiterate strongly that all these issues were contained in the reform of that time, even if that was much better written, both in terms of form and content. [[Applause from the LN-Aut and FI-PdL XVII Groups.]] But let's go back to us. The reform that we are examining today, fortunately ours and the country, is a completely different text from the one initially proposed by the Government, which contained a series of original sins. Firstly, the number of Members was not affected, and 630 Members seem frankly too many. [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group and Senator Zin),]] both in terms of costs and in terms of the balance of the democratic system. Secondly, the Senate, as defined by the Government, had no legislative or control functions, to the point that someone rightly called it a post-work job for governors and mayors. There was general talk of a link between state and territorial autonomy, but it was clear to everyone that it would not connect anything. It was absolutely clear that a Senate with these characteristics had no sense of existence and that it would be better to suppress it altogether. Thirdly, its composition would then have been laughable, if not to mourn: for a large part it was made up of members of law with regional governors and mayors of the capital cities of Region or Autonomous Province. So we are talking about 42 members of the law, to which 21 senators of nomination of the President of the Republic were incomprehensible. I'd like to know who the genius who wrote this nonsense is and I'd like you to explain to us what 21 presidential nomination senators have to do with a Senate of Autonomouss: a real joke. [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group and the Senators Campanella and Di Maggio.]] But even more to be laughed comes in reading the distribution of senators between the various Regions: Lombardy, Region of almost ten million inhabitants, would have had six senators, or two less than the eight attributed to Trentino-Alto Adige, which has little more than one million inhabitants. Fourthly, the method of indirect election of the Senate is incomprehensible and without real democratic participation of the citizens. Fifth, not only on the reform of Parliament, things did not work: Title V was also an aberrant thing. We were faced with a return to total centralism, to the point that the Constitution of 1947 seemed far more autonomous and federalist than the one proposed by the Renzi Government. I know I didn't say something good, but the Albertine Statute was much more autonomous than the Government's proposal, only that then there was the king, today the king is not there, or rather it shouldn't be there. [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group and the Bell and Di Maggio Senators]] , but democratically, things risked getting even worse. Sixthly, the third paragraph of Article 116, on so-called federalism at variable speed, had been abolished, so that the region would continue to have the same competences as the more advanced and efficient ones. Seventhly, in Article 117, the competing matter was removed and brought back in full under the exclusive authority of the State; the Regions would have remained the so-called residual matter, or nothing. After having mistakenly and falsely abolished the Provinces, in fact the reform of the Government would have abolished the Regions from the legislative point of view, transforming them into mere administrative bodies. In this respect, one of the two amendments which I did not agree with Mr Finocchiaro is that relating to large-scale agencies. [[Applause from Senator Endrizzi]] , because using the word "enti" it is evident that the suppression of the Provinces is a pure fiction [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group and the Senators Di Maggio and Zuffada]] ; in fact, in doing so, the Provinces will continue to exist, changing only the name on the facade of the palace: ==References====External links==* Official website In the eighth place, also Article 119, that is, that which speaks of revenue and expenditure of the territorial authorities, as modified by the Government's proposal, would have meant that once again the wasteful agencies would have been rewarded and the virtuous entities would have been penalized. In the ninth place, beyond the individual articles, what was completely lacking in the Government's proposal was a balance of system, weights and counterweights. The combined arrangement of reducing the number of senators, maintaining the current number of MPs, and an electoral law, such as Italicum, which would allow a party to 20% to achieve an absolute majority of seats, thanks to the role of dumb servants of allied parties that were not to reach 4.5%, would have delivered into the hands of a party but rather a relative majority, but a minority in the country, the Presidency of the Council, the Presidency of the House, the Presidency of the Republic, a large part of the members of the Constitutional Court and the Supreme Council of the judiciary, as well as the power to appoint the leaders of the guarantee bodies, i.e. a real regime. In view of all this, I and President Finocchiaro, taking into account the truly general, broad and participatory debate, have prepared a unified text to be proposed as a basic text to the Commission, taking into account the Government's proposal, those of individual parliamentarians or political forces and the findings that emerged in the debate. The government, with inopportune stubbornness, demanded that its text be adopted without modification, thus mortifying the work done by the rapporteurs and commissioners. In order to protect the dignity and role of Parliament, I have, as a surprise, tabled a single agenda for my signature, based on twenty points, which committed the Commission to completely rewriting the Government's bill. That agenda, despite the government's opposition, was approved. All that followed was not a good page in parliamentary history. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] : Two majority senators of the Commission were replaced by their respective political groups, because they refused to vote on my agenda, with good peace under Article 67 of the Constitution. I am sorry for them and for democracy, but fortunately, nineteen out of twenty items on that agenda have been incorporated into the text that the Commission is proposing today for the House. The only item on the agenda that has not been accepted is that relating to the direct election of the Senate, which has always seen me favorable, because in Article 1 of the Constitution it is said that sovereignty belongs to the people. [[Applause from the LN-Aut, M5S and FI-PdL XVII Groups and Senator D'Alì]] and not to regional councillors or mayors. However, if the overall approach of the reform found in the Commission were to stand up, I will not oppose an indirect election, provided, however, that the elective moment is really there, which is not taken for granted, since last Thursday, thanks to the agreements between the Government and Forza Italia, the indirect election had also disappeared and had been replaced by nomination mechanisms, which had nothing to do with democracy. That very day I was still hospitalized in resuscitation and I ran away from the hospital so that I could come to the Senate and stop that unclean act that was consummating on the altar of the Nazarene agreement. [[Applause from the LN-Aut and M5S Groups.]] No bilateral agreement, no political convenience, no personal convenience or, worse still, judicial can put democratic systems at stake, so it was worth putting your health at risk to put things right. But what has changed compared to the original text - President Finocchiaro has made a perfect illustration and so I will not go into too much detail - and what still needs to be changed? The Senate has returned to being a real branch of Parliament, even if it no longer expresses confidence; it has summed up important legislative functions, control functions, guarantee functions and represents a real link between the State and territorial autonomy. The Senate, with its 100 senators, of which 95 are elected, becomes a synthesis of the US Senate and the German Senate, even if the decision between direct and indirect election will have to be this Assembly. And - live God! - the 21 senators of presidential nomination have disappeared; but not only: even the five remaining will last in office seven years, they are all-inclusive in terms of number and in the current state - I think no one has noticed - you have also forgotten to give him the salary, so they will no longer receive anything. The quorums for the election of the President of the Republic have been raised, even if it will be necessary to achieve a greater balance between the numbers of the Chamber of Deputies and those of the Senate than those today fixed. The possibility of prior appeal to the Constitutional Court on electoral laws by the two fifths of the members of each Chamber was also provided for: this would have prevented so many mistakes of the past. This is an appeal which, as far as my personal point of view is concerned, I would extend to other cases. The issue of direct democracy was then addressed, even if I think the subject is still perfect. There has been an increase in the number of signatures for proposals for popular initiative legislation, but it has been foreseen that parliamentary regulations should provide for certain deadlines for the conclusion of the debate. The quorum has been lowered for the validity of the repealing referendums of popular initiative, but increasing to 800,000 the number of signatures of the signatories of the referendum proposal, and also providing, at an altitude of 400,000 signatures, the pronunciation of eligibility by the Constitutional Court on the referendum question. It is true that six months have been spent collecting signatures instead of the current three, but I still believe that 800,000 signatures are really excessive and that the number should be reconsidered during the examination of the proposal. [[Applause from the LN-Aut, M5S and Misto-ILC Groups]] But let us now turn to Title V of Part II of the Constitution. With the re-introduction of the third paragraph of Article 116, the so-called federalism at variable speed has returned to life: that is, what allows the regions, which have already proved to be able to govern well and virtuously, to acquire additional legislative functions on condition, however - as already mentioned - that they are in balance of budget. Article 117, that is, what defines the legislative powers of State and Regions, has been completely rewritten, defining once and for all what are the exclusive competences of the State and those that are of the Regions, establishing once and for all the famous and hoped for by all who do what they do. One thing I still do not like in Article 117: the so-called state supremacy clause that, as planned, allows the State to cancel the legislative power of the Regions in their competences even in regions where there is no need for it. The clause, as set out in the text today, seems to me to recall the famous saying that it was read behind the counter of certain commercial activities: "Because of someone no more credit is given to anyone." [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group]] It would be paradoxical, for example, that if one or two regions were not able to guarantee the essential levels of health or social assistance, they would want to intervene on all the regions and not on those two: in the face of federalism! This will have to be settled. Excellent work has been done on Article 119, which is the article on the revenue and expenditure of territorial authorities: for the first time, the concept of cost-reference indicators and requirements that guarantee the best efficiency practices, i.e. the so-called standard costs, has been included in the Constitution. Never again will any government - as did that of the never regret Monti - be able to cancel or suspend the application of fiscal federalism. On this subject I feel like giving advice to my friend Renzi: despite the government continues to deny economic manoeuvres, everyone, but everyone, even the major economic experts, know that, in the face of current public accounts, a maneuver from 20 to 27 billion will be needed. You will tell me that it is not true and the good Renzi, who is certainly very smart, the maneuver will not do it, but however he will have to make the law of stability and the knots then will come to the comb: the missed cuts of the Letta Government, which are estimated at 4-5 billion; the spending reviews of Bondi and Cottarelli, that have been lost in the street; the emerging expenses that, every year, they have to face, and that they have to face all, that are estimated at 5-6 billion; the partial coverage of the 80 euros for 2014; the total coverage of the 80 euros in 2015, already do, making the accounts of the servant, well more than 20 billion. To avoid this manoeuvre, Renzi will have to become the most passionate supporter of standard costs and fiscal federalism, because only this tool will allow him to recover those tens of billions not going to pick them up from the pockets of citizens or businesses, but through a targeted cut of waste and inefficiencies of the country system. However, I do not want to get lost in details that can best be deepened during the examination of the articles and their amendments, now I want to make some reflections on what still needs to be done in relation to my personal opinion. The first is that, in order to re-establish a true and balanced system of weights and counterweights, the number of Members must be re-examined; it must not be seen by anyone as an action against the other branch of Parliament but, objectively, 630 Members are really too many, not only in terms of costs, but above all in terms of balances: a Parliament in common sitting that should include a Senate represented by 100 members against the 630 Members of the House would become a Bulgarian Parliament. One proposal that I would reasonably like to make is to reduce the number of Members to at least 500, not only because that was the number envisaged in my 2005 reform, but because this number represents the relationship between citizens and elected people that applies in France and in Western democracies to us most alike. The second is that, if this House wants to open the debate on Rule 68, that is, on parliamentary immunity, this will have to concern both the Chamber of Deputies and the Senate of the Republic; I would also be in favour of the total abolition of immunity, provided that this is done for both branches of Parliament; alternatively, I continue to be convinced that the body that has to deal with it must be a third party and the maximum expression of guarantees, i.e. the Constitutional Court. The third is that in the field of legislative procedure, that is, the competences of the Senate, it seems frankly absurd and curious that the Senate cannot deal with the great ethical issues that concern science and morals; just as unruly it seems to me that the Senate cannot decide on the coordination of public finance: so, if this House decides to foresee it, I am also willing to discuss the competences of the Senate on Article 81, i.e. on the budgets. Let us try with a little good will to find a healthy and just balance. The fourth is that on Article 117 I believe that some filing is still necessary, but without any disruption, because if someone had the temptation to bring everything back to the state, he already knows that the reform would stop and everything would go to 48 cards. I obviously do not want to threaten anyone but I do not want to be fooled by anyone either: if the pacts are respected the reform takes flight, otherwise the reform crashes. The fifth reflection is that we deliberately, and in agreement with the co-rapporteur, did not want to touch on Article 138 in the Commission, namely the referendums on constitutional matters, precisely in order to be able to discuss them in this House, but we are both convinced that the referendums on constitutional reforms should be able to take place regardless of the majority with which Parliament approved the reform and if this House agrees we will proceed to this. The sixth is that on the direct or indirect election of the Senate I have already expressed myself and the decision will be up to this House, even if, in the electoral law under regime, we must remember the protection of linguistic minorities, which today is absent in the text to our examination. However, I feel that I have great doubts about the transitional rule for the first election of the Senate of the Republic. As it is today configured, it foreshadows a serious injury to Article 51 of the Constitution, which reads: "All citizens of one or the other sex shall have access to public offices and elective offices under equal conditions, in accordance with the requirements laid down by law." I will try to explain better: a constitutional law cannot attribute ex post, i.e. ex post, the right of active and passive electorate to regional councillors who had been voted by voters to be the pure and simple regional adviser and not the senator or the great senator electorate. [[Applause from the M5S Group and Senator Casaletto.]] Therefore, a constitutional law cannot prevent an outgoing citizen or senator from running for office in the Senate, since they did not know that in order to do so they would first have to be elected as regional councillor, which is, among other things, incompatible with a senator. [[Applause from the M5S Group and Senator Buemi]] I know this is a complicated thing to understand, but let us not write with the transitional rule a reform of the second part of the Constitution that does not respect the first part of the Constitution itself, because, paradoxically, we would be writing an unconstitutional reform. The amendment that deals with this matter is one of the two that I did not sign, not sharing it with my colleague, Mr Finocchiaro, but as rapporteur I intend to table an amendment in this regard. Frankly, in the transitory part - but I fear that this may happen under the regime - I once again review the famous blocked lists. For eight years I have been massacred for writing a law with blocked lists; now, once with Italicum, once with the electoral law for the Senate, the blocked lists are raised. So don't say it's my fault for the blocked lists anymore. [[Applause from the M5S and Misto-ILC Groups]] Finally, what is missing from this reform and I regret it? Reform of the form of government and justice. [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group and Senator Bulgarelli]] But let's take it one step at a time. I do not want to introduce new arguments that would risk slowing down or stopping this reform. In Bergamo it is said: "Pietost de nient, l'è mei il pietost"; but in addition to flying high and talking about great reforms, we immediately return with our feet on the ground and to fly lower. We quickly approve this reform and try to do it well, but then we come back to thinking about how to solve the real problems, those related to the crisis, how to prevent companies from closing, how to save jobs and create new ones, how to give hope to our young people, how to get families to the end of the month and not let each of us live the nightmare of unsustainable taxes. These are the biggest and most challenging reforms that we will have to face, even if, by approving the reform we are examining, we are laying the foundation for all these problems. [[Applause from the LN-Aut, PD, FI-PdL XVII, M5S and NCD Groups]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u12	The minority rapporteur, Senator De Petris, requested permission to carry out the oral report. No comments were made and the request was accepted.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u13	I therefore call the minority rapporteur, Senator De Petris.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u14	Mr President, today's draft constitutional reform reached the debate in this House does not find the consensus of the Senators of the Mixed Left Group, Ecology and Freedom and of a very large number of other Senators, who have tried in the Commission's work to make changes and try to arrive at a project that has its own solidity. The rapporteur, Mrs Finocchiaro, said that she had sought comfort in the work of the Constituent and many of us have tried to reread many important steps of that work in recent days. Of course, for me and for many of us who have worked in the Commission, one issue has always been very clear: the democratic system, as the constituent Fathers gave it to us, is characterized by weights and balances, combinations of representation, pluralism and governance, which must have their internal coherence, in order to prevent the democratic system from coming out completely unbalanced. For this reason, in dealing with the key issue, that is to say the overcoming of bicameralism, many of us have put to the attention of the Commission's work a project, on the contrary, several projects that have been confronted and cross-referenced, which, in my opinion, certainly have a system coherence, precisely from the point of view of balances, absolutely solid. As far as we are concerned, in giving a very negative opinion on this project, which is now coming to the attention of the House, in the Commission, and we will do so again at this beginning of the debate in this House, we wanted to put forward a number of issues which we believe would have been consistent from the point of view of the democratic system. Mr President, fellow senators, there has been a long debate in the Constitution on monocameralism, but it is clear to everyone that that monocameralism, carried out by some and later debated in the country, had in itself the very sense of balance. Although it only envisaged a Chamber, it was actually representative, elected by proportional electoral law and thus capable of guaranteeing the balance of the system itself, of the relationship between Parliament and the Government. It is therefore clear that in approaching the theme of overcoming equal bicameralism it was and is necessary to have in mind a framework for balancing the system. Instead, the product of the government's initiative - I mean frankly - is already a vulnus of constitutional review processes. We believe that the government's initiative and pressure for the approval of the basic text in the Commission represent a real imposition and therefore a vulnus in the process of constitutional revision, which had been conceived and must, in fact, be Parliament's exclusive heritage. The result of the process of overcoming the perfect bicameralism through the reform of the Senate, considered by the speakers the key proposal, if it were approved by the Senate as it is, would be very heavy from the democratic point of view. From this point of view, I immediately come to the heart of the matter and therefore to the imbalances produced by the text approved by the Commission. The issue of bicameralism could be dealt with through the node of the diversification of functions between the two chambers and the reduction of the number of parliamentarians. Our proposal was very clear - there were a lot of them in this direction - and it was intended to reduce the number of Members to 450 (other proposals included 315 Members) and senators to 150 or even 100, as was the case in the Commission's final proposal. In this way a system was created in which the Senate, directly elected, no longer competed for the relationship of trust with the Government but were attributed functions of high qualification, called by us as guarantee and control, all the more important because, in our vision, the Upper House was detached from the relationship of trust of the citizens. It is clear to everyone that the presupposition of such a bicameralism cannot fail to see always and only the respect (as far as we are concerned, but violated by the proposal that is now under discussion in this House) of Article 1 of the Constitution. Dear Mr President, unfortunately we did not swear on the Constitution when we became senators and Members. But let us remember that Article 1 of the Constitution says that sovereignty is of the people and that the legislative function, exercised by Parliament, is based on the fact that the elected are directly elected by the people. We, on the other hand, are with a Senate that remains a series of legislative functions, but that will no longer have direct legitimacy from the citizens. A Senate composed of 95 elected at regional councils, between regional councillors and mayors (with a second-level election which, as we will then see in the same transitional rule, will in fact no longer be an election but a proper appointment) and a Chamber of Deputies of 630 Members. As the rapporteur, Mr Calderoli, has also said, it is clear that there is already a serious problem of balance here. Article 1 is infringed. However, the Senate will contribute to the constitutional revision laws and, even, to the constitutional revision process, those who have not been directly elected by the citizens will participate. Our Constitutional Charter, even the first part of the Constitution, can be revised without those participating in the constitutional revision process having an express legitimacy on the part of the citizens themselves. The imbalance which, when the constitutional revision is carried out, we must always calculate and keep in mind (because we know that democracy is made up of weights and counterweights), stems from the fact that we will have a number of 100 senators compared to 630 Members. This fact will have, on the other tasks that are attributed to this type of Senate, a very obvious imbalance effect. In the election of the President of the Republic the Senate will obviously have a rather small role. Because, Mr President, this draft reform of the Senate and overcoming the perfect bicameralism, we must frame it and read it in the other ongoing reform process, which is that of the electoral law. All this process takes place while a law (the so-called Italicum) has been passed in the House that has a strong majority distortion and that will ensure, for the type of thresholds and barriers, that, for the access to the majority prize, a party, even with only 25%, could have an absolute majority of seats. Thus, in fact, with this combined arrangement of a Senate so reduced and no longer elected by citizens and the strongly majority distortion of Italicum, a single party will be able to elect the President of the Republic alone, the judges of the Constitutional Court and the CSM. [[Applause from the Mixed-SEL and M5S Groups.]] It will be able, in fact, to determine heavily and totally the election of the guarantee bodies. Everyone sees the fundamental issue that we Senators must bear in mind - and you also know, Mr President, that you are so attentive to these aspects - when we are preparing for such a profound constitutional review process, namely the balance of the democratic system, which, only by this example, falls, because it is a completely unbalanced system. I have learned, modestly, that the history of constitutionalism is both ancient and modern, it is the exercise of the legal technicality of limiting the power of the sovereign, not of his freedom, as we are trying to do in fact with this reform. We are not here, Mr President, for a constitutional revision which contradicts the two cornerstones of democratic constitutionalism, the limitation of power and the legitimacy of power and democracy that comes from the people. [[Applause from the M5S and Mixed-SEL Groups]] : these two aspects, as I said, are being called into question with the reform under consideration. The rewriting of Article 55 of the Constitution represents another vulnus, because the Senate, thus composed, will no longer represent the nation, in its unity and that of its general interests, but only and only the whole or recomposition of territorial interests. The composition of 95 members, 74 elected between regional councillors and mayors, five presidential nominations, by its nature and due to the situation in our country will pose a question of homogeneity. In fact, in every regional council there is a different electoral law: we have electoral laws with blocked lists, others where there is preference and others where the President has another list of nominations on his list. The source of legitimacy, therefore, is no longer unique and direct, i.e. it no longer belongs to the people through direct election, therefore this also raises a question of homogeneity, in addition to the violation of the general rules on the passive electorate, the possibility and the right of access of all citizens to public office. In fact, perhaps we are asking questions that do not concern or fall outside the intention or philosophy of those who strongly wanted this kind of approach to Senate reform, because what we want to achieve is a Senate of appointed people, who will be chosen within each regional council. In fact, if the majority party in each regional council chooses the complacent minority at that time we will have the same final result also in the composition of the Senate. I have heard of pluralism, but seeing the mechanism and the transitional rule, I do not understand how it can be implemented with this kind of Senate composition and with this method of election. We will therefore have an uneven legitimacy, as I said, and the non-stability of the organism and, to prove it, I use the following example, relating to what we are facing at the moment: the constitutional revision begins with the new Senate, which participates in the laws of constitutional revision, then arrives at the House and, when it returns, it can be changed in its composition. In fact, one or more regional councils may have been dissolved for many reasons or have reached a natural maturity and therefore be awaiting elections and consequently will have changed the regional councilors acting as senators and we will then have a composition of the Senate in fact variable. As you can see, we also come to the subject of a number of issues which, if we really wanted to implement a serious reform for the modernisation of the system and its efficiency, would have had to lead to tackling it in a different way, which we did not want to undertake and none of us ever had an answer on why. I am referring to the reduction of Members of Parliament and two Houses with very different functions: a Senate of Warranty and Control and a House that gives confidence and carries out a wider legislative function. Instead, stubbornly, you choose the way of the nominations. Thus, if the type of path undertaken on Italicum continues, for which the lists will be a little shorter but will always be blocked lists, we will have handed over the entire system of representation - I repeat - into the hands of very few, who will choose the elected and the appointed, and, with the majority prize, we will de facto hand in the hands of the majority party also the election of the President of the Republic. An amendment by Senator Gotor was adopted in the Commission, which would have liked to mitigate this aspect in some way, so that the President of the Republic elected himself by an absolute majority of votes from the eighth vote onwards. You understand very well, Mr President, that this amendment is a little warm cloth and that the issue is not directly addressed, and it cannot be addressed because there is this great disproportion between the number of Members and that of the senators and this vulnus, resulting from the fact that we, here in the Senate, operate this constitutional revision and are already booked, immediately afterwards, to conclude the process of electoral reform such as Italicum, on which I believe that the Consulta will still have much to say since the issues raised by the Constitutional Court, in my opinion and in the opinion of many constitutionalists, have not been resolved at all. Mr President, I think we still have an opportunity in this House to do a good job, if every senator will take care of his role in the constitutional revision by heavily correcting these distortions, by changing the composition of the Senate and the system of election, by giving the word back to the citizens, by reaffirming a principle that we cannot violate: we cannot reform Part II of the Constitution if the founding principles of Part I are affected and this is being done. The second issue that we should have been wisely addressing is that we have a crisis of representation and of the relationship between citizens and institutions that is profound, very profound. There is a total detachment and we have seen it from the percentages of the citizens who go to vote. Well, I think it should be an imperative for all of us to find ways to strengthen representation and rebuild a relationship between citizens and institutions. It is a matter of politics and parties and we should have taken care of this aspect, but what is done? We intervene on institutions of popular initiative and direct participation, always with the excuse of innovation that has now become a constitutional value of Part I (innovation end in itself) we raise from 50,000 to 250,000 the number of signatures that serve for the bill of popular initiative, without having any kind of guarantee, if not generic, in the times and ways of discussion and approval. It was possible to introduce, on the basis of the debate on the laws of popular initiative, the proposed referendum; it could and can be ensured that, by means of an automatic mechanism, if a law of popular initiative does not reach the work of the Commission and of the House, as the history of these fifty years shows, then after a certain time we arrive at the proposed referendum. On the other hand, I believe that this issue has been dealt with superficially and in fact limiting, once again, the possibility of direct participation of citizens. I now come to the question of the repealing referendum. Certainly there has been an improvement because the quorum established is no longer on the whole audience of those entitled, but on the majority - it will therefore be a variable quorum - in relation to the number of voters in the last parliamentary elections. But in fact, it brings up to 800,000 signatures the necessary number, which actually means a million signatures. This corresponds to the number of votes obtained by Europeans by some political force that has overcome the barrier with little more than one million votes, which for a referendum should mobilize its entire electorate. It also introduces the element of the early eligibility judgment half-collecting signatures (i.e. 400,000) to arrive at the eligibility judgment. We believe that this number is too high and this is once again a strong limitation of citizens' right to direct participation. President, I close on Title V of Part II of the Constitution. Title V and reforms are the example of what the debate on reforms in our country has been and what it means to give life to hasty and thoughtless reforms. The spirit of Article 138 in the Constitution is instead that of weighting, calmness and reflection, exactly the opposite of what is happening now. President, we have already had experiences of hasty constitutional reforms that have caused problems and failures in the country. One of these concerned Title V. Today we are taking action again, but we are causing other problems with a process of centralization on certain subjects which we believe must still be the subject of discussion and agreement with the territories and regions. I am referring to major infrastructure and energy networks. [[Applause from the M5S and Mixed-SEL Groups]] It is not by chance that we want to refocus these choices and remove them completely from the evaluation of territorial communities. We believe that action could be taken by avoiding and returning to a series of failures. For example, I find it incredible that when the essential levels of civil and social rights are incorporated into the Constitution, we forget - this is another violation of Article 3 of the Constitution - the principle of substantial equality. In fact, no one thinks that it is necessary to talk about equal levels of performance, according to the principle of equality of civil and social rights throughout the country. Mr President, I hope in this House and would like to remind all the senators that we must work freely, without party or group orders, but according to our conscience. Faced with the fact, as has been said here, that this is propaganda as a major constitutional revision project, I think that this is certainly a profound revision project, but it affects some basic elements of the democratic system, which together with the electoral law will in fact lead to the annulment of pluralism within Parliament and will result in failures which we risk repenting very soon. I therefore make one final appeal, recalling what I said earlier about the history of constitutionalism: we are not here to guarantee the sovereign's freedom. Let us also put the so-called guillotine in the Constitution, Mr President! [[Applause from the M5S Group.]] It is not enough that all parliamentary activity is subject only and only to decree-laws, because this happens, but instead of intervening to limit them strongly, another instrument is inserted in the Constitution for the Government that is that of the preferential lane, introducing the so-called guillotine in the Constitution. We say that we are not here to make a constitutional revision to guarantee the sovereign's freedom. We, I always say this to the Minister, are a little old, in this we are a little conservative, that is, passionate about popular sovereignty, and we think that in the democratic system to proceed to the revision of the Constitution means first of all to guarantee a system of limitation of the sovereign's power and, first of all, the pluralism of representation, the possibility - certainly - of governability, but with respect for the relationship between Parliament, democratic pluralism and government, and to widen the spaces of direct participation of citizens. [[Applause from the Mixed-SEL, M5S and Mixed-ILC Groups]] Mr President, I ask for permission to submit the written report to the Minutes of today's sitting.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u15	The Presidency shall authorise it accordingly. I communicate that a number of questions have been referred for a preliminary ruling. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u16	Colleagues, what we are going to decide in the near future is of importance that I certainly do not have to stress, but for this very reason I feel obliged to remind everyone of some of the thoughts that have been made in the Commission too. This concept of justice is the one that represents the Constitution in two parts: the first, the one that contemplates the principles, and the second, the one that presents the organizational norms.
