ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u17	Mr President, we believe that the election of second instance is a violation of Article 1 of the Constitution, because the people, who have sovereignty, are deprived of the right to exercise it in one of the fundamental elements, namely the election of their representatives. This fact also determines in fact the dequalification of a branch of Parliament, because its legitimacy no longer comes from the direct election of citizens. The transformation of the Senate from elective to non-elective, since this new Senate would maintain forms, albeit weakened, of legislative power, but also the election of important constitutional bodies and participation in the processes of revision of the Constitution, deprives the elector of the power of direct legitimacy of the legislative process. The progressive detachment of citizens from the institutions in this way is substantially increased and intensified, where it would have been more appropriate to strengthen the institutions of direct democracy, also in view of the great demand for participation that comes from the people. Instead, institutions of direct democracy are mortified. The presentation of the laws of popular initiative can be made, if this bill passes, no longer by 50,000 but by 250,000 signatories. Moreover, the initiative to revise the Constitution, in a parliamentary Republic, is exclusively the responsibility of the Chambers and does not concern the political direction of the majority. The Government's bill No. 1429, on the other hand, creates the risk - I would say more than a risk - of procedural distortions resulting from the government's initiative, such as in the case of recourse to the emergency procedure or as well as the imposition of the question of trust on its approval, which would be a real aberration. On the other hand, in this respect it is illuminating a sentence made with reference to the constitutional revision procedure by the President of the Council of Ministers, when he said that it is now important to arm the majority. Moreover, the effect of the proposed reform of bicameralism, combined with the amendment of the electoral law (with the Chamber of Deputies with a hyper-majority vocation) and the strengthening of the prerogatives of the Government in Parliament, is likely to lead not only to distorting effects of the balanced relationship between the powers of the State, but also to an intolerable narrowing of political representation through a system of barrier thresholds that penalises the smaller parties excessively and which, as a result, leads to a very large social and political exclusion, which will make it more difficult to resolve social conflicts, which, especially in recent years, are dramatically becoming unsettling. Furthermore, in the text we are examining, there is a risk of a forced simplification of the political framework, which is also contrary to the principle of popular sovereignty, which is the basis of the constitutional order and which postulates the maximum valorization of political, institutional and social pluralism. Basically, social conflicts are being extra-parliamentaryized. The allocation of a preferential lane to the draft laws indicated by the Government as priorities is part of an overall framework clearly oriented to favour governance at the expense of representation. Now, that governance is one of the needs of this country is beyond doubt. The problem is how much representation and balance of powers can be sacrificed to this need. Among other things, detailed deadlines and procedures for the examination of government measures are specified in the Constitution, which could instead have been governed by parliamentary regulations, as has been established - for example - for proposals for popular initiative laws. The problem is that perhaps you consider the government's bill more important than those deriving from the citizens' initiative. It would be at least appropriate to limit the list of matters removed from the priority procedure in order to introduce elements of guarantee in favour of all political formations, both majority and minority, against possible authoritarian torsions, at this time not present, but still possible, which may not be entirely averted, unfortunately considered our history. The Government, with this constitutional bill, also proposes to amend the provisions contained in Titles I, II, III, V and VI of Part II of the Constitution and in the Final Provisions. It is a constitutional law with a dissimilar content that, if it were to come to the confirmation referendum - as we hope - it would violate popular sovereignty and freedom of vote, since it would force voters to vote on the whole text only one vote, even though the changes to the Constitution are varied and disparate. And this is also contrary to the inspiration of the norm, approved in the Commission, in which it is stated that in the course of the examination of the draft law of conversion into law of the decree-laws, provisions outside the object or purpose of the decree-law cannot be approved. The homogeneity pursued in the discussion of decree-laws and their conversion is not taken into account when citizens have to assess what is a real upheaval of the institutional order. I am beginning to conclude, highlighting an important fact. In doctrine there is unanimity on the following point: the power must be divided between several subjects and organs in such a way that none of them is in a position to overpower others. The plurality of the constitutional bodies means that they are mutually independent and are in a position of balance so as to effectively guarantee the role assigned to each of them. Democracy cannot end up in a mere structure of government, but more generally it concerns the correlation between society and the state. One of the corollaries of democracy is the need, in order to counter an excessive concentration of power, to improve the instruments of the people's participation in political decisions, to extend the judicial remedies for the protection of fundamental rights and to implement a pluralistic structure that favours a wide decentralisation of functions. In their opinion, the Constituents have been able to build a system based on weights and counterweights able to function perfectly, capable of resisting the test of time and profoundly different contexts. The text before us, in conjunction with the text of the electoral reform already approved by the House, determines, as a consequence of the different composition of the two chambers, both the considerable diversity of powers, and the enormous majority prize provided for by the Chamber of Deputies, the only one entitled to vote for the Government, a sort of de facto mono-cameralism dominated by a coalition of parties not legitimized by the majority of voters, among other things, without counterpowers and with an exponential increase of new powers determined by the combined provision previously stated. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. Please conclude. CAMPANELLA (Misto-ILC). I'm gonna finish. I propose not to proceed with the examination of this act because, for everything I have described before, it is going to upset not only the institutional order, but also the spirit underlying our Constitution, which, in the attribution of sovereignty to the people and in the balancing of powers, finds the elements that denote it and do what our State has managed to hold up in these decades since the post-war period. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u21	De Cristofaro (Misto-SEL). Mr President, the Mixed SEL Group will vote in favour of the questions referred for a preliminary ruling. Of course, in the general debate, I will have time to speak in more detail on the reasons why I feel that this constitutional reform presented by the Government is wrong and counterproductive. In any event, the reasons why we wish to vote in favour of the questions referred for a preliminary ruling are very serious questions of method and substance. As a method, so to speak, also because we consider it very significant that this process of constitutional revision, which should normally compete in the Chambers, comes from a government initiative. This, in our opinion, represents a real processical twist; it is indeed as if history had taught us little. Over the course of time and of many passages of democracy in this country, some constitutional reforms presented in the eyes of the country with a unanimous choir that made it difficult, for those who opposed it, to propose arguments of dissent and criticism regarding what the unique thought was supporting, we have seen how often, over the years, have demonstrated many strong and clear criticalities. I am thinking, for example, of the reform of Title V of Part II of the Constitution, which was also launched at the time with great haste and also in that case with a great element of propaganda behind it. I am also thinking of what, more recently, meant the reform of Article 81 of the Republican Constitution, which Parliament, in a virtually unanimous way, approved, without even listening to the wise suggestion that came from outside Parliament, not to do so by a two-thirds majority, thus allowing a constitutional referendum to be held on that subject. Well, Parliament did not want to listen to the kind of reminder that came from significant circles of civil society; it went on all the way, approved the law on balance in the Constitution, amending Article 81 and the results of that bad choice today are before everyone's eyes. We would not want then that, exactly as happened for Title V and Article 81, once again the haste was bad counselor. The haste, as has also been said in the minority report, is the opposite of the spirit of Article 138, also because I think I can say that if there is a real reflection to be put on the ground in a reasoning like this, it is exactly about the state of democracy of our country. I would like to ask myself all about this, including my colleagues in the majority, including those who will be voting in favour of this reform, although, of course, I hope that in the course of these months, already from this debate in the Senate, amendments can be made. It is clear that there is a need for a process of change and that there is a need for a process of profound self-form of politics and institutions. The point, however, is to wonder, given the state of difficulty in democracy in our country, whether what is presented today is actually an antidote to the crisis, a way out of the crisis of democracy and representation, or whether, as we fear, there is a profound risk of worsening even more the spaces of democratic agibility and positive tension that can be in our country. In this regard - I would like to say this very clearly and I also heard it in the report by Senator Finocchiaro - the issue of popular sovereignty cannot be just one of the points we are dealing with today, it cannot be one of the issues we are dealing with: it is the heart of the political question, it is the centre of the problem. Then, even if, as in this case, it happens to express an element of agreement respect, for example, to the need to overcome the bicameralism paritarian in our country, we pose a very serious problem with regard to an aspect that is greatly underestimated: the arranged combination of a Senate elected with a second-level mechanism, moreover by regional councils that will probably proceed to the election, even in that case, with the mechanism of nominations, and Italicum (i.e. an electoral law that provides blocked lists, abnormal barriers, majority prizes, in short, a mechanism unpublished in the history of the country) risks to determine a huge political problem for Italy, which is experiencing a deep crisis of democratic representation and, never as before, a separation and detachment between rulers and governed, and which, instead, should have started a process inverse to what is being undertaken. Today it is a question of recovering that vulnus, so that politics and institutions appear far from the interests of the citizens; it is a question of intervening precisely on those aspects, working to widen the spaces of democracy. This is the great topic of discussion and not from today, but from twenty years to this side; this is the point on which even part of Italian constitutionalism over these years has been confronted and which has made us say for a long time that the reforms made in recent years, which have always looked at the theme of governability rather than the theme of representation, have left a nerve uncovered compared to a complex and delicate terrain such as this. This is the time of the crisis of the forms of politics as we have known them; it is the time of the crisis of the mass parties, it is the time of the crisis of representation, it is the time when the elements of public distrust towards the political class have increased enormously. How, then, does one react to this crisis? How do you get out of this mechanism? I think that the only answer to getting out of this crisis is to work to make spaces of democracy cross-border and participatory, much more than before. That is why, even if I understand the ratio, I do not in any way share the fact, for example, that the number of signatures needed to submit a repealing referendum or a legislative proposal is rising, even if I understand that there will be an innovation in the parliamentary regulations; but until we see that innovation, we think that the political problem remains very serious. Today, the weights and counterweights that the constituent Fathers had imagined as a limit to the power of the Executive are also called into question. And as for this reason we believe it is right to vote on the preliminary questions, we think it is very wrong to imagine that in the new order that will rule our country the decrees indicated by the government must have a priority preferential lane. This arrangement is the product of a misconception. But is it possible that the story of all these years has taught nothing? Is it possible that he did not teach that all too often the elements of representation have been limited and all too often an idea, that of governability, has been brought forward, but it has ultimately proved to be a slogan and propaganda? Well, for all these reasons, we think that this reform does not help to develop what our country would need. I shall have the opportunity to say so even in the general debate later when I try to ask this House to discuss this fundamental issue: we need today to change our democracy in the foundations; we need to overcome, to break that link which over all these years has seen representative democracy consume more and more, until the paradoxical result that Italy sees today and, of course, the other European countries mark an element of dissent so strong with regard to politics and its traditional forms. That is why it would have served nothing else: it would have served to insist on the mechanisms of participatory democracy, which present the idea that democracy can be crossed by social actors. Personally an Italian democracy as we are building it today, which makes it more difficult for social actors to cross it and be agents of change frankly scares me and worries me. For these reasons we shall vote in favour of the questions referred for a preliminary ruling. [[Applause from the Misso-SEL Group.]]

I cannot even dwell on what it would take to amend Article 70 of the Constitution, which we could also call ridiculous if it were not unfortunately very dangerous. Today Article 70 of the Constitution defines the procedure for the formation of laws with a single line which states with great clarity: The legislative function is carried out collectively by the two Chambers. In the new text, which this Senate will soon be called to vote, Article 70 needed as many as 56 lines, necessarily confused, weak, destined to produce many interpretative contrasts. Not bad for a government that at its beginning promised (...) a drastic simplification of the legislation and that instead manages only to complicate simple rules that have worked well for decades. Perhaps in front of this mess every comment is really superfluous. With the new Article 70 we have reached the maximum possible of institutional confusion, of approximate and out-of-place transposition of mechanisms present in other foreign constitutions, of introduction of devices that make any decision of serious technical deficit in the drafting of the text of the norm. If the current text were to become the new Article 70 of our Constitution, we would not only have a gravely wrong Constitution, but also illegible and, in the specific case, in the process of forming laws the practical impossibility of bringing the laws to their definitive approval. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u26	Mr President, in Italy, the debate on constitutional reforms and the establishment of the Federal Senate is now a constant of every legislature, which has been going on for thirty years. I am therefore surprised that someone is still talking too much of a rush in launching this reform. In 2005, however, thanks to the strong reformist will of the center-right government, the long parliamentary path of the constitutional reform law was closed and a text that already provided for the establishment of the Federal Senate, as a representative chamber of the interests of the territory and local communities was approved. The text, which is absolutely consistent in its reformer system, if it had passed positively to the scrutiny of the confirmation referendum, would have avoided other years of perfect bicameralism and therefore of legislative impasse. Too bad that those same left-wing forces that today loudly proclaim constitutional reform were the first to boycott the 2005 reform, making themselves the protagonists of a rearguard battle that led to the rejection of the text by means of the June 2006 referendum. (At 13.28 a.m.) (Following Mazzoni). That reform reduced the number of parliamentarians and streamlined the process of passing the laws: the model was that of single-camera proceedings, respectively, within the competence of the Chamber and the Federal Senate on the basis of the matters dealt with. It was a courageous reform, which created a balanced system of weights and counterweights, which had the objective of offering the Commission the ability to govern and citizens the sacrosanct opportunity not to see its vote frustrated by post-election agreements. In the reform there was also devolution, with the clause of supremacy in defense of the national interest, which surpassed the legislative tragedy caused by the concurrent legislation provided for by the reform of Title V of Part II of the Constitution wanted by the center-left. That said, there is nowhere to go with the recriminations: the abolition of the perfect bicameralism is an unavoidable turning point to begin a real reform process and the Nazarene agreement represents the basis and the framework of it. It is not the best possible, but the best, as you know, is often worse than the good. What, ultimately, does the Berlusconi-Renzi agreement provide? That the Senate is no longer elective, that it has no cost to taxpayers and that it no longer trusts the government. I immediately say that the original reform project presented by the Government was an indigestible mess, starting from the same regional representation for Val d'Aosta and Lombardy, from the prevailing presence of the mayors, from the twenty-one senators appointed by the Head of State and above all from the non proportionality of the senators expressed by the Regional Councils. Now, thanks to Forza Italia, the text has been improved: the criterion of proportionality has been introduced in the allocation of senatorial seats as we strongly wanted to prevent the Senate from becoming an anti-democratic monopoly of the majority. But it's not enough for someone. You are hanging yourself from the principle of the eligibility of senators, considered as the mother of all battles. I urge you to reflect on this point, because I do not believe that the elected Senate is at the top of the priorities of the Italian people and especially of the voters of the centre-right. The second-level election is not only the cornerstone of the agreement signed by our President, but it is also a balanced proposal, in line with the institutions of the other major European democracies. Just think that of the fifteen countries of Western Europe, seven do not have the Senate and in the other eight it is elective only in Spain. The two main issues surrounding the reform of the Senate, in short, are its composition and the functions assigned to it. Some say that these are two closely interwoven issues. In part it is true, but often those who support this thesis do so to say that if the Senate has significant powers it must also be elected directly and that is not true. A Senate may not be directly elected and have important powers: this is the case in Germany. That is, he can be elected directly and not have real powers: this is the case in Spain. The new Senate will not, however, be a useless clasp of the Chamber, since it will have competence over regional and European legislation, will elect the President of the Republic together with the Chamber, will elect the Supreme Council of the magistrates and the constitutional judges and will have a voice above all on electoral and constitutional laws. A body dealing with regional, supranational and institutional issues, leaving only the Chamber of Deputies the task of carrying out ordinary legislative work. So, no more perfect bicameralism, no compensation, no vote of confidence. What interests Forza Italia are essentially three things: that this reform does not adversely affect public finance; a clearer division between what is the responsibility of the State and what is the responsibility of the Regions, recovering some subjects from the State; that the new balance of powers that is determined through the new Senate does not lead to cases where the majority becomes overly predominant. These are the three points that we have put forward and that the text that came out of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs fully respects. Overcoming equalism is an important objective but the strengthening of the government's role in the legislative process is also important. One of the most serious problems of our model of government has always been the weakness of the Commission in Parliament. It is from this weakness that the abuse of decree-laws was born. Once this constitutional change has been approved, along with the Senate reform, the government will no longer have any alibi, because one of the rules approved by the Commission provides for a preferential path for the draft laws presented by the Commission. Finally, there is the question of immunity, a very delicate issue in a country where part of the judiciary is used to trespass in a political role that is not constitutionally its own and that has caused incalculable damage to the institutional balance, ending up trampling the popular will. Well, despite this, in my opinion it is not justifiable to extend tout court immunity to all those who will sit in the new Senate. It's my personal position. I believe that a different speech is to give them a regime to protect the indisputability of the opinions they express as senators - and therefore strictly confined to the exercise of their parliamentary function - which, however, cannot be extended and also applied to their work as regional advisers or mayors. One last thought, Madam President, is addressed to the dissidents of my party: we have to ask ourselves whether it is the agreement on reforms with Renzi that is bad for us or the image that we have given in recent months, of a party torn apart by internal disputes and the obnoxious attitude that we have had on reforms. We in this Parliament are essential to achieving change, and that certainly does not mean accepting everything that is proposed to us. We have not done so and we have achieved many important changes in relation to the draft reform presented by the Government. But, ladies and gentlemen, having a thousand strategists means having no strategy. The last thing we can afford right now is to appear as conservatives, caste defenders, those who actually want to keep the Senate unchanged. This really would not be forgiven by our constituents. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII. Congratulations.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u29	Senator Lo Moro is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u30	Madam President, representatives of the Government, I begin by expressing a deep feeling, not of inadequacy, but of emotion for the role we are playing. To participate in structural reforms, to have an important impact on a Constitution that for many years we have considered the only reference not only political and ideal but also moral is an act that must be done with due seriousness and knowing the responsibilities that are assumed. This I want to say from the reform of the Senate, because a further element of emotion is the feeling, that everyone must feel, of the possibility of incurring errors, that it is always right to keep under control. For this discussion we start right from the Senate, but the discussion, which on the outside has been mediatically imagined and communicated as concerning the reform of the Senate, is not only - I insist much on this element that I consider important - trivially a modification of the role of the Senate, but it is a more structural and overall reform, which affects the bicameralism paritarian, making it no longer so, and therefore requiring a modification of the functions and roles of Chamber and Senate, and profoundly affects the role of the Chamber itself. I want to start from the role of the House, and then focus on that of the Senate, to emphasize, at this stage of my reflection, the fact that, for years, since I have been engaged in parliamentary roles (first in the Chamber of Deputies and, in this legislature, in the Senate), I hear debate and passionately participate in discussions on the need to put an end to equal bicameralism. It seemed obvious, and for us in the PD Group, for us in the PD Group, more generally, two things were taken for granted: that one could not go beyond with this system and that the diversification of functions would necessarily have to pass for a completely different electoral system, because it was not conceivable, we said for years, that with different functions Chamber and Senate had the same type of election and legitimacy. So there was a direct election for the House of Deputies and a second-level election for the Senate of the Republic. All this can be called into question, but it is part of the political heritage that we have cultivated for years. That is why on this point in the electoral system I would be more cautious in taking sides in political controversy, because we are discussing things that we have grown up on and which we have been discussing for years and which seemed to have reached a high definition point. I want to start with the House to say that the House of the future is profoundly different, also as a system, not only and not so much because of the electoral law, which is outside today's debate and on which we will be very careful to maintain the majority approach (which, moreover, with a popular referendum, also the population, and not only the politics, has confirmed) and to make those necessary changes so that we can no longer talk about the House of Representatives, about the House of Deputies. I believe, however, that at this moment we must discuss the reform that we are considering, knowing that the element of the majority system and of Italicum (which is looming and which, I repeat, needs to be changed deeply) has certainly not helped the debate, because it has dragged it onto a terrain that is not its own. The House of the future, in fact, is quite different because it responds to the principles of governability not only by virtue of the electoral system, which we have not yet voted on, but also by structural elements that have passed away from this reform. Colleagues, for years myself, in the roles I have played above all in the Chamber of Deputies, I denounced the phenomenon of excessive recourse to decree-laws (Mr Volpi well knows the discussions in this regard), the fact that it was not permissible for omnibus decrees to arrive and, all the worse, that it was not admissible that the decree-laws, when they were not omnibus, became laws that contained everything and more. This discussion lasted for years in our country, in the institutions and outside of them, up to the request of the Constitutional Court, messages even heavy of the Head of State. Well, let's talk about the positives of this reform: this reform gives an answer and also courageous. In fact, a government such as the current one, which accepted and provided for in its draft law (so it is not only the favourable opinion given to the whole article and every amendment approved in the Commission) to bind itself, in the sense that from now on it will not be a declaration of principle, but it will be such an important and imposing necessity that it affects the constitutionality of the laws, well it is a government that says a clear word. He says that innovations bind him, that he wants to win with innovations and that therefore he tends to reform also the behavior of politics, which is always done on the basis of himself. I would like to say with the same loyalty that I absolutely agree with another element introduced in the measure we are debating today, namely that a government can ask for some draft laws to have a priority: not all, but those on which it pays particular attention for reasons relating to the political project that it carries out. Why has there been excessive recourse to decree-laws in all these years? Perhaps only because there was an inability of the House and the Senate to work quickly? No, also for another reason: because a government may have needs that do not necessarily have the characteristics that are needed to issue a decree-law, but that must have a form of feedback. Well, in our reform, in what we still have to discuss, amend and approve in this House in the Senate and then in the House, there is a possible answer. I would like to remind everyone that with regard to the concept of governability, which today seems to be the opposite of representativeness, we must also note the fact that, after the decision of the Constitutional Court on Porcellum, it is useless to discuss it further, because it is an issue that has to do with the Constitution of our country. The principle of governability is not only a political objective: it is an objective that the institutions give themselves to an existing Constitution, according to our Constitution. So I started from these examples to say that the path we are also supporting as a Group of the Democratic Party finds within it elements of satisfaction that prescind from the diversification of functions between the House and the Senate, because it gives answers to age-old problems, which are not only these, but that they needed an answer. We cannot continue to do questions of principle and to make informal commitments on the behaviour to be carried out. When we move on to another phase in which commitments are made by the Constitution but it is said that, since it is necessary to govern, we also want a priority road, I think we must have courage and be loyal and transparent, because these reforms do not concern the Renzi Government, but the Government of the Italian Republic and the future. I was saying this to shift the attention of all of us to the fact that we are not just discussing the Senate of the Republic. Turning to the Senate, I understand that the greatest effort on this subject will be made here in the Senate of the Republic. I am sorry to have to say this, but I see a lot of inattention to the outside, even among my colleagues in the House, who will certainly be very attentive to the elements that will concern them, but they risk instead being not attentive to the change of the Senate of the Republic. The same debate on the number of Members and the fact that some of us have put forward proposals in this regard (I first tabled an amendment to reduce them to 500, which I withdrew because the Government had given a negative opinion on it), shows that many of us had different ideas, more than those that went through the Commission. There may be inattention, but there must not be, because the future Senate of the Republic is not about the senators who are in office, but the senators of the future is about the future of the Republic. It was then thought to try to build a Senate that had a valid, objective and measurable, because to say that in Italy it was necessary to maintain bicameralism, as present historically, would be too reductive and inconvenient. Some colleagues also proposed the suppression of the Senate; it could be a possible option, but we did not opt for this solution. We felt that doing what seemed to be a possible option in the Constituent's discussion, that is, giving value and representation to territorial authorities, made sense. This above all in a logic in which the Senate of the future, of which it will be positive to be part, will have functions of connection and not only between the Regions and the State, because in this connection will have to be included another element pertaining to the current Constitution. Things change, because we are in a Europe that tends to be a political body and that, even before it becomes a political body, issues measures that need to be implemented and, before that, they need to be codified. What contribution has Italy made to the codification and formulation of European acts? Until now, according to my own direct experience, which I have gathered and lived on my way to Brussels as a representative of the House and then of the Senate, the failing element, which was also pointed out by our Offices in the European Parliament, is the poor ability to engrave European measures. To affect European measures means to have prior knowledge of them, to be able to amend, reform and even propose them. Today's Italy is able to propose them. This linking function is then very ambitious and fills the gaps that we register today when we complain ex post of measures that seem inadequate to us and do not take into account our territorial reality and the characteristic of a country system that is evolving. I believe, therefore, that the challenge we are taking up, aimed at making sense of this Senate, maintaining a bicameralism that is not equal, even by increasing the possibility of our bicameralism to give answers that it has not so far given, is a positive challenge. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. You have to finish, Senator Lo Moro. LO MORO (PD). Yes, ma'am, you understand that we've been arguing for months. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. I didn't set the time allocated to her. LO MORO (PD). In conclusion, to say that the Senate represents the territorial institutions bears consequences. The hypothesis of the electoral system - which is the responsibility of the ordinary, bicameral law, and we are not discussing it - that the Senate is part of regional councillors and mayors means that the most important territorial institutions of our country (the Regions and the Municipalities) will send their representatives to the Senate to represent the territorial authorities. There is consistency in the system, we will have plenty of other opportunities to discuss it, but above all, by addressing my so-called dissidents, who are my colleagues with whom we must discuss, not dissidents, I say that we have done a great job, even hard work, and it is right that it is recognised. The Government has worked with us and I want to acknowledge the Sub-secretaries present, but also Minister Boschi, who has worked with us effectively, giving a favourable opinion to amendments that have also radically modified the text of the Government. This is the House. The real challenge is that the House will also find the strength not to stand up for or against something, but to change what needs to be changed to make the reform more acceptable and voted for all. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u31	He is enlisted to speak Senator De Cristofaro. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u32	Madam President, I think that no reasoning on the reforms we are debating in this House can ignore the analysis, albeit short, of the time we live and put our hands on them. It is a historic era marked by a deep distrust of the institutions and in which the wind of the crisis of the mass parties, of the disaffection towards politics, of the old and the new populisms that have manifested themselves with great force even in the recent electoral round is now blowing on the whole of our continent. In particular, in our country the combined arrangement of the economic crisis, on the one hand, and the crisis of the representativeness of politics, on the other, risks constituting a real explosive mixture, dangerous for the same consolidated forms of representative democracy. In my opinion, this situation presents unprecedented aspects, which have never, if I may say so, seen the Republic in this way. In fact, there have been moments, in the recent and less recent history of our country, marked by a profound economic and social crisis, but at least the representativeness of politics was not so strongly questioned, indeed, it itself could represent even a sort of reference point from which to start, and other moments, instead, in which the crisis of politics did not correspond a so profound social crisis. A crisis - I would like to remind you always and also on this occasion - so lasting that has changed our country in the last ten years perhaps much more and much more deeply than we ourselves have been able to perceive. Well, I believe that this arranged combination, the social crisis on the one hand and the moral crisis on the other, perhaps our country had never known it in such striking dimensions. I make these considerations, because without this premise we do not fully grasp today's debate, and we do not even fully grasp the general risk that we run in our country: the risk that democracy runs. A democracy that, moreover, and I would like never to forget, is a young democracy, which has not yet been consolidated in its basic elements, starting with the failure to implement our Constitution, which remains the undiscovered nerve with which we have been confronting ourselves for many years. Ours is a democracy that is still measured by an element of incompleteness of popular participation in decision-making processes and with a structural deficit of interposition of the intermediate bodies (first of all the political parties in crisis, but also for example the trade union world), as a historical link between citizens and institutions, until the profound questioning of what should instead be the positive tension between representatives and representatives. So we have to ask ourselves this in our debate: why has this happened over the years? Why did the link between citizens and institutions go so far? I believe that it has not benefited even a little 'the trend of reforms that have been there over the past few years (those that have been made and those that have just been announced), because they all generally concern the effectiveness and speed of government action much more than they did in terms of representation. Certainly it has affected, within this difficulty of making democracy fully live in our country, the repeated attempt of formal and substantial reform of the Constitution, and also the real democratic involution that has generated some symptomatic phenomena, with which we also confront ourselves daily in this Parliament: the abuse of the question of trust, the electoral laws for a long time now focused on governance rather than on representation, the excessive use of decrees and maxiemaments. The absolute bond of fidelity, if I may say so, introduced by the last electoral law has really done the rest, putting Parliament, in fact and substantially, in a material impossibility to offset the strength and power of the leaders. In this context, which should see as an antibody to the crisis the use of increasingly marked and profound forms of popular participation, obviously compatible, from my point of view, and certainly not a substitute for the model of constitutional democracy, is the reflection that we are doing in these days. Madam President, I do not like to say that we are faced with neo-authoritarianism, because I think it is wrong to bend words to propaganda. But I think - and this I would like to say quite clearly - that, compared to what our country needs, this reform is inadequate, as well as messy in some fundamental elements, as has also been recognized by many external observers. Therefore, especially for this reason, it is wrong, not only because it is not necessary to widen the spaces of democracy, in order to promote those processes capable of integrating political representation in a democratic sense (e.g. by intervening on a key concept such as citizenship, which is less and less identifiable only with its legal configuration), but also because it makes the spaces of democracy less permeable, less passable, less participatory, in a historical era - as I have tried to say - in which the effort should have been another one in my opinion. And it should have been conducted not simply as a purely institutional initiative, but with a discussion support able to cross the entire country, the living country, not simply the parliamentary classrooms. Madam President, with the draft law we are discussing here, we are faced with the broadest possible reform of the Republican Constitution from the moment it came into force until today (it was also mentioned by those who spoke before me). Nothing comparable to other changes made in recent years to the constitutional text, some for the truth - let me say - really not too happy in the effects, like that of Title V of Part II, which was marked by the same haste and anxiety, even at the time too much propaganda, which we still see today, as if history had not taught anything. And also other recent changes, such as the rewriting of Article 81 on the obligation to balance the budget, result in a fallback on the functioning of the country not irrelevant and, above all, not positive. This is the largest draft constitutional revision - as I said - which has as its instrument a draft law that proceeds for the decisive impulse of the Government, also here with a forcing in relation to normal procedural rules. It is a reform - I come now to the merit - which introduces substantial changes in the relations between the Chambers, not only because it overcomes the perfect bicameralism (on this, however, we are not opposed), but because it profoundly changes the nature of the Senate, predicting - as we know well now - a second-level elective system, on the other hand, which we clearly mark a profound dissent on. To get to the point immediately, I believe that here we nest the aspect of greater criticality, on which it would have been more opportune to spend more attention and much less haste and engage a more careful reflection than we have been allowed to. To deny this possibility means anything but to avoid a waste of time, a defiant negotiation put in place by presumed brakes or supposed saboteurs. Of course, even in this case, propaganda can always be used, and it has often been done during these days, as if those who advance doubts, perplexities and dissensions were an enemy of progress or even an agent of the caste engaged in defending who knows what privilege. Of course, this can be done; it is a very convenient shortcut, if I may say so. But it is of no use to the country, it is of no use: it is simply a propaganda operation. And in any case, Madam President, I can assure you, it certainly does not help to frighten those who for a lifetime have been accustomed to hearing from the unthinking choir of the unique thought of being an impediment or an obstacle to who knows which trials, and then to discover instead that things were very different from how they were often unanimously represented. We have deep reservations about the substance and also about the way in which this reform is being carried out. We believe that the rewriting of a fundamental part of the Constitution, which concerns a representative body, is a huge change, which would have required greater weighting, greater possibility for Parliament to assess the options that can be implemented, because it is a project that profoundly changes the institutional structure and will affect the functioning of representation probably even for the next decades. I say this because I do not think at all that the best way to face the need, which we too feel no longer procrastinable, to renew the institutions is to hastily draw a line on what is there. We too think that the times are ripe for overcoming the perfect bicameralism. But are we really sure that by suppressing direct election, you choose the best system? We don't believe that at all. And we do not believe that especially if one of the reasons is that this reduces the cost of this constitutional body. We know that: it is not the senators' allowances, and with them the Members' allowances - on which, moreover, we are ready to intervene in a very drastic way, and even to a greater extent than the Government asks us - the greater weight for the state's coffers. As the state budget clearly shows, the prevailing cost is property, personnel, services and everything else. However, as a result of the changes that we are discussing these days, we will perhaps reduce the costs to a very small extent, but we will certainly reduce the representativeness and political weight of the Senate by a great deal. This is the opposite of a corporate claim. We would like to see a more serious reflection on the actual role of counterweight and rebalancing of the new Senate than the House of Deputies itself. In this regard we really feel that we share the opinion expressed by many authoritative constitutionalists, according to which a Senate such as the one envisaged will in no way be able to balance the powers of the Chamber, especially if it will be the so-called Italicum, with its anonymous barriers and its blocked lists, to determine the formation of the latter, i.e. if the absolute majority of the Members will be enough to cancel the deliberations taken in the few legislative powers remaining to the new Senate. It seems clear to us that in this way there is a risk that any idea of balancing with the full legislative power of the House of Deputies will be thwarted. It is for this reason that we believe that this reform is not an extension, but a narrowing of the spaces of democracy: on this we want to see a confrontation in this House and, above all, a confrontation between the country. The residual role of the new Senate appears here in full evidence and drives us to say not by paradox that perhaps the idea of providing for the total repeal of this branch of Parliament would have been wiser. Again, as I said earlier, we have great doubts that indirect election can make the new organ more functional, but with a blocked system that greatly increases the discretion of the great voters. I really don't understand how you don't see it. This creates - in our opinion - a twist that restricts the spaces of democracy and gives the composition of a Chamber to a self-referential circuit that, in conjunction with an electoral system such as Italicum, blocks the representation circuit and creates precisely the famous and so vicious Chamber of Appointments. I am not going to say any more about the effects of the quorum formation on the election of the Head of State, because the distortions that are determined there are frankly so obvious that we can avoid even describing them, and from this point of view I do not think it is enough to mitigate the positive sub-amendment adopted over the past few days. Here is the blow that is being given to democratic representation. If this system does not even pose the problem of tempering the most questionable aspects, with the introduction and strengthening of forms of direct democracy and democratic participation, it will become more difficult for citizens to make their voices heard. We have lowered the quorum threshold for the referendum, but the number of signatures to be reached is rising to be able to present it. And the same is true of popular initiative laws: from 50,000 to 250,000. In essence, it seems to us that the whole of institutional reforms constitute a reduction and not a widening of the spaces of agility, and we say so - and I insist on this ground - without the fear of incurring the all too often abhorrent replies that regularly reach those who allow themselves to advance doubts. We say this because we care about the issue of reforms and because we are light years away from any element of self-preservation, more: as I said at the outset, we are fully aware of the crisis that goes through representative democracy. If you will allow me, Madam President, we have already understood this for a few years. Already in 2000, when the first world forum on democracy and participation was organized in Porto Alegre, we were present, but at the time the unique thought probably didn't even know what we were talking about. Let me say this, Madam President, with a certain dismay at the unceasing demand for simplification, speed, procedures treated as annoyance and as a quirk, the objections reduced to annoying background noise. All this is not good, but not because it is not good for us, but because it cannot work so a democracy that can and must be self-formed. We believe that the self-reform of politics and institutions is necessary, very necessary, but another thing is when the elected bodies are asked for the pure and simple ratification of what the government has decided, when the speed, that is, presupposes the reduction of the counterweights in order to make room for the myth of a decision-making democracy, when it wants the decisions to travel on the direct line that connects the leader to the citizens. This approach is dangerous and brings us rights to that particular evolution of Western democracies that many scholars have called with a name that says it a lot: democracy of the public. I think we must be radically opposed to this torsion that can also go through the democracy of our country. Of course, the crisis of representative democracy is the great political problem of our time, on this we must reflect and build appropriate counter-measures. The unpopular measures required by international bodies to deal with the effects of the economic crisis seem to have accelerated the crisis itself, calling into question, rather than the effectiveness of the decisions of the Executives, once again the speed with which they are taken and the ability to bypass the intermediate bodies of society. This too seems to us to be very dangerous, not really a contribution to the quality of democracy. It seems to us that this crisis highlighted by the success of the populist movements that propose the emptying, the overcoming, even the destruction of all forms of organized politics, has, however, become a sort of permanent exception, an opportunity not to widen the participation and the instruments of control of the governed over the rulers, but to dismantle the balances and counterweights necessary for a pluralist democracy. It is thought that the cure is to increase the power conferred on a single legislative body and, from here, on its winning side or even on its leader, to perhaps assure that leader that, once he has won the place of power, the place of command, then he will be the only one able to exercise it. We do not think that this is the way to remedy the crisis of representation: instead we think that in this way we take a shortcut that leads not far away. It may be true that this may not be the case, but it happens, Madam President, that sometimes when we take a road it is the road itself that leads us and not we decide where to go. We do not want this reform to lead our democracy to a path that is not only dangerous, but also without an exit. [[Applause from the Mixed-SEL Group and the Senators Bulgarelli and Bencini.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u33	He's enlisted to speak Senator Airola. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u34	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to make a brief introduction, as I am the first in my group to speak. It seems to me correct to clarify initially a very important point, a point that is constantly mystified by the Government and the press. Today we are starting a debate between parliamentarians elected by the sovereign people who will discuss a reform that reaches the heart of our democratic system and which has a profound impact on its institutional architecture. Minister Boschi has not missed the opportunity to point out that the 5 Star Movement in these hours will obstruct to block the country. It's not like that. We are here to discuss, confront and try to persuade this House, discussing with other Members, not to vote for the reform of this Senate as the Minister and the Government have thought and imposed it, first in the Commission and then in the House. That this handful of a few hours missing from the vote on the choice between canceling an elective Senate, a direct expression of the people, in a part-time job percent nominated, that this short time is called "obstructionism" is the sign of the little respect that this Government has for democratic practice and of how Parliament no longer has any role, has no weight anymore, in this "Italy" that changes towards you imagined. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] As my colleagues go into examining the rules and revealing the loss of power of the citizen who hides this reform, I will concentrate on the general plan and begin, precisely so as not to waste this precious time available, from indicating immediately the point of arrival to which this reform tends and above all the inappropriate, anti-democratic and authoritarian methods by which the objective of overcoming the perfect bicameralism is being pursued. The aim is to concentrate power in the hands of the Government and, in particular, in the hands of one man. Our action is therefore not - as we hear obsessively repeating - aimed at stopping the country, but at ensuring that any reform of the State, any profound change in our Constitutional Charter is the result of a reflection shared by all, majority and opposition. Our aim is to prevent the people from being deprived of sovereignty, guaranteeing the eligibility of parliamentarians, their direct representation and, above all, guaranteeing a system in which the powers (and this is very important) are in balance. The balance of power is the founding principle of any democratic and parliamentary system and it is that which will fail with this reform, also thanks to the coupled with the electoral law, the Italicum (if it is voted, also that). Renzi aims to have everything and in an authoritarian way acts to pursue an authoritarian objective. In this it is consistent, for charity, organic in methods and objectives. With this reform you will have the opportunity to decide all the President of the Council: your Government and its majority will decide the laws, the President of the Republic, the CSM. We will be very, very far from the distinction between the three autonomous and independent legislative, executive and judicial powers, as our Constitutional Charter would like. We are talking here about vivisecting the Constitution and replacing its organs with an open-heart operation. The result will be a Frankestein Constitution, or better still a Constitution from Dr Moreau Island, without any respect for the legal harmony and the organicity of the principles contained in our Charter. There are no perfect systems, but everyone, I say everyone, has systems for balancing powers. Imagine then how efficient and productive the work of a regional councillor or a mayor can be, who for two or three days stays on the board and for two or three days comes to Rome. It's unbelievable to think that he can work profitably. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u36	Madam President, in the examination of this draft constitutional law, I would like to start with a few comments that have been made this morning by the two rapporteurs who have spoken and, in particular, I would like to acknowledge the start of an excellent work which has been done within this Senate - especially in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs - in order to remedy an approach to the draft law which created several difficulties and major problems, especially from a legal and constitutional point of view. As I have also said in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, the draft constitutional law we are debating, in my view, is in a way that is even de-grammaticated from a constitutional point of view. The text, fortunately, has also been corrected - and in part this correction has been started within the Commissions - so much so that, when the two rapporteurs tabled their amendments, so to speak, 'unified', we already saw that the original text had been radically transformed. I would like to remind you quickly of a couple of issues: basically, out of 31 articles of which the text of the constitutional bill was formed - except for the last ones dealing with transitional and final rules and provisions - eight were replaced in full and one was asked for the deletion, while then there were substantial deletions of several paragraphs of other articles. This, therefore, has already given rise to a lack of acceptance by the two rapporteurs and the Commission of a text which, I repeat, was constitutionally grammatical. Following further work carried out by the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, therefore, we are faced with a different text than the one initially presented by the Government, which, as we have seen, had already changed it at least a couple of times in some of its initial approaches. Last week, in a room in this Senate, a conference-debate was organized with high-level constitutionalists on constitutional reform, which was titled "Constitutional democracy and balance of powers." The Apories of Constitutional Reform. Now, rather than the original meaning of the concept of aporia, I would like to refer to the concept of aporia in the socratic sense, adapting it to the case: rather than talking about an unworkable passage or a road now without way out, I prefer to stick to the socratic meaning of aporia and then talk about a phase of the socratic maieutics aimed at liberation from false knowledge, liberation from the conviction of having certain truths. Here, in my view, the mistake of origin from which this constitutional bill was based consisted in the claim to possess the absolute and indisputable truth, which could not be touched, and criminalized anyone who allowed himself - especially within the government majority - to express legal, political and constitutional assessments and observations in dissent. That is precisely why, not even sharing this form of aggression, we have tabled as a group of Senators of the Democratic Party, in the Commission (and we will do so in this House by the deadline set for tomorrow) a series of amendments which we consider to be qualifying precisely to overcome these aporias. Having said that, I would immediately like to make it clear, and to avoid any controversy that has already been raised and formulated, that we are absolutely not opposed to this constitutional reform as well as we are not opposed to constitutional reforms, because there are some points - which I would like to remind you - on which we agree. For example, the fundamental issue of overcoming the prefect, or equal, bicameralism that we want, sees us absolutely consenting: we have been talking about it for decades; it is a subject that must certainly be addressed and overcome also from the legal-constitutional perspective that is beginning to come to light in this House. In the same way, we fully agree on another fundamental point, which is that of cutting, as it were, the number of Members and consequently the expenditure. Indeed, I would add that on this point we have also been much more radical, in the sense that, for example, we have advocated from the outset a reduction to 100 in the number of senators - so this is also well indicated in the text of the Government and the Commission - but at the same time the halving of the number of Members, who in our view should go from 630 to 315. On the other hand, there are other interim amendments that propose a reduction in the number of Members (some speak of 500, others 470): we will see, during the debate in this House, what number we are attested to and then how to vote. Another important point that we absolutely share is the fact that the relationship of trust with the Government must be with only one House, in this case with the Chamber of Deputies. So, these certainly important points are shared. I would like to add immediately that there are equally important points which in our view are not acceptable and we propose amendments to them. One of the fundamental points that we do not agree with is the well-known election of the Senate. We are also convinced of this because we, but I believe in virtually everyone in this House, including Senator Calderoli, as he said this morning, have fought that notorious law called Porcellum for years; Well, after so many years of saying that it is no longer acceptable for citizens to be unable to vote for their representatives, now the government is proposing a text for which the Senate would continue not to be voted by the citizens. They would be second-level, even third-level votes, and the senators - as we know very well in political practice would happen - would be appointed by the parties and not by the citizens, which we absolutely do not want. We therefore want an elective Senate according to the instructions we make. Another fundamental point that we believe needs to be reviewed in depth is that of the functions to be given to this Senate. Look, ladies and gentlemen, it is true that on this front too the government has opened up the meshes, the grid it had formulated at the beginning, as the Commission has done, adding to the competences in the field of equal legislation other than the constitutional ones, but there are points that remain outside and are decisive for the evaluation of the state of a democracy. We believe, for example, that when we intervene on matters relating to personal or religious freedom, when we intervene on guarantee bodies, such as the Constitutional Court, the Council of State, the Court of Auditors, the Supreme Council of the judiciary, that is, everything that has to do with guarantees for citizens, we need to approve, evaluate, discuss and vote on by both Chambers. From two Chambers because, as we have seen in recent years, the presence of a Chamber with respect to the other has often determined the correction of laws that go out badly, sometimes, let's say so too, made with the feet, and in some cases laws that have also been considered provocations. Then, the question of so-called parliamentary immunity was included at some point in this approach. Our small group of PD senators believes that only the guarantee provided for in the first paragraph of Article 68 of the Constitution should be maintained, i.e. that the opinions and votes expressed during the parliamentary term of office should be unassailable. We believe that the parliamentary immunity referred to in the second and third paragraphs is now completely out of history, a medieval heritage that certainly made sense in 1200, when it was introduced given the presence of feudal regimes, emperors, popes who had temporal power and vassals of various kinds. At that time it was necessary for the representatives of the people to have a guarantee against the abuses and restrictions of freedom of the representatives of the people themselves. Now, after the repeal of the Albertine Statute and the fall of the fascist regime, it no longer makes sense. We will say it better when we present our amendments in detail. In 1948, when our Constitution came into force, this immunity still made sense because we came out of a particular historical era, because the judiciary in 1948 was still heavily and culturally fascist and because in 1948 we had a procedural system that gave no guarantee because any prosecutor could issue an order of capture, writing as motivation: there are sufficient clues as in acts, and capturing anyone. It doesn't work like that anymore. Now we have a different cultural and social formation of the judiciary, we have a completely different process and guarantee systems (the gip, the review court, the Court of Cassation), which are certainly end-of-process guarantee systems that work.

This is the perspective in which we must move and it is from this perspective of institutional awareness, which is also social, political and ethical, that we will move, because we are convinced that we need to find an institutional balance in order to safeguard and guarantee the pillars of our democracy, not in the immediate and for the next three or four years, but in the decades to come.
