ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u41	Madam President, last week Prime Minister Renzi, at the end of the Council of Ministers, said that he would like to discuss the substance of the reforms, because when he hears about authoritarian management, a smile is printed on his face, so much is the absurdity of this accusation. Now, considering that he always smiles because of the natural inclination of his face, if not for maxillary conformation, I would really invite President Renzi to come to the Senate to discuss reforms, rather than in via del Nazareno, because, frankly, I do not think he understood the gravity of his evil project. The accusation of authoritarian management that is addressed to him is not unfounded, indeed I would say it is very well founded, given the repercussions that the reform will have on the country system and its democratic system. When it comes to constitutional reforms, the impression is that the focus is on political alliances and not, as it should, on the content of the reforms themselves. Indeed, on the merits of reforms, for example on issues such as the elective Senate, parliamentary immunity, referendum, election of the Head of State, participatory democracy, many senators of the majority have expressed, who explicitly, who behind them, their disappointment. Many agree with the 5 Star Movement, which claims that these reforms are an attack on the Constitution and democracy. Others, who more or less, for some conjuncture - or perhaps it would be appropriate to say "postura" - are condescending to Prime Minister Renzi. As we read about the Daily Fact a few days ago, the package of constitutional reforms was born under a confused constellation. I dare say that package was thought of by twisted minds, and then translated by too unaware pens of the damage that would result. Of course it is that, without numbers in Parliament, no reform is possible, let alone constitutional reform. For weeks now we have seen that the majority are trying to establish contacts with the individual opposition forces and even with the individual Members in a scattered order, in order to verify the margins of negotiation with each one, in order to obtain the promise of a few more votes. Perhaps the majority have counted to see what will happen. Who knows? The truth is that this race to launch the reform is destined to conclude a goal that does not want to bring a real benefit to the citizens, but just wants to allow this government to put the flag on the abolition of the Senate, or rather on the transformation of the Senate into the Chamber of Autonomies, and on the electoral law, as if these two arguments were really the priority for the rebirth of our country. It seems to me that Renzi wants to have this reform approved at all costs, not because it is right and necessary, but only in order to be able to say: "You see, I am capable of reforming the country." The President-in-Office of the Council, however, had a bad fall, he did the mathematics without an innkeeper. He forgot that within the Senate, which wants to abolish, there are us, simple citizens who still believe in democracy and who will not allow this reform to pass. Of course, if this reform were to pass, we hope to go straight to the elections to be able to overthrow the table. Have you ever had the idea that one day in those government benches there might be those who are in opposition today? Have you wondered what the guillotine would mean to you? But perhaps not: there is so much consideration that you have of yourself that you have not even put this hypothesis for a moment. After all, you too have been infected by the disease that is born of arrogance and presumptuousness that is difficult to eradicate. On the other hand, we expected an extra ambition from those who have the responsibility of government today: to be able to reform well. We expected no one to put his hand on the perfect bicameralism, also because arriving at a Senate of 100 appointed as well as changing the electoral law is not the reform that the country needs, first of all because it will not allow the great savings that Renzi propaganda since he arrived to the government (I remember, without being elected by someone), but especially because it is not the reform that will solve the crisis of the state. I would like to remind you that the state crisis is a crisis of social growth, and not the opposite, but you may not have realized that you have aggravated it. In the institutional debate interwoven around these blessed reforms (which perhaps it would be better to define clashes between parties) the question that I think is missing is the following: not what Senate you want, but what Senate the country serves. In fact, the single reform must be thought not individually, but within an overall institutional project. It was enough to reason in mechanical terms, that is, you had to understand that, in order to choose a gear, you have to know first which engine you want to get and with what performance. And instead, you first thought about making a road stretch at 130 kilometers an hour, and then you realized that you had a bike. In short, the fear is that the game of bullets has prevailed over the project of innovation to be realized, forgetting that old methods hardly bring new results. To complicate the situation is not the summer heat, which certainly increases the mental confusion, but the fact that you take advantage of this heat so that citizens do not get a precise message, that the bill carried out by Matteo Renzi is too ugly to be true [[Applause from Senator Di Maggio]] This bill, focused on the existence of a single Chamber dominated by the government coalition, angers us of the opposition because, for example, we do not accept that the Senate of the Republic becomes a Senate no longer elected by the citizens, but composed of mayors and regional councillors who would work - pass me the term - part-time. What goes unruly is that Minister Boschi's bill, which came to the 1st Commission to say 'conform' or 'non-compliant', violates a basic principle of the rule of law, the one for which the laws make it the direct representatives of the people and not of the persons elected to other positions that the senators do when there are particular laws to be passed. I remember that Article 57 specifies what will be the characteristics of this Senate not directly elected: apart from the five presidential senators, the other ninety-five will be divided among the Regions, in proportion to the respective population, so that each one has at least two senators, of which one will have to be a mayor. But there is more. Perhaps the public is missing another affront from Renzi and his majority, namely the one consisting in raising signatures from 50,000 to 250,000 for proposals for popular initiative law and the increase to 800,000 signatures for the referendum. For the latter, the intervention of the Constitutional Court was envisaged in mid-collection, which should endorse the goodness of the referendum proposal in a short time. In any case, experts have forgotten the practical problems due to the timing that would arise, since by law the requests for referendums must be filed in each year only from 1 January to 30 September. Of course, we find it unfair, as well as constitutionally illegitimate, to propose to raise the minimum threshold of signatures necessary for the proposed law of popular initiative. In fact, limiting the popular legislative initiative means, again in this case, violating Article 1 of the Constitution, which proclaims that sovereignty belongs to the people, who exercise it in the forms and limits of the Constitution. And it is precisely this principle that directs the concept we have on referendums as well as on popular initiative laws, even on participatory democracy. These three are the concepts that my parliamentary group, since its inception, has defended, has tried to guarantee also within these institutions, as it will continue to do during the debate on reforms in this House. It must be clear to everyone that democracy, as it has been thought, cannot be dismantled or raped by anyone, not even by those who claim the right to face great institutional battles or by those who are offended when they are told that they are managed in an authoritarian way. If our constituent Fathers have indicated particular instruments of direct democracy, which allow voters to be direct protagonists of the law, there must have been a reason, right? Or should we assume that a referendum, by which the electoral body is required to consent or disagree with a particular decision, or a bill of popular initiative should become a dead letter within the normative document most envied by the civil societies of the world? Speaking of democracy, I would like to make a note of what we call participatory. Participatory democracy is emerging when there are particularly strong orientations and mechanisms for participation. It cannot be conceived as a substitute for representative democracy, nor must the opposite be the case, but above all it cannot compete with other aspects of constitutional democracy, understood as the most important protection of the fundamental rights of citizens, in the pluralism of powers and in the superiority of the Constitution. After all, the 5 Star Movement is the fruit of the idea we have of participatory democracy. Certainly we do not believe that you can accept the project of participatory democracy that the 5 Star Movement proposes, but at least let the Constitution still protect certain prerogatives to citizens. To those, at least, who decided not to leave the country. Let the Italians make decisions for the good of the cities in which they live, for the future of their children, for the community in its entirety. Participatory democracy is the only way to give concrete answers to the needs of the community. Democratic coexistence is structured in the close dialogue between the moment of participation and that of decision-making responsibility. Surely it is not structured in a soft dictatorship that puts the opposition at the corner. We cannot call democracy the one that gives power to only two people, least of all to the President of the Council and the President of the Republic. It is from the dialogue between majority and opposition that the life and survival of every democracy depends. Participation and responsibility are not on different sides of the same river: participation is the responsibility of those who have the mandate and the duty to make decisions; and responsibility is the responsibility of those who participate far beyond the individual electoral moment. In Western democracies, however, the scissors tend to spread and place on separate planes what makes sense only in constant exchange. And so, those who participate do not decide, and those who decide do not participate. Democracy in the absence of participation is that confined to a political technique, as a balance of powers, as an instrument of consensus, as a method among others to decide who must decide. These days I wondered: but the voters, the activists, the representatives of the PD, who were rightly coming to the streets against the constitutional changes of the Berlusconi government and who mobilized in 2006 for the referendum (later won) to counter them, didn't the doubt arise that there is something wrong, deeply wrong, very dangerous and very little democratic in Renzi's "meno-democracy"? Something was missing in democratic participation, but what? In order to participate with others we must remember two essential things: that the human person is all the more self the more able he is to collaborate, and therefore is not assimilable to an individual already accomplished in himself before his relationship with others; and, above all, that one cannot participate without granting credit, without believing, in the existence of others. Giving credit to the existence of others translates from all points of view - social, economic, political, intellectual - into the practices and logics of participation and cooperation. In 1958, the Spanish philosopher, Maria Zambrano, in her book "Person and Democracy" wrote that democracy is the society in which it is not only allowed, but it is even required to be a person; she wrote again: "We will reach the democratic order only with the participation of all as people, which corresponds to human reality." Until the existence of the others is taken into account, instead of a participatory democracy there will be only accounts and numbers, perpetual scales of individual units stacked together and moved from here to there, strategies and maneuvers, calculations and intrigues. So I ask myself: with this reform, which in fact would like to hinder democratic processes, which would complicate existing practices, where do you think you are going? Prime Minister Renzi said that the process of structural reforms that has been initiated is producing stages at a fair pace. He said he was not afraid of the vote in the Chamber. He even sent a message that tastes like a psychological threat rather than a moral one, which I bring back for better knowledge: If there is someone in the Senate who wants to brake (...) it's because he may not even remember the recent past. At this point, President Renzi, I wonder: wasn't the accusation of authoritarian management of constitutional reforms that someone dared to address to them so unfounded? Do you want us to remind you of that less recent past? Unfortunately, for reasons of time, I cannot recall all the names of our constituent Fathers, to whom all our gratitude goes as citizens of the Italian Republic and that we of the 5 Star Movement will always thank. However, I would invite all those who wish to vote on today's bill to go to a Senate building, Palazzo Giustiniani, where the Italian Constitution was signed, for which women, men and children have lost their lives, in front of which we all can only bow our heads. Constitutional reforms should start not from the electoral slogans, because with slogans institutional balances are drifting; rather, they must allow the democratic institutions to strengthen in order to face the difficult challenges of our times, driven by an economic, political, social and moral crisis of extraordinary dimensions. It is a question of grasping the present and future potential of a Constitution that is and remains a living and vital norm. And, precisely on the most beautiful Constitution in the world, someone wrote that "for the full development of the human person, to whom our Constitution was to tend, it was necessary not only to affirm individual rights, not only to affirm social rights, but also to affirm the existence of the rights of the intermediate communities that go from the family to the international community." To those social and individual rights this Government must not put its hands on; rather, take a step back and apologize for having hindered parliamentary work for months with a confused reform, but above all anti-democratic. If you don't want to do it for you, at least do it for the future of your grandchildren. [[Applause from the M5S Group and Senator Di Maggio.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u45	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to begin with two points. The first is that those who, like me, do not want the Senate to become a strange animal, neither elective nor non-elective, with contour functions that mask its substantial elimination, are not on this position to safeguard their own compensation, and I say this in particular to the President-in-Office of the Council. [[Applause from the Mixed-ILC and M5S Groups and the Senators Buemi, Di Maggio and Lo Giudice.]] That was frankly not worthy of his role or of our country. The second consideration is that those who do not want the reform done in this way do not mean that they do not want reforms at all: many of us have proposed correctives to the functioning of the Republic's machine and have done so with respect due to the establishment and well-being of those who will come after us. Therefore, if - as I hope - this reform project does not come to fruition, I will not feel it on me as a responsibility but as a merit, because we can want reforms and we want real reforms, not one like this one, which, in our opinion - but we are not the only ones, because there are many professors and citizens who think like us - upsets and disfigures the institutional structure of our country. Our Republic comes from many attempts at reform, one of which was mentioned a moment ago by a colleague of the League: everyone had the stated aim of making democratic institutions more efficient, but many had to meet lower political objectives, namely to provide reasons and elements for a dialogue in the light of the sun between distant political forces on paper, or to generate distraction from more pressing problems or, still, bland political forces to induce them to give support to a political project of government, thus mixing government policy and institutional politics. Now, this issue of reform brings together many of the reasons I have mentioned: I do not remember, however, overall plans of reforms - if not what my colleagues in the League spoke about just now - at least openly adopted by substantial parts of Parliament, with such an important alterative role in the institutional balance of our country. In the past, the constitutional revision was approached by most Members - or at least so it appeared to me from the outside, when I read it in the newspapers as a citizen - with a sort of respect and with that healthy fear of error that suggests prudence and leads to mutual listening in the parliamentary debate. On the other hand, let's talk about the most sacred rules for a state, those that arise in the great moments of solution in the historical continuum, like a great war, even civil, that led first to defeat and then to the liberation of Italy and that found in the process constituent synthesis, the defeat of violence and amnesty, if not forgiveness, for that part that had transformed our country into a hell without freedom. Now, in this XVII Legislature, full of important novelties, not least the drastic renewal of parliamentary representations, with the numerically significant entry of a new political force, it seems that the system, taking advantage of the initial uncertainty deriving from the inexperience of that force, instead closed itself curly, putting together its most conservative instances to protect itself. This trend, mentioned in a customary way, although with some improper behavioural innovation in the attitude of the institutions in the story that led and then accompanied the Letta Government (I think of the constitution of the Committee of Wise Men by the Presidency of the Republic or the presentation by the Government of a constitutional bill also preceded by the institution of the Ministry of Constitutional Reforms, another novelty), is manifesting itself in a completely new way with the Renzi Government. I am referring, for example, to the display of informal contacts, such as the Nazarene Pact, between President-Secretary Renzi and Dr Berlusconi, a pact often mentioned not only in the media but also in the Commission which, with a provocation, Mr De Petris asked for to be deposited in the proceedings because of its institutional importance. As I am referring to the replacement in the Commission of my colleague Mario Mauro and my colleague Mineo, whom I believe to be in accordance with the wishes of the Government and its head in particular. I am also referring to the attitude of constant psychological pressure to induce colleagues in the majority to vote for the reform even when they, on several occasions and with plenty of arguments in support, have stated that they find in it such criticalities as to consider it "unvotable." I am still referring to the almost predatory speed with which this debate has been carried out by the Government, speed and unwillingness to open confrontation that neither the experience nor the self-denial of the two rapporteurs has been able to influence significantly. All this for the methodical. It is certainly not better for the content of the reform. It nourishes itself in a concentration of power never seen in Italy after the war, Italy that, although with so many contradictions, in the first 20-25 years of its history has been able to rise from the war and become one of the most developed nations in the world. What gave rise to the problem of governability, from Italy of the sick parties to that of the ad personam parties, was the demise of the quality of political staff and this was born from the transformation of political representatives first into class and then into caste. And this transformation, let us be clear, has arisen from the demise of the democratic quality of the functioning of political parties. Today, the government exposes to the people what it believes are the obstacles to the exit from the political crisis: bicameralism, smaller parties, regional autonomy. And so, with the reduction to the minimum terms of these elements, it promises speed of decision. The feeling is that to treat fever you choose to lower your immune defenses. But how, from all over Italy is raised the demand for greater participation in political life and the Government chooses to reduce the spaces of participation? To have the Senate elected by the regional councils? To turn it into a hybrid between the State Conference - Regions and a post-work for regional politicians and mayors? To make access to the repealing referendum more complicated? To increase the number of signatures to submit a bill of popular initiative from 50,000 to 250,000? We also say that with the amendments to the regulations we will be able to regulate the actual debate in Parliament. Colleagues, we all know very well that this Parliament could have been made effective and efficient with the amendments to the regulations that cannot be done because all the reforms can be done except those that are effective and that make this Parliament effective. The most grotesque thing is that all this is promoted by a man who had become a popular champion of the fight against caste, who has arrived in the place he occupies starting from the primaries and asking for the support of all citizens. Now that you've arrived in the government, lock the doors behind you. Let me make a joke: it looks like the re-edition of the Orwell farm of animals. We at Italia Work in Corso presented a bill, a contribution to overcoming the perfect bicameralism, to try to make more efficient the system of legislative production and the control system of the Government. That's not what you wanted. The government does not seek this with this reform, nor do they tolerate proposals different from the solution reached at the Nazarene with Dr Berlusconi. Senator Finocchiaro, President of the Commission, said this earlier: the draft law of the Government has been corrected, but the constitutive presuppositions have remained those, nor on those it has been possible or wanted to negotiate. The Senate was not to be elective; the Senate was not to be relevant in the new institutional architecture. If this House were to dismiss the bill without any changes that would make it look as if it were upset that they are reporting that balance of powers that is absolutely essential for us, we, together with every conscious citizen, inside and outside this Parliament, will do everything we can to ensure that this plan does not take place. Let it be clear: the reasons for the failure of this reform season will be due to the arrogance and unwillingness of this government's dialogue. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u47	President, ladies and gentlemen, Mr Renzi (president who is not present), press and television stations accuse the 5 Star Movement of blocking the country through the practice of obstructionism and then I say clearly: I am proud to belong to a movement of citizens who instead want to save the country. We want the institutions to function, the Constitution to be applied as much as possible, of course, even improved, as has been explained previously, and rewritten but not of sound plant by a Parliament of elected with an unconstitutional electoral law and, even more serious, through a secret agreement between a former mayor and a delinquent. Sovereignty belongs to the people, who exercise it in the forms and within the limits of the Constitution, is solemnly affirmed in Article 1 of our Constitution. Our constituent Fathers, with this and with other provisions contained in our Constitutional Charter, wanted to reaffirm a basic and fundamental concept of every healthy democracy, further recalling this belief in Article 50 of the Fundamental Charter, allowing all citizens to be able to petition the Chambers in order to ask for legislative measures or to present common needs. Constitutional democracy does not end with the vote; it is something much more complex and requires the constant exercise of popular sovereignty. The constituent Fathers had understood this, guaranteeing the constant access of citizens to the political life of the country by means of important instruments of direct democracy, such as referendums and proposals of popular initiative law. Over the last twenty years, on many occasions, referendums have been invalidated by the failure to reach a quorum and many proposals for popular initiative law, such as the one we filed in 2007 and called "Clean Parliament," have remained in the institutional drawers without ever being discussed. Ensuring equal parliamentary dignity to the laws of popular initiative is one of the cornerstones of bill No 702, at my first signature, with the title 'Norman zero initiative and more democracy', which you have fully examined during the work of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. Taking inspiration from this bill, which, among other things, fully recovers a bill of popular initiative, I have tabled an amendment with the aim of binding Parliament to respond to and provide a response to requests from civil society, to those requests that take only to start from the needs, needs and difficulties with which Italians are in close daily contact and all too often disowned by those who are in these and other Palaces. Mr Renzi, appointed and not chosen by the citizens, with the text delivered to the House you have decided to do the opposite: to limit even more the areas of popular participation, increasing from 50,000 to 250,000 the number of signatures to be collected in order to be able to deposit a proposal of popular law. Sant'Agostino said that: "Humanum fuit errare, diabolicum est per animositatem in error manere," that is to say that falling into error was man's own fault, but it is devilish to insist in error by pride. It must be said that in these five months of government we have noticed that one thing you are not lacking: pride. It is with pride that you have also raised the number of signatures necessary for the submission of referendum questions by citizens from 500 000 to 800 000, with a preventive opinion on eligibility given by the Constitutional Court after the first 400,000 signatures and with a unclear rule for which questions must have an autonomous regulatory value. Mr Renzi, appointed and not chosen by the citizens, a reform of the second part of the Constitution based on monocameralism and the strengthening of the powers of the Executive, therefore reflected by the President of the Council, cannot fail to define itself authoritarian, just as the new majorities required for the election of the President of the Republic and the constitutional judges cannot fail to arouse concern. For the President of the Republic we had 1,008 great voters and the absolute majority to be reached was 505 votes; the new Parliament in common sitting would count 725 and the absolute majority would be 363 votes; with Italicum the coalition of majority would already have 354 seats in the Chamber and, with the addition of 10 senators out of 95, elect the President of the Republic alone. As with the constitutional judges: to the five nominations, the government can add five more with the help of very few senators, probably only four or five will be enough. Do we have any idea, colleagues who are absent, what it means to have 10 judges - so expressed - out of 15? The two main institutions of guarantee and control of the country, the President of the Republic and the Constitutional Court, are delivered to Palazzo Chigi. Mr Renzi, appointed and unchosen, what we are discussing here is a reform that is ultimately aimed at saving and greater governability and, together with Italicum, wants to achieve many other objectives: to make politics an even more closed space, as all the colleagues who preceded me have explained well. [[Applause from the M5S and LN-Aut groups]] , by further reducing the already limited margins of control of political activity by citizens and by saving those who have been convicted. What else can a House elected without preference and a Senate serve that more than having a legitimacy, so-called second degree, is clearly useless? Other doubts about the text make you think, such as the amendment you would like to make to Article 70 of our Charter. In the new paragraph 3 of the article it is expected to insert that every bill approved by the Chamber of Deputies is immediately transmitted to the Senate of the Autonomous States which, within ten days, at the request of a third of its members, can arrange to examine it. The reflection on the word 'dispose', I have already put it in my speech in the Commission and it seems to suggest that a simple request by the minority is not enough to be able to examine a measure, but instead an ad hoc resolution by the House is needed so that this can happen. This is a lexical question that should have been resolved - and it does not appear to have been done in this text - because it could have a significant impact on the scope of the Senate's action; the power of minorities to have the Senate pronounced on the bill produced by the majority of the other House, in case the request is sufficient, could theoretically be very large, in the case of deliberation, instead, it could be virtually null. My proposals, which I have also tabled here in this House, aim to extend the referenda rights with the introduction of the confirmation referendum, which would allow citizens, if they consider it necessary, to express their views on the effective entry into force of the laws adopted by Parliament, as well as on the proposed referendum, which would allow Italians to express themselves on proposals for the law of a promoting committee and to oblige the legislator to implement the proposal within a period of time. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u51	Madam President, let the previous speaker finish the applause. Indeed, some of the ideas and passages of your speech are acceptable, especially when you call all of us to the importance of the votes we are about to take in the next few days and to the delicacy of the subject we are dealing with. Talking about constitutional reforms, in fact, means touching one of the backbones of the country and touching on concepts often very important with regard to democracy and therefore the daily life of our citizens. I began to deal with reforms in Parliament during the parliamentary term from 1996 to 2001. The first intervention I made in 1996 in the House spoke of reforms and recalled the need to go in the direction of implementing a federalist reform in our country, recalling also the importance of certain concepts such as those of Rousseau on small patriae, concepts of 1700, but that gave that freedom of autonomy and self-management to the many realities and communities that are present in Europe and that today rightly do not recognize themselves within the European Union, a monster that was built only on the basis of substantially economic values. I say this because talking about Europe and talking about reforms that concern our Constitution basically means talking about the same thing, it means talking about the application of the principle of subsidiarity, it means talking about so many values that are shared by the many peoples who live within the countries of the European Union and who should find their recognition in their respective Constitutional Charters. I was saying that we tried in the legislature from 1996 to 2001: you will all remember the experience of D'Alema's bicameral, which is aborted without even arriving in the Chamber, and then end that legislature with the approval of a messy and confused change, that of Title V of Part II of the Constitution, approved with only four rejection votes by a majority that was to chase the League. That majority, faced with the expectations, needs and desire for freedom and self-government of the North and the communities of Padana, who wanted to be able to freely dispose not only of the tax revenues, but also of the decision-making capacity to affect the daily problems of their citizens, with the then President of the Council Amato, made a constitutional change that ultimately did not turn out to be a correct change and that caused so much damage, so much so that from 2001 to 2006, first with Minister Bossi and then with Minister Calderoli, began a further path of constitutional changes that led to the definitive approval (with the two readings of Chamber and Senate) of a text that, however, was rejected by the citizens with the referendum confirmation. However, that text was rejected not so much because of its content, but because it was sold badly on a political side that had the interest in discrediting the opponent. The content of that proposal, which then the citizens did not accept (the citizens of the North understood it, while the citizens of the South understood a different message, which went against them, and that was not true), already spoke of the end of the perfect bicameralism, already spoke of the reduction of the number of parliamentarians, fixed the problem of competing competences and went in the direction of involving the territories and regions within the election of certain fundamental institutions, such as the Constitutional Court. That reform - alas - was not approved by the citizens, but if it had passed that referendum, since 2008, with the elections after the entry into force of that constitutional reform, we would have had a Parliament with a single Chamber that gave confidence to the Government: a Parliament that probably would have had a Berlusconi Government from 2008 to 2013 and a Bersani Government (not a Renzi Government) because whoever won the House anyway won everything, from 2013 on an indefinite date. That reform - I repeat - has been rejected, because there was a short-sighted policy that is right to remember at this time too. [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group]] Today we are facing another possibility: we are in 2014 and we are starting a path that takes up again the constitutional reform, thanks also to the contribution of President Finocchiaro and rapporteur Calderoli, who has certainly treasured and knows how important it is to re-produce certain issues, because precisely the citizens want in particular that constitutional reforms foresee this kind of change. We are - I repeat - facing a very difficult path, different from the longer one of 2001-2006, and certainly different - at least I hope - from the messy one of the end of the thirteenth legislature that should lead us to touch on the same issues and should convince all of us of the need to put an end to the perfect bicameralism. We have remained the last Western democracy in which there is perfect bicameralism. There are countries in the rest of the world where bicameralism exists, but not perfect bicameralism. There are countries where even, within federal or federal states, there are parliaments that have almost equal powers in the Central Parliament, but there is no country that, like ours, provides for a system that is now obsolete and outdated and that no longer meets the needs of functionality of the country and the need to give timely answers to the problems of citizens. But a monocameral system cannot be a dictatorship. It cannot override the right to apply a proper democratic formula and, therefore, it will be useful to counterbalance this choice with an electoral law that will certainly have to take into account the new delicate institutional structure that will be created. Of course the problems we are talking about are important, but let me remind you that, alongside institutional reform, there are important problems that all citizens live on a daily basis, such as work, employment, taxes and illegal immigration: on these I see that the Government continues not to give answers. [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group]] Perhaps he can give answers in terms of constitutional reforms because he has some good advisors close to him who indicate the way and the solutions that must be pursued. I believe that all these reforms that we should evaluate and vote on in the next few days should be carefully analysed. I am not in favour of an excessive haste in the analysis of important measures such as this, but above all I believe that the Government, or rather the majority that wants to reform the Constitution, will have to win vote after vote in this House the confidence of the majority of this House in order to be able to say that that reform is a necessary reform of the Constitution. He will have to make it paragraph after paragraph, word by word, because there are still many open issues, starting with the number of signatures for the repealing referendums, to which we have referred several times, the number of Members and many issues that also concern the Constitutional Court and its composition, or the competences of the Regions, which cannot weigh in the background. I have heard several times, even a moment ago, that our Constitution is the most beautiful in the world. I frankly know some of them that are just as beautiful, but our Constitution has a peculiarity: the first part of the Italian Constitution enunciates the fundamental principles and the reference values that are inescapable and certainly sacred, but they have only one defect, that is, they are poorly implemented and are disassembled, so much so that the Senate, together with the Chamber, has approved laws that often forgot its content. My hope is that in the amendment of the second part of the Constitution, or rather in the part relating to the reform of the constitutional bodies, first of all in Parliament and with particular regard to the instrument of the referendum, a different system will be used, which leads everyone to say that what matters is not so much the image, it is not so much the choice with effect that must be made and therefore the word (which can be tweeted to please the citizens giving the idea that everything is changing and then actually not changing anything), but that we must find solutions that allow us to give practical, pragmatic and concrete answers in terms of operating methods and in terms of the effectiveness of our work and therefore of effectiveness towards the citizens. An old 18th - century Irish philosopher said quite simply that useful institutions are those that do not complicate the lives of citizens. Let us at least do this within these reforms: let us make the necessary reforms and do them seriously. If they are made in a serious way, the League will be there; but to do them in a serious way means to respect the needs of all the components that are present in our country at territorial and political level. If we do this, if we can think seriously, without rushing too much, taking the right time but certainly looking at word for word what can be the practical consequences of the application of the constitutional norms that we will go to change (so what will happen in this country with the new norms), then we will surely be directed on a method of profitable work, otherwise we will have to return to raise the flag linked to identity, to the fact that there are peoples willing to remain within certain territorial boundaries only if they are respected in their individual identities. We believe that going in the direction of giving strength to this country with new instruments, with the possibility of seeing everyone respected is the road that must be pursued. However, we do not think, unless there is this mutual respect, this solution, that we can remain silent and accept in silence in the name of the necessary reforms to make any solution that this Government proposes to us. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u52	Senator Collina is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u53	Madam President, members of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, what is before this House today is a draft law on reforms not only constitutional but institutional; it is a broader vision of changing the structure of Parliament, the process of formation of laws and a policy of containment and revision of public expenditure. In the game of reforms, the action of the Government and the Democratic Party in recent months has wanted to play as a protagonist, with the aim of an institutional change, a real renewal in tradition, which looks not only at Europe, but above all at the heart of the country. This is not the place to call on the many study committees that have intervened to reason on constitutional reforms (from the Commission Bozzi of 1983 to the Committee Speroni of 1994, to the working group, first, and to the Committee on Reforms, then, last wanted by President Napolitano), it is enough to emphasize that all the study committees so far established have reached, albeit with different solutions, the shared need to overcome the perfect bicameralism. In 1982 President Giovanni Spadolini, drawing up his decalogue for institutional reforms, identified the problem of complete and incisive constitutional reforms capable of neutralizing the causes of dysfunctions that too often have paralysed the efforts of governments. This quote only serves to rip the veil that so many spread to confuse the crisis of representation with the functioning of the State; on the one hand, the mechanisms are not up to modernity and, on the other, the models of representation no longer know how to collectively interpret personal needs. Today, thirty years later, the weight of this problem has been raised by the Renzi Government, which has undertaken to promote a constitutional bill that overcomes the perfect bicameralism, in order to speed up the iter legislation and to contain the costs of public spending with the abolition of organs and bodies now exceeded, such as CNEL. The biggest challenge of the new Senate will then be to integrate the autonomy, which reproduces in the Upper House the division of various levels of territorial authorities, as in Article 114 of the Constitution. If the House of Deputies is to represent our form of government, the Senate of the Autonomous States will have to reflect the form of state. A necessary pluralism of voices, which was already sought in the Constitutional Assembly in the writing of Article 57, in the definition of that Senate on a regional basis. A Senate of the Autonomies, therefore, able to dialogue downwards with the territorial authorities, but also upwards with the European institutions, where we are protagonists today with the Italian Presidency of the European Semester and since the past European elections on 25 May. We are, therefore, at the stage in which the approach to the first stage of constitutional reforms is outlined and the thought necessarily runs to the words spoken by President Giorgio Napolitano in his speech to the Chambers on the occasion of his installation, more than a year ago. The re-election of the President of the Republic finds no precedent in history, but it was necessary to overcome the difficult state of impasse, certainly created by the electoral result but also by an attitude defined as irresponsible and closing by factions who did not intend to create the premises for the formation of a government. Hard words, his clear, clear words and a reference to the responsibility of all of us parliamentarians and to those words must always be reported the experiences of successive government for the exceptionality of the commitments to be faced. In short, in those words is the reason why the Government presented a constitutional bill against 53 draft laws filed in the Senate, because - and we must state this clearly - these reforms have to do with the lives of citizens and businesses. If there are people in this House who think that reforms are, of course, to be done, but citizens are interested in real things, then they reflect on the role of politics. Politics must give concrete and punctual answers, but in a blocked country such as Italy today, politics must find the strength to identify and correct those elements that make the times of solutions disproportionate to the times of problems. The proof that Italy is a blocked country is, for example, that the drive to reform so many ancient worlds that the Renzi Government is attacking, collects both approval and resistance, but fails to generate movements of self-form. Everyone is waiting to see if politics (and Renzi today is putting us face to face for everyone, whether you want it or not) will live up to the challenges that concern itself. The great push that Renzi Government has given to the reform process remains an objective element, a merit and a proof of the conviction and courage that is going down to restore authority to politics. I therefore believe that the moment of reform before us can be a good time - it has been said - for renewal in tradition, a real revolution in the sign of constitutional maintenance rather than a rewriting of the Charter. Ultimately, a modernisation of our democracy. Precise, targeted actions to ensure speed, effectiveness and efficiency of parliamentary action. Reforms must serve to improve the institutional structure, first and foremost to overcome the degenerative practices of recent years, from omnibus decrees-laws, to the continuous position of the question of trust by the Government (often on texts that have been merged with maximed amendments), to the abuse of decree-laws even when the requirements of necessity and urgency do not exist. Let me now propose some political assessments in view of the extent of the passage that is about to take place in this House. If we have listened several times to calls to separate the contingency of the political context from the content of the reform, it is useless to hide as much as this contingency weighs on the assessments and attitudes that have been measured in recent weeks. Despite this, the meeting point in the confrontation between government, political forces and Parliament - and here I would like to thank the rapporteurs, President Finocchiaro and President Calderoli for their work in the Commission - is to be welcomed for its value, both in terms of content and in terms of synthesis. Keeping Parliament at the centre, without touching the parts related to the Government, is a qualifying point of the reform that does not substantially change the balance between powers and guarantees, highlighting primarily the desire to give greater efficiency to the state. It is disproportionate and instrumental to evoke authoritarian drifts, when a House is sufficient to express confidence in the Government, to legislate and to vote on the state budget. But a Chamber is not enough to make the institutional and territorial structure of the Italian State responsible for a process of harmonization of all national and regional laws that must generate, in combination, opportunities and safeguards. In other words, today it is not a question of discussing the role of the reformed Senate in relation to today's Senate within which we hold this decisive discussion, but of discussing how, starting from a substantial mono-cameralism, a useful presence of a Chamber of Self-government is structured, whose presence seems obvious because it is adapted to the configuration of the State outlined by the current Constitution. This is what we are doing today, knowing that immediately afterwards we will also have the responsibility to deal with the rules by which the House is elected, since we do not act in conditions of legislative vacuum, that is without an electoral law in force, but equally conscious of the fact that the electoral law must bring out from the ballot box, that is to legitimize, a political majority in the House able to express a government. Moreover, this happens regularly, for Regions and Municipalities without there being any or any assessments today concerned about the democratic depth of these levels. Proof of this is the passion and participation that go through all parties and groups that face municipal and regional elections. On the contrary, the stability achieved by local institutions such as Regions and Municipalities will be counted precisely in the reformed Senate which will draw great incisiveness from this second-degree nature. A final consideration must be made, then: too many attempts have been in vain, too many work of Study Committees remained in the dusty shelves of Italian constitutional science, too many reforms missed, too many reforms approved but never implemented, just think only of Article 11 of Constitutional Law No 3 of 2001 which has provided - still to be missed - for the participation of representatives of the Regions, autonomous provinces and local authorities in the Parliamentary Committee on Regional Affairs. The hope, then, is that this reform will finally achieve that multi-level constitutionalism, which operates on a new framework of Title V of Part II, a redefinition of the legislative procedure also because of the European legislation that Parliament transposes every year; and that on the day of this week (or next week) in which we will approve it, can be considered the date of a first goal, the date of a new beginning and no more than the umpteenth missed opportunity. Or perhaps King Renzi is so naive as to think that mayors and governors should come at their own expense with sandwiches and sleeping bags and come to Rome on foot? [[Applause from the M5S Group.]] Or worse: he thinks that naive are the citizens and wants to download the costs of the transfer of the new senators to the agencies of origin, replicating the game of the three cards to you so dear, that we have already seen many times done in this majority. As if the citizens didn't realize that to pay those costs would ultimately always be the State and therefore ourselves! And was it worth amputating our Constitution in this way, concentrating more power in the hands of the Government and depriving citizens of the possibility of choosing their representatives directly, in order to obtain, in the end, a savings of 43 million euros? By removing the end-of-term allowance, the supplementary health care of senators and bringing the gross allowance to 5,000 euros to all parliamentarians would have a savings of about 60 million euros. Without considering that if every parliamentarians returned the non-expensive part of the lump sums and not taxed - let's say it still loud! - Who knows what other savings we'd have! [[Applause from the M5S Group.]] Besides, do we want to talk about the vitals? This is what we would like to ask today of this same left, which in 2001 had the unfortunate courage to overturn Title V of Part II of the Constitution in an attempt to give the State a federalist imprint, instead giving the Regions more autonomy and competences and party power. From this wretched autonomy, especially financial, that has allowed to waste freely the money of the citizens, used for expenses in weddings, villas, travels and various vacations, it was then had to resort to raise the taxes to citizens impoverishing them and lifting the bread from their mouths to shut the holes of the budgets of the Regions. This is what the left has done by reforming Title V: raising a herd of regional Councillors who have plundered the electoral funds, made available to them by the Regional Councils. What's Renzi doing today? He even wants to give them a double assignment! Phenomenal, a genius, man! Moreover, there is a gigantic question in this case of reform, which concerns the right to change the Constitution or, better said, the right to change it on the part of this Parliament. The question, which might appear as rhetorical or even provocative, is actually not for two reasons, that the Constitutional Court's judgment No 1 of 2014 brought together in a single envelope: firstly, the fragile legitimacy of the current elected parliamentarians through the majority prize; secondly, the method of choosing the current parliamentarians, namely that they (and we equally) were appointed by political parties through blocked lists and not chosen by citizens through preferences. Both these points have been sanctioned by the Consulta that, precisely, with the judgment n. 1 of 2014 has sanctioned the manifestation in-co-sti-tu-zio-na-li-tà - I spell it out to you, at least you understand it! - of the electoral law known to all as Porcellum. Common sense would suggest going to elections first with a constitutional electoral law and not with Italicum, born from yet another secret agreement between Renzi and Berlusconi. Then, with a new Parliament, finally legitimized, we could put our hands on constitutional reforms. Another example of constitutional reform that has been adopted quickly and without a real parliamentary debate is the inclusion in the Constitution of the rule on budgetary balance, which we so much regret today and do not want anymore. That's nice. Let's do it and then we'll undo it. Another issue that arises from the question of the substance of the content of the constitutional reform is the subject of the initiative to amend the Constitution. As everyone knows, this input is traditionally the responsibility of Parliament in our system, and this is because of the obvious and obvious reason to prevent a momentary political majority, hopefully, from intervening on the rules that affect all the players in the system. The fact that not only the initiative has been taken by the government, but that the margin of discussion has been greatly reduced with a text that has been largely armored by extra-parliamentary agreements and secrets signed at the Nazarene with a prejudice, the fact that the President of the Council has repeatedly threatened the spectre of early elections to overcome the internal reluctances also to his party and that he has even come to expel members of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, rejecting to express ideas other than his own, says a lot about the imbalance of the powers already existing in favour of the Executive and the harm of Parliament. In the reform that the government wants to approve there is still a clear imbalance in favour of further strengthening the government. If there is a power today that must be limited, it is that of the Government. As President Grasso recently reminded us, Parliament was and is the place of representation, the place where the country finds its synthesis, the place - to use the words of Giacomo Matteotti, of which this year is the 90th anniversary of the murder - where the Italian plebs must cease to be plebs and become conscious, mature and referee people of their own destiny. This must be the Parliament for all the citizens of the Republic, not a bunch of insulting Premier cards by those appointed and in its full power. It is necessary, on behalf of the sovereign people, for Parliament to regain the dignity that must be conferred upon it and for all of you parties, no one excluded, to have lost it by dragging it into the mud of your actions over the last thirty years. Now, with your reform, President Renzi is planning to bury him permanently. We're not in. The citizens don't fit. [[Applause from the M5S Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u56	Senator Mauro Mario is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u57	Madam President, I would like to thank the President of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, Senator Finocchiaro, and Mr Calderoli for their attention and dedication in these days of work on a debate which apparently has as its object the amendment of the Constitution. Thanks also go to the undersecretaries Pizzetti and Scalfarotto, who also showed with their similar attention from the Government. I said that the change of the Constitution is only the apparent object of our meeting here today because there is not so much at stake the constitutional change that allows the overcoming of the perfect bicameralism. I say this without raising my voice, because one thing is no longer true if you shout louder. And I say that without using hyperboli, because it is not the violence of images that can betray or better translate the meaning of things. However, the meaning of my speech is to make it clear to the Italians that the object of our reunion is not the overcoming of the perfect bicameralism, but the beginning in our country of an authoritarian democracy of Putinian style [[Applause from the M5S Group]] which finds its foundation, not in what is written in this constitutional bill, but in the joint application of what will be the result of our constitutional revision with the so-called Italicum, i.e. with an electoral law designed in such a way that the institutions become the ground of application of the principle of who, being in the majority, wants to make institutions in his image and likeness. In fact, at the time of the rediscovered democracy, in 1948, the Constitution of the Italian Republic was made to defend those principles that had been felt - I would say - nostalgia in the time of abuse and violence. Today, unfair rules are being made to defend not new principles, but new princes. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] , their strategy, their interests, the abstract idea of a simplification of democracy that is opposed to the simplicity of a true democracy, that is, made with adequate balance of powers and objective dependence on the will of the people. In short, Renzi's Senate is a courtesan harp that, instead of overcoming bicameralism, will make the whole Parliament insignificant and confusing. Just now that there could be a balance between representation and governability through overcoming the perfect bicameralism, giving a guarantee function to a elected Senate in a proportional way that does not give confidence and has no ordinary legislative power, right now it is moving towards a system that essentially cancels the functioning of constitutional guarantees that are based on qualified majorities. The combination of Italicum and the current reform project leads to: essentially rendering inoperative the guarantees laid down in the Constitution. The most serious problem is the reflection of the reform on the Presidency of the Republic and the Constitutional Court. When the constituent in 1948 drew that architecture assumed the proportional system and introduced a series of rules that somehow had to force it to find an agreement between the political forces for the election of the offices of control and guarantee as the Head of State and the Consulta. Now the 58 regional councillors participating in the election of the President are abolished, the Senate is reduced to 95 people, plus those of presidential nomination who are no longer alive but increase by 5.5% the total of the Assembly; however, with Italicum, which assigns to the majority 354 MPs, it is enough to have 9 senators to elect the President of the Republic and the constitutional judges of parliamentary appointment. The Head of State then appoints the other 5 constitutional judges, and in this way the majority is awarded 10 seats of the Council. In fact, is this a result or not equivalent to a regime? I would now like to make a second comment. Renzi says we're going towards a German model: it's not like that. Firstly, Italy is not a federal Republic but a regional State; secondly, the President of the Republic in Germany has smaller powers, and the Registrar has more powers, but is subject to considerable scrutiny and the Constitutional Court works differently. The German Parliament, that is, is elected with a rigidly proportional system and the only limit is the 5% barrier threshold. Above all, however, the reform of the Senate meets once again with Italicum, which is designed in such a way that, out of absurdity, eight percent of the votes are hypothetically enough to win 54 percent of the seats. I remember that the so-called Acerbo law, which cemented the fascist regime, provided for the majority prize to be taken if the most voted list had at least 25%. That is the third point. We people are for a Senate elected by the citizens and this reform instead provides a bad example of political nomenclature folded on itself and almost afraid of the will of the citizens. In the Commission's text, these appointed senators will be able to legislate on constitutional laws, and I find it unacceptable that second-appointed parliamentarians should be able to decide on such fundamental issues. It is also obvious that in the presence of a hyper-majoritarian law, such as Italicum, the Senate must retain certain prerogatives in relation to the major issues, rights, guarantees, religious freedom and civil rights; they are matters that are taken from the Senate as it comes out of the Commission and this cannot agree with me. Let us also say that tomorrow, in our country, with a hyper-majoritarian law, a majority will win the elections, for example, an extremely secularist who decides to introduce forms of euthanasia for children with serious pathologies or for the elderly. In the face of these risks, we need a Senate of guarantees that will provide a further moment of assurance and reflection. I would like to make one more point: it has been said that Europe is waiting for this reform. False. Europe is waiting for the reform of the labour market, of a tax system that is among the most unjust, of justice, which is now outside the standards of the acquis communautaire, but not constitutional reform. I would remind you that the European institutions never had a trade union about the fact that the Member States were even monarchies or republics. The important thing is that they are democracies, efficient and respectful of the will of the people; what we will no longer be when we become an institutional system in which citizens are forbidden the free expression of the vote. I conclude by saying that if senators owe loyalty to someone or something, this is the nation and certainly not a secretary, a leader or a party discipline. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u61	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, despite us, we are called upon to change the nature of our constitutional order. What we are called upon to vote on today is undoubtedly an epochal reform. (17.04) (Follow BUCCARELLA). Yes, the problem is not in the Constitution. The frenzy that seems to have possessed this Government is not compatible with reforms of this importance. The haste is never a wise counselor and seems to have forgotten that the last historical character, who liked the masses so much for its decisive decision-making, has led the country to live the twenty darkest years of its history (and I am not referring to Berlusconi). I did, and I realized that many things that today take for granted and want to cancel with a sponge stroke are actually painful conquests, the result of the tragic experience then just passed. It's not just about today. Almost seventy years ago we were talking about the French system, the German system and the US system; we were talking about what was the best way to avoid the mixture of the various powers of the State and what were the appropriate and better counter-measures to keep the whole system in a stable balance. The use of one term in place of another was perplexed. Entire days were spent searching for the words that could best express that particular concept, avoiding that then that phrase could be the subject of wrong interpretations or bad faith. All the other powers didn't have any.
