And what is at stake is not only constitutional democracy, but perhaps democracy as a whole: a reflection for everyone. [[Applause from the M5S Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u62	He's enlisted to speak Senator Cocianchich. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u63	Mr President, I have listened with great interest to many of the previous speeches and I must say that I would have been very impressed if Italy were a small island in the middle of the Pacific Ocean, where all the industrial or cultural relations were managed within a small community. Unfortunately, or luckily, that is not the case. Italy is a great industrial, manufacturing country, a country that has an important financial dimension and is an important actor in international trade. Today we are experiencing a profound economic crisis resulting from a fact that has a global dimension: today investments are made in countries where the conditions of the world of work are more advantageous, where there is greater efficiency of the bureaucratic and administrative machinery and where justice is more efficient. Italy, among the countries of Western democracy, remained the last, the least efficient, the most neglected from the point of view of foreign investments and, therefore, the one in which the labour crisis feels more heavy. Everyone today fills their mouths by claiming that they want to think about what is really important, that is to give back work to our young people and to those who have lost it, but we forget that Italy today is neglected by the great international investors and therefore we remain marginal in the commercial and industrial traffic of the Western world. This is because our institutions are now rusty, unable to respond to the challenges posed. Therefore, reforming our institutions is not the idea of a young dictator, as in some way Renzi is painted by those who preceded me, but it is the awareness that today such reform is a necessary game to give back momentum to our economy, without which any hope to give back strength to the world of work, to give back wealth to our fellow citizens turns out to be a mere chimera. In the draft law presented this morning, there is one element which has been highlighted little but which I would like to stress: the European dimension of reform. Article 55, as amended by the Commission's work, highlights the aspect I would like to comment on: today the Senate will also be recognised as a link between the European institutions and our national and local institutions. This is a fundamental issue, because Europe, which I regard as the only possible response to the great economic crisis that gripes us, is today our container, our fundamental horizon, also from the institutional point of view. Everyone today is talking about a perfect bicameralism that you want to modify. Few people realize, it seems to me, that in reality we are not in a bicameralism today, but we are living in a much more articulated system than sometimes, a little to laugh, I have defined imperfect tricameralism, because there are also the European institutions. In fact, the situation is even more complex because at European level we have not only Parliament, but also the Council and the European Commission. There is an internal dynamic between the European institutions which must be taken into account. These institutions were not present when our constituent Fathers drew up the Constitution that we want to amend today, because neither did the European Community exist at the time. Today we are talking about the European Union with 28 members and a highly articulated legislative system. At present there is a lack of democracy at European level, because decisions are taken not primarily with the Community system, therefore with the involvement of representatives of the different Member States within the European Parliament, but with an intergovernmental system. The famous fiscal compact, for example, is the implementation of an intergovernmental system of enhanced cooperation. It is on this system that we must succeed today if we really want the principle of democracy, representativeness and participation of our citizens to have a voice. We risk pointing to the moon but only looking at the finger, if we think it is just a problem of relationship between the Senate and the House and between the Senate and the Government. Today the important games, the fundamental decisions for our future are taken more in Brussels than in Rome. 70% of the legislation in force in Italy originates in the Community, indeed in Europe and does not always have a parliamentary origin. It is therefore important that the Italian parliamentary institutions make their voice heard in the debate taking place in Brussels, at European level, between the various governments. A Senate dealing with European issues, which serves as a link between the European Union and the regions, will play this fundamental role, which seems to me much less secondary than a deliberate debate on whether or not to have an allowance, to have an elected Senate with a system of first or second degree. These, in my opinion, are details of the great challenges we face. Today we must ensure that the voice of Italian citizens, the voice of Italian companies, the voice of Italian interests can echo strongly before the European institutions. Today the Italian Parliament, after the Treaty of Lisbon, has a say and must make it heard. Unfortunately, the cumbersome nature of our procedures, the eternal delay in our speeches means that the voice of the Italian Parliament is considered to be somewhat marginal and irrelevant. The opinions given are not considered: anyone who has gone to Brussels to take a tour can see how inadequate the considerations of the Italian Parliament are judged, for the reason that they are always late, they are always capitious, they do not focus the problems. On the other hand, we must equip ourselves with a Parliament capable of dealing with the problems, capable of having impact plans and schemes on the basis of which we can understand the economic and social impact of certain financial measures, and of being able to react and bring our voices, both in ascending and descending stages, in a corresponding way. It seems to me that this is the great democratic challenge that we are facing, because today we must find the strength of a voice that is very dim at this moment. I am therefore grateful to the rapporteurs who have accepted the suggestion (which I too gave) to open a window on the European issue. I believe that the statement of principle, now contained in Article 55, will enable us to work, I hope, already in a further amending phase of the text of the Constitutional Bill, but also in the process of implementing ordinary rules, on a reform of Law No 234 of 2012, in order to provide concrete tools to have a stronger influence at European level. I repeat that this is the real scenario on which we measure ourselves. In conclusion, I believe that what is offered to us is a truly extraordinary historical opportunity. I have no sense of inferiority compared to the great constituent Fathers who preceded us and I believe that none of us should have it, not because we are who knows what great lawyers (I personally consider myself as a dwarf on the shoulders of giants), but because this is our time; if our constituent Fathers had felt in awe before Justinian, we would probably still be in the legislation of Roman law. I repeat that today is our time: we must seize it and do our best to try to give voice to our interests and also a little voice of hope and idealism in a Europe that is currently too focused on economic aspects. Italy has this voice, this idea and this heritage, but it does not have the capacity to express them in an effective political action. I would therefore like to stress the linking dimension between the European Union and the regions, which are directly interlocutive (including on the basis of the European Treaties) of the European Union, have a role and a way of making the voices of local institutions and citizens heard at a territorial level. I believe that Parliament will sum up - I hope also choral - all this, which will give strength and prestige to our country. [[Applause from the PD Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u64	Senator Maran is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u65	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the aim of the reform is to provide a solution to the problems and malfunctions of our institutional system. The reform therefore aims to overcome the perfect bicameralism, to limit the relationship of trust in the House and to make the second Chamber the representative body of the autonomy. A goal, that of the regionalization of the Senate, which has been discussed for at least twenty years. In most cases, the criticism of principle of the plant (which we have also heard today) reproduces the old rational anomalies, and they also propose new ones, and are mostly the result of an antihistorical complex of the tyrant. It is hardly appropriate to point out, in fact, that the Assembly wanted a weak system of government, so that no political side could win to the end and no one could be cut off entirely from the Government. A slow and repetitive Parliament would have been a useful brake, specifically aimed at exhausting any majority out of the ballot box. The presence of two chambers invested with the same political powers and legislative powers is the most blatant contradiction that has no equal in other parliamentary democracies. A wreck of when - as Scoppola recalled - each of the sides feared on April 18 of the other. It is not by chance that every time the issue of constitutional reforms is put on the agenda so as to give the Italian governments the same institutional force that governments have in all other European democracies we are back to talking about an authoritarian turning point or we are pulling out the P2. Since the beginning of the crisis of the representation of traditional political forms (the Bozzi Commission, in fact, was established in 1983), institutional reforms have been discussed in Italy, more or less since the end of the 1970s, i.e. since the beginning of the crisis (the Bozzi Commission, in fact, was established in 1983), the fundamental question is always the same: can Italy become a "normal" parliamentary democracy, or not? That is to say: can it become a democracy in which those who win the elections can implement their programme within a framework of guarantees provided above all by the Constitutional Court and in which the valorization of autonomy takes place without conflict between center and periphery? The political crux of the reform of bicameralism lies here. As always the discrimination, the watershed is among those who want to take advantage of the opportunity offered by the economic crisis or the need to adapt our institutional system (little important) to trigger a process of alignment of Italy to the best European standards and who thinks that this project is unrealizable because - not even to mention it - Italy is different and because in Italy these things cannot be done. In other countries, those who win the elections in the first chamber rule, and the guarantees that they do not go beyond the limits set by the Constitution are given by the body of constitutional justice and not by a Senate designed to do, in a completely abnormal way, to counter the government. In other countries the second Chambers that do not give confidence and where the Government cannot put trust have an equal power only on constitutional laws and little else because they must not prevent governability. In other countries, the issue of the development of rights guaranteed by the Constitution also prevails everywhere the first Chamber (of course, under the constraints of the Courts), because almost all the key legislation launched by the parliaments in fact affects the rights and is included in the platforms with which you apply for the government of the country. First of all, then, we must ask ourselves what is the reason for being a second House today, also because if we do not find a compelling reason the Senate would be better to abolish it altogether. Moreover, the hypothesis of a pure and simple abolition of the Senate cannot be seen what contraindications it would have, especially if the aim were only to save money. In my opinion, it does not make any political and institutional sense to assume that the second chamber should have its justification in a political role of counterweight to the first, whether explicit (maintaining a double trust relationship) or implicit (widening the area of bicameral equality laws in an unreasonable way and so on). In this way, it would not represent the autonomy but would be a worse duplication of the House, because the only sure guarantee would be to undermine the governability, leading to a great coalition in fact with the main parliamentary groups of the Senate even those who had lost the Chamber. It does not escape anyone that a Senate without the trust relationship, but also free from the question of trust where the government cannot put trust, could, could, could have, paralyse the government and the majority in a completely abnormal way compared to the European parliamentary democracies. Moreover, what should be the guarantee, especially if we consider that this is a requirement that the Constitutional Court already entrusts to the Constitutional Court, as well as to the President of the Republic, to an increasing extent in recent years? Constitutional rights must be protected by the Court, if ever by prior recourse to it by parliamentary minorities. But if the Constitution is not violated we are faced with choices for which the parliamentary majority must take responsibility, as is the case in all European countries. As I see it, the fundamental reason for a second Chamber is to be the place of connection with the territories by completing and correcting the design begun with the reform of Title V. Outside of this scheme, no second Chamber is justified. On the contrary, the urgency of the reform is justified precisely by the need to create institutions in which the Regions and local authorities are called to "collaborate" the definition of public policies, so as to avoid that the laws of the State try to impose unilateral choices on subjects who will then be called to apply them and who will fatally express their dissent by feeding the litigation before the Constitutional Court. There are those who claim that a reform that is copied by the German or American model, born in other cultures and in different historical circumstances, is badly adapted to our situation. And it is true that after years of lack of promises today federalism, i.e. the demand for autonomy and institutional reforms, does not enjoy great popularity and seems to become a problem. But it wouldn't be bad to keep in mind that, to put it with Ilvo Diamanti, that of new rules and new institutions is a road imposed by emergencies and fractures that we have chosen precisely to heal the contrast between society and state, the contrast between society and politics, a contrast that is not resolved because now of marches on the Po no more and the newspapers have stopped talking about Veneto as if it were the Ulster. If nothing else, because one of the components of federalist thinking has always been the search for spaces of autonomy and freedom for citizens, precisely through forms of containment and articulated distribution of public power. There is no precise index to distinguish a federal system from a regional system: both try to combine a measure of unity with a measure of diversity. The distinction looks essentially to the past, to the way in which the system, the state, has been formed and the regional systems are the result of a process of decentralizing the functions of a centralized state, so Italy but so also Spain, France and even the United Kingdom, because wherever the demands were more or less the same. And in the face of the great changes that have occurred in the last seventy years in the organization and in the same function of the State, more or less the same have been the problems that the systems of relation center-periphery have had to face and more or less the same have also been the answers that have been tried to elaborate. The fact remains, however we see it, that our Republic is no longer that of before, it has already changed and today it is unfinished, in half. However consider it, the reform of Title V, which even Augusto Barbera had defined as "sgangherata", wanted by the center-left at the end of the legislature under the second government of Giuliano Amato and confirmed by the popular vote in the referendum of 7 October 2001, has brought to the Part of the Constitution that regulates relations between State, Regions and local authorities, profound changes, and it is from here that we must start. That Title V has operating defects is a shared opinion. The first critical point is precisely the excessive faith placed in the division of subjects. The legal order and its laws do not lend themselves to being included in specific subjects and in any case the pre-determination of the subject cannot be exhaustive: always new and ever new subjects will emerge will be the interweavings between one and the other matter. The border line is uncertain by definition, but mobility in itself is not bad, since all modern federal systems or regional systems are leaning for a flexible division of skills and a subtle game of interference. This mobility becomes, and has become in our case, a very difficult problem to solve when the instruments of coordination are lacking, particularly when there is a lack of a branch of Parliament that can take on a role of mediation and absorption of conflicts between state and self-government. The lack of the parliamentary place of mediation is the second and main critical point of the reform, not to mention that, in the absence of an institutional compensation room for interests, uncertainty has generated many conflicts that are donated to the Constitutional Court, which has been forced to settle issues that have a high rate of interpretative opinability and therefore a high rate of politicity, which does not favour the composition of interests, but has encouraged the emergence of the conflict and the judicialization of relations between central interests and interests of the territory. In short, this transformation and then metamorphosis of politics into legal litigation has imposed on the Court - just to understand us, as its President has emphasized - a role of supplement not required and not welcome. So, since the current bicameralism is unsustainable, the question is very simple: do you want to adjust and revise the reform of Title V, as is necessary and urgent, or do you think that that reform should be reset in order to return to the previous status quo? That is the choice we must make. It seems to me that to go back is an unrealistic objective, as well as unsatisfying. It would suffice to ask: if much of the administrative implementation of the laws weighs on the regions and on the local government, how can one govern against or even without the system of autonomy? Not only that, but the action of the regions and municipalities depends largely on the provision of social services, the implementation of state laws and policies, public spending. Putting the centre of coordination between the legislation of the State and its implementation in the territories within the constitutional institutions is today an indispensable necessity for the proper functioning of the constitutional system. But if we really have changed our mind, if we really think that there is no way to make the Regions work, if we think that these have become, as I have read, the repository of corruption, the place of collusion between private interests and political caste, if we think that the reform is a gift to the evil of the local caste, then the Senate would be better to abolish it completely and recentralize. On the other hand, if we want to give an effective role to a second Chamber, which no longer has the vote of confidence, the best solution is the one which assigns the new Senate a very limited but incisive role in the legislative process, on the basis of a real need to complete the reform of Title V of Part II. Also because Title V, even if it is written, rewritten and again rewritten, works only if it has as terminal a Chamber in which the regional legislators are responsible. So if we want to maintain the Title V plant, we need to make a regional chamber. How? Of course, you can do it in many ways, as always. But let's start by saying that that of the direct electivity of the second chamber is not at all a rule, but all the opposite. This is not the case in Germany or Austria, it is not the case in France, not to mention the United Kingdom or Canada: I can make a list of second rooms. I personally find that the most sensible model is that of German federalism, in which the second chamber, the Bundesrat, is not elected, but is formed by representatives of regional governments, because the basic law specifies that both parties (through their parliamentarians to the Bundestag) and the Länder, that is the regions (thanks to the members of the regional executives) cooperate (the German word used is emblematic: mitwirken, that is to say they work together) in the implementation of German politics at the federal level. The role that the Senate must play, in a proper federal or regional structure is clear: not only the Chamber of Reflection in relation to the deliberations taken in the first branch of Parliament, but, above all, the place of representation, in the decision-making process of the federation, the regional system, the territorial authorities that make up it. It is not by chance that in Germany the second Chamber is not organized in parliamentary groups, like the other (the Bundestag), and that, as a rule, in its Commissions the more technical guidelines prevail over the party ones. Therefore, if we want to maintain a regional structure (and finally want to make it work), we must do the Bundesrat or something that looks very much like it, because we must avoid proposing again in the second Chamber the same oppositions (dependent on the influence and party belongings) that characterize the daily life of the directly elected branch of Parliament, thus ending up distorting the Senate in an improper instrument of opposition to the political line of the parliamentary majority, more likely in the event that the prevailing political orientation in the second chamber is of different sign from that represented in the first. This is what the Germans are trying to avoid, because it would be the entire regionalist structure of the state organization that would cost them. I take note of the fact that the text that came out of the Commission (which takes the road of differentiation on the Austrian Bundesrat model, just to give an example) deviates, in some respects, from my approach and I believe that the text should be amended, going as far as possible in the direction I have tried to illustrate. First of all, I consider the maintenance of senators for life inappropriate: I believe that the Senate should represent territorial interests and nothing else. It would also be preferable, as far as the voting mechanism is concerned, to adopt, in the Bundesrat, as an ordinary method, the unitary vote per regional delegation. Each region decides how to vote in the Assembly and expresses its votes in a unified way, which means that the vote of the Region is declared "a package" by a single spokesman, chosen from time to time by the different representatives, according to the German model (which then, in principle, is not unlike that of the Council of the Union: it is not that nothing is invented). Obviously, if all the members of the Senate of the Autonomouss, in this case of the territories and the Regions, represent precisely the territories and if they are not directly elected as senators, in my view it makes no sense to prohibit the bond of mandate. It also seems inappropriate to me to keep the metropolitan cities among the entities that constitute the Republic and then quote them, later, next to the Municipalities and the Regions. It seems to me that it is inappropriate to place an unnecessary body, such as the Metropolitan City, on the same line as the necessary bodies. Already with the Provinces he had to back down and he would have to learn the lesson. I try to quickly define two more points. Many of the innovations introduced in Article 117 of the Constitution are largely already consolidated by the Constitutional Court's judgments, so that transfers of jurisdiction in favour of the exclusive power of the State and the introduction of the supremacy clause do not change the current framework, except in the sense of settling what the Constitutional Court has already drawn up. Finally, the introduction of a regional chamber, as was also stressed in the report to the bill, and its association in the formation of laws allow to disengage the system of division of competences from the strict listing of subjects, a technique that has demonstrated all its ineffectiveness. The most important defect is the absence of a discipline of collaborative procedures, because moving from the dispute downstream to the collaboration upstream requires some procedural device that serves this objective. In the amendments I also refer to another proposal, which has always fascinated the doctrine: the introduction of organic laws, in our system in particular with regard to electoral law, therefore a legislative source that goes between the Constitution and ordinary laws, as happens in France and Spain. In conclusion, Mr President, my impression is that criticism of the reform system in principle is born of a nostalgic way of looking at the subject: it is noted that it is no longer possible to practice the old form of participation in politics, but it is believed that that specific form of participation in politics, that specific political-institutional system are the best and therefore tries to get as close as possible to that model and to save as much of that experience as possible. But this attitude stems from a static and conservative vision and our inaction, our spirit of conservation could in fact harm the strengthening of democratic institutions, contributing to their weakening. We have made it a fetish, but the Constitution cannot be a totem to venerate; it must be a living, active instrument, capable of ensuring cohesion and regulating the full development of democratic life. To understand this, from the post-war period up to 2002 Germany has changed 51 times the Basic Law and the 2006 Reform, precisely the one aimed at improving the decision-making capacity of the federation and the Länder, has modified 25 articles of the Basic Law; and if the Germans, in order to settle their federal system (and they are Germans...), have intervened more than 50 times, obviously we (who are Italians) will be forced to intervene, to adjust and to put our hands back again and again. And the ordinary maintenance of an innovative set-up such as the one we started with the regional reform is normal. If we can also reopen the issue of extending the presidential electorate in this House, the work will be considerably improved. [[Call the president back.]] I'm done, ma'am. Even so, however, we can say that most of the anomalies of Italian bicameralism, due to the conditioning of the cold war in the original constituent process, which forced us to reject solutions similar to this one (read the debate at the time), are on the way to sunset, are about to disappear with at least thirty years of delay. That is why I also consider the acceleration at this stage to be meritorious. [[Applause from the SCPI, PD and PI groups.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u69	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the absence of Mr Boschi in the Chamber, whose presence is absolutely essential, is a matter of concern to us in this debate. Among other things, it appears that the extension of the House's work, without any continuity, was requested by the Minister himself. It is therefore regrettable to see here, in one verse, the absence of the Minister and, for the other, the constant presence of the rapporteurs who, up to this moment, have not abandoned the ranks where they sit to listen to all our speeches. With regard to our intervention, we particularly wish to talk about the question of the reorganization of State-Regions competences and, above all, the reform of Title V of the Constitution and Article 117. In fact, this issue is embodied in a history of reforms that has taken place in recent years with absolutely questionable effects. In essence, these reforms are the result of an insane operation of many who have not been able to equip our country with the necessary innovations in the institutional landscape of the state order. It is recalled that the 2000 reform, which was then adopted hastily in 2001 by the then centre-left majority in order to try to win over the electorate of the North League, which - it is recalled - was indeed at that time the bearer of the demands of institutional reform and among the first movements to express doubts precisely about it, i.e. a reform that has actually demonstrated its lack over the years, particularly in the various appeals submitted to the Constitutional Court. This shows how that reform had been poorly structured and probably poorly written. Indeed, the whole problem of the competence shared between the State and the Regions has given rise to considerable doubts of an interpretative nature, doubts which were then devolved to the Constitutional Court, thus confirming the incompleteness and darkness of the same regulatory dictation. Since that reform there has been no simplification or improvement and legislative intervention has not been sufficient to ensure greater involvement of the self-government and to achieve that path which, for years, we have considered necessary - as we now believe - towards a change in the federal sense of the state. But Italy absolutely needs change. It is essential, essential to reform the modernisation of public administration and to adapt it to the needs of society; to enhance territorial autonomy; to respond to the needs of business and citizens; to finally make this country competitive; to simplify compliance and adapt our legislation to that stepmother Europe that, as long as it exists and imposes its dictates, we will have to respect. But a great change was contained in that reform repeatedly cited by those who preceded me, in particular by Senator Calderoli and other colleagues of the Northern League Group. The 2005 reform was a courageous reform: a courageous reform that, however, did not see the other parliamentary groups, the other movements and political parties, who demonstrated an absolute blindness in the face of what could be a true reform. Now we are talking about so many issues that were included in the same reform. In that passage, which was well explained by the rapporteur, Mr Calderoli, as well as by Senator Stucchi, it was seen that, practically, that choice (I refer to the inappropriate choice of rejecting that reform with the referendum) was actually the result of political considerations, without looking at the true goodness of the reform. Today, again, in this House, we have to talk about reforms: reforms that, however, have been transfused into the initial text of the Government, into a plan that we consider absolutely contrary to what should be a modernisation of the state. In fact, we have returned, almost anachronistically, to assign all those competences to the central state, strengthening the same. It has not been enough every year to demonstrate how this centralistic mode of operating in public administration and in governing the State has created a series of difficulties that - let us remember - were those difficulties that gave rise to the North League itself. In fact, the North League was born bringing those complaints and problems that concerned the management of a centralist state. Now, as if to say that we have run out of history, we have to go back. Fortunately, the work of the senators in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs has pointed out what were the problems inherent in this structure. Not for nothing, fortunately, there was the amendment Finocchiaro-Calderoli that upset the original layout of the text proposed by the Government. Therefore, it has eliminated the competing competence and distributed the competences; exclusive competences are maintained in the regions. Perhaps this work has succeeded in overcoming, with regard to at least this part of the reform, the distortions inherent in the text of the Government. However, this does not mean that the work is done. On the contrary, as the rapporteur mentioned earlier, there are still persistent problems, and there is the possibility that this House will make further improvements to the text that came out of the Commission. It is enough just to recall that clause of supremacy - already the same name is clarifying - which would allow the State, however, to invoke to itself the legislative and regulatory power in some matters if certain requirements exist. Italy is a homeland of all leguleans and doctors of the law: it is very easy to work on these issues, on what to consider and whether or not the assumption exists, thus leaving the possibility that conflicts and interpretative difficulties are still open. However, what we would like to remember at the end of this whole debate on Title V is that we have to think about what it means to talk about Regions even today. Let us remember that Article 114 of the Constitution states precisely that our order is made by the State, the Regions and the Municipalities. In fact, it starts right from the municipalities, to get to the regions. Therefore, it is a matter of recognizing that the Regions ontologically exist; and they exist even before the ordering. The Region, in fact, is not a creation of the order, because the Region exists in our Country: there is a cultural value, traditions and a particular economic and social context that distinguish one Region from another. It is also what, in some ways, makes the strength, characteristic and beauty of Italy: it is precisely this differentiation. But it is a differentiation that requires a differentiated treatment: we understand it very well. For years we have been bearers of the northern issue and have repeatedly denounced the difficulties inherent in Italy of the coexistence of very different economic and social views. We confirm this still now: the Regions are different from each other. Those who live also the world of law well understand my reasoning: in my profession I discovered, precisely from my direct experience, how there is some difficulty in handling some issues in one Region instead of another. We therefore believe that we cannot be blind enough to think that the cultural difference, the way of thinking and the economic and social situation do not require a regulatory framework that takes account of this differentiation. In some ways the way of thinking about law in the Mediterranean is the result of a history that has distant origins: even only from the Giustinian Digest, in which we practically find our legal system, we see the difference between our way of seeing the regulatory system and that of the Anglo-Saxon countries. But we also think that the Mediterranean peoples are very different from the Anglo-Saxon peoples. They are different cultures and the law must comply. We also think that the right should be attached to a certain context. For this reason we believe that certain competences must remain with the Regions, because only in this way can one manage and govern in a wise way a particular territory. At the end of all the speeches that we can make we are now engaged in this sitting to the full to discuss reforms, but let us not fall into the error in which we fell in 2001. The haste is a bad councilwoman and this reform, like that of constitutional reforms in general, cannot only please the belly of the citizens. We have to realise that when we are sitting in this House we are not just pickers of votes, we are not simply those who have to get on a stage and find the topic that emotionally can please the citizen most. [[Applause from Senators Candiani and Puglia]] We must also think that we are structuring a constitutional system, which is something fundamental: we will be able to work (we and our successors), in the parliamentary dialectic, within a structure that is the Constitution, the constitutional order. Senator Casson is right (I do not want to steal his words, but I can also draw a hint) when he says that this reform does not stop with this Government, but will survive ourselves, and the real effect of everything we will do - whether our voters like it or not - we will probably evaluate it in many years' time. And perhaps in many years we will come to say: in that place we could do better, but we didn't do it because at that time the political choice was the one that linked to an orientation. On the other hand, we have to get out of this in some ways, we cannot pursue a supposed consensus of the electorate. We must create good solutions for our fellow citizens. These are things that we are not doing, unfortunately, for other issues as well. You can't build legislation in an almost emotional sense, a regulation... [[The microphone automatically switches off]]

ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u74	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, we are faced with one of the epochal moments of a parliamentary term; imagine that in such a hurry - in a Chamber with such rarefied presences and with the times compressed, from 11 a.m. to 10 p.m. - the Italian Constitution must be changed in itself symptomatic of something that does not add up. I will not linger to mention the constituent Fathers, nor to remember that they were directly elected by the Italian people, chosen from among the main protagonists of political and cultural life, and I do not offend anyone if I repeat that I do not see Calamandrei, I do not see Aldo Moro in this assembly or in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. But those who are preparing to reform the Constitution in general silence (because also the vestals, the Zagrebelsky, the Flores d'Arcais, the Travaglio, those of the Forum of Assago, the red, orange, purple peoples have been lost in the street), in this muffled air, where the mass media, the main newspapers, the ancormans, the so-called intellectuals are silent only because this reform comes from a young man who gets the sympathy of the Italian people, seems to be here making a decree on Parmesan cheese or Parma ham. We are radically modifying Title V of the Constitution, and we are doing so - let us be careful - not with a law of parliamentary initiative but with a bill proposed by the Government, which is the denial itself of the most correct procedure to modify the rules of the game. It is as if the rules of the game changed the captain of the team who at that moment holds the ball. I don't want to bother Tacitus, but I can't help it. With the arcane expression imperii Tacito reminds us that power, when it wants to be absolute, never chooses public forms: when power wants to be damned, idolized by the crowd never makes the crowd participate itself, that is to say the rulers, of why the rulers change the rules. At the time there were the famous three capes: salus publica (I did this thing for the health of the people), bonum publicum (I did this thing for the good of the people), and (the most torn of the three capes, what the President of the Council Renzi wore), intent, good intentions. Arcana imperii, that is to say all that the people must not know. In fact, power is in the hands of a class of elected people, of an esoteric circle of people, the only ones who can know the secrets of power and thus exercise it. Mr President, I would also like to see a classic definition of liberalism (in a Chamber where everyone, either in a ruffle or in a raffle, refers to liberalism), that of Lord Acton, who stated that liberalism is essentially the limitation of powers; Karl Popper replied to it, arguing that it is not important in a society to know who is in charge, but to know how to control who is in charge. Thus, democracies in the developed countries are basically the means to change power and government without bloodshed. It doesn't matter who replaces the former Government, but it's important to know what the tools are for an opposition, a minority, to be able to subject those who govern to the tightest and most constant criticism and to the tightest and constant control. What do we do? We get rid of one of the two chambers on the basis of two false assumptions, which are then our Prime Minister's cheap propaganda. The first thing we give to drink to the Italian people, afflicted and distracted by its economic vicissitudes, is that we must eliminate the perfect bipartitism... I apologize, I meant perfect bicameralism: it is a lapsus linguae, because in the end we will verify that the suppression of one means the revival of the other. In the last legislature, a draft law was unanimously approved by this Assembly, presented by Senator Caliendo, in which it was planned to reduce Members by 200 units and by 150 units senators, dividing the competences by subjects between Chamber and Senate: it was over all the manfrina with which it is said to want to speed up and not to waste time! Today, with Articles 10 and 12 of the measure under consideration, there is still a sort of ping-pong, where it is not clear whether the Senate should say yes or no or ask for something in three weeks and, in turn, may propose legislative proposals to the Chamber of Deputies on the basis of matters. In addition, the following 30 days for promulgation should also be added to the 60 days mentioned by my colleague from the M5S Group. Therefore, a constitutional structure is changed without changing the state model. Someone, at every step, repeats to us the examples of France and Germany. It should not be forgotten, however, that in France the President of the Republic is directly elected by the citizens with his powers; moreover, in Germany, where the Senate is the Chamber of the Länder, there is a proportional system, and there is no Italicum or the "Porcellissimum" that you say you want, where we continue with the camarilles of party secretariats, not allowing the elector to choose the MP. Therefore, let us continue to cultivate a class of courtiers and rulers, who must - obedient - ensure that the prince or the holder of political power and party power is not disturbed, with the penalty of non-appointment. I would like to remind Luigi Sturzo when he admonished us in the book "Against Proportionality" saying that the ruling classes do not improve because of the electoral system: the ruling classes improve if they reform the parties by making them bodies governed by public law subject to third control of all the rules (not only those of the budgets), of all the rules of internal democracy that the parties freely intend to give themselves and that are codified in their statutes. We cannot understand, for the truth, what is behind this urgency, what horizons this haste wants to reach. Maybe taking the country to the elections next May? [[Applause from the M5S Group.]] Why should we foresee justice in the hands of the Regions? Some may have escaped, but in constitutional reform we are talking about justice of peace and proximity. This means that not only are we creating the conditions to have a differentiated health care between citizens living in Piedmont and others living in Calabria, but that we will also have a justice that, in the distance from the places of justice and in the difficulties of reaching these institutions in the territory, in some regions is a justice closer, faster, more efficient and in others a little less, because the regions are perhaps more careful, as often happens, on other issues. I, who feel like a senator of this Republic, although expressed by a Region that has made a big contribution to the unity of this country, enter into crisis and I would like the Government and friends of the majority to make me overcome this crisis through the adoption of many amendments that we have proposed, and that are not all referring to the issue that is becoming the center of the world: the direct election of the Senate. In other countries the direct election of the Senate is not there, but there are good institutions, and in particular there are good Senates, which are not fictions of parliamentary assemblies, but have precise rules and competences, clear popular mandates. Do not tell me that Italy is like Germany, because Italians are not like Germans and the Italian constitutional order, the fundamental one, is not like German. We have a different story from our German friends. Before I give my consent to this constitutional change, then I want to be convinced, and there can only be one way of doing so: thinking about the changes and proposing good solutions. [[Applause from the Aut Groups (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT) - PSI-MAIE]] , M5S and Misto-SEL and Senators Candiani and De Pin). PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. He is registered to speak Senator Corsini. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u77	Mr President, I must confess that I am speaking with a certain awe, because I am neither a jurist nor a constitutionalist, and I am aware of the complexity and delicacy of the matter. So I will make some premises, perhaps more challenging than the discussion on the microphysics of standards, and then a development. In recent days there has been a often distorted debate. Well, I want to say that, in my opinion, consent enables the government, not the exercise of command [[Applause from the M5S Group]] There is no restraint, no sabotage, no ambush, but only the passion of those who, with a straight back and with a high head, defend their beliefs without looking for any gain; and it is also worth the civilization of language and the purification of the terms with which we value our arguments. Also because I do not hear in this Chamber the creator Spiritus of whom Benedict Croce spoke in a memorable speech on the occasion of the approval of the Constitution. A second premise. There is no doubt that this House has a formal legitimacy; in this respect the Constitutional Court has made this clear in an incontrovertible way. But, I wonder, we have proper ethical and political legitimacy. [[Applause from the M5S Group and Senator Di Maggio]] , being all of us expression of an election-name through clearly defined unconstitutional norms? But there is a fundamental problem that intrigues me culturally, and it is the prophecy of Tocqueville. As you know, Tocqueville in America denounces the risks of mass democracy, of democracy that is at risk of paternal despotism and tyranny of the majority: that tyranny - he writes - which demeans men without tormenting them. In a passage that I judge of extraordinary foresight, Tocqueville writes: In politics (...), as in philosophy and religion, the intelligence of democratic peoples welcomes simple and general ideas. The complicated systems are repugnant to it and it willingly imagines a great nation in which citizens all resemble a single model and are directed by a single power. What does this far-sighted prophecy have to do with the problems we face today? Here lies the origin and the foundation of my fundamental disagreement with the text, not only with the rather grammatical one proposed by the Government, but also with that which is the result of the serious and committed work of the Commission. I was for some time vice president of the ANCI and therefore I think no one can accuse me of underestimating the perspective of a Senate of the Autonomouss, beyond the fact that, fortunately, the text has been corrected and today we are talking about Senate of the Republic. I believe that, in the face of the fold and the risks of an involution of the democratic rule, which belongs to the components of contemporary coexistence - precisely mass democracy - the great challenge we faced was to play on other ground, that is to say the very scope of a Senate of guarantees. My colleague said well before: democracy is also the theory and practice of the limitation of power. This is the problem of contemporary civilization. Moreover, in order to work towards a view of self-government, there are already institutions in our system for this purpose. There is another subject that worries me, which raises me questions of right: the link between representation and democracy because on the whole I see - without dramatizing - a risk of involution, where I see the exercise of an improper role on the part of the Government. It does not belong to the liberal-democratic tradition that the government is promoting constitutional laws, nor does it belong to the liberal-democratic tradition that the government imposes - because this is the true fact - a text on the Commission. Having said that, I am also entirely convinced of the need to overcome the bicameralism of equality and symmetricism. On the other hand, I am not convinced by the prospect of abolishing bicameralism tout court. How does this debate actually come about? It was born on the basis of a prospective distortion. On the one hand, there is the motivation for the cost reduction, which is certainly a problem; on the other hand, there is the motivation for the simplification of procedures, although from this point of view the statistical data that is reflected in the work of our Study Service tell us that the so-called shuttle is operating for 3.5% of legislative procedures and that the time for the conversion of decrees and the approval of the law rules lies perfectly in the European average. I would like to point out here that there is a further distortion which I would like to stress. As I said, I am not an expert on constitutional law and I find it difficult to get into the microphysics of rules, but I think I can say that the root of the crisis is parties and parties. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] Ours is a crisis of the institutions because it is a crisis of the modalities of formation of the will through the mediation of the parties. It is a crisis that affects the institutions, but it is a secondary crisis, which proceeds from the drift that the Republic of the parties has taken. I cannot pretend to myself, because intellectual honesty is not an option: the Commission has certainly made significant and positive changes and has acquired landmarks that I can certainly share. I would like to mention a few of these changes here. First of all, the reduction in the number of senators: it was a proposal which we had initially made with some of our colleagues and which had been looked at with suspicion: I note positively that this port has been reached. Among the amendments I would like to mention the embryo of a statute of opposition, the abolition of the CNEL and the determination of how referendums should be carried out, the validity of which must be related to the number of voters at the last elections. Let's try to think what evolution the history of Italy would have had - I don't think I exaggerate by saying the history of Italy - if these norms had been adopted previously. The history of Italy would change and the same identity and configuration of our political system would change. I recognise, as mayor, that, compared with an initial proposal for state-owned neo-centralisation and the reduction of regional self-government, it has certainly gained from the Commission's work the system and the role of self-government has been enhanced. All this satisfies me. There are, however, aspects in this text which I cannot agree with. First of all, the lack of election of senators. No one, even among my colleagues who are more calm and aware of my parliamentary group, has yet offered me an adequate answer to a question that I am raising: what would have prevented the people from deciding which regional advisers to act as senators at the time of their election? Moreover, how can a non-elective Senate be thought of in relation to a Senate of the Republic which has constitutional and electoral prerogatives? [[Applause from the M5S Group]] This contrasts with any orientation of liberal democratic inspiration. Again, matters of bicameral interest: if it is true that there is a problem that is at the heart of the crisis of mass democracy - as Tocqueville taught us - why exclude from the competence and bicameral interest the great themes of religious freedom, bioethics, civil rights, the protection of linguistic minorities, that system of guarantees that constitutes the foundation of the rule and democratic principle? Let us try to imagine an elected majority with an ultra-majoritarian system which decides, on the one hand, the legitimacy of euthanasia for children and, on the other hand, the abolition tout court of Law No 194, depending on political orientations. Is it conceivable that there is no institution in our system that functions on the basis of proportional election in terms of guarantee and balance? Moreover, the election of the President of the Republic is a crucial issue, taking into account the creeping presidentialism that has characterized the last years of the affair of our Parliament. If we do not expand the audience of the great electorate or reduce the number of parliamentarians, we come to the paradox of a party secretary - today of my party, tomorrow of another party - who is also President of the Council and to some extent has the possibility of election of the President of the Republic, who then has to choose a significant proportion of judges of the Constitutional Court. The number of Members: what prevents a number of Members from being present in the European average? For Italy there would be about 500 Members. What is the reason for this? I understand the problems of practical usefulness for this legislative process to be completed, but what does it prevent in principle from addressing this problem, as well as the problem of immunity which must be traced back to the issue of insinability? And again, I quote the way in which regional councillors are elected, where the theme of an exorbitant presence of parties returns. It is time to give back to politics its ambition and its dignity and it is time - as one of my masters, Mino Martinazzoli, said - to return the parties to their role, beyond any exorbitant invasion of the field. [[Applause from the M5S Group and Senator Di Maggio.]] After all, if anyone had proposed - but I see that a colleague who now stands up to the role of a great inquisitor had done so - the Bundesrat, I was very ready to support it. In France the electoral audience is about 195,000 voters and French law now makes incompatible the role of parliamentary, mayor and regional exponent. Finally, the last question: the link between Italicum and Senate reform. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] It is a political link, it is a constitutional link, it is a legal link, because, even more so, the Senate of Guarantees is an indisputable requirement in the presence of a majority law which, in its present state, even assigns 18% to those who obtain 37%, that is, more than half of the consents obtained. I believe that those I support are legitimate questions. Finally, my political formation has rarely led me to oppose or to express dissent or contrast with my party's positions. This is not an easy problem for me to solve, but to a journalist who asked me the other day what is the answer I give to the fact that, with this position, I end my political career, I said that I found the answer (I am a practicing Catholic) in the letter of St Paul to Timothy of the liturgy last week: I fought the good battle, I finished the race but, dear God, I kept the faith.
