ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u79	Mr President, representative of the Government, fellow Senators, we are discussing a massive constitutional reform without an extensive debate taking place in the country; Indeed, almost to take advantage of the fact that, between mid-July and mid-August, Italians are notoriously distracted and unwilling to follow our parliamentary work. It seems a well-established practice in our country to dismiss the worst reforms in this period: I dare say it is an all-national tradition. Of course, the voting process will go far beyond August, as it is a constitutional law. But this first vote will strongly mark its next path. The size of the majority that will - if it goes there - be formed on this draft law cannot fail to have consequences in terms of subsequent parliamentary passages. So there was not, as I said, a sufficient debate in the country on the substance of the content of this reform, but some key words were passed very well. Too bad they're all wrong. On today's Republic, just to quote a daily newspaper, Ilvo Diamanti said: Sure, today the reform (better, the abolition) of the Senate will arrive, precisely, in Senate waiting for a first vote. The abolition? What abolition? But let us also leave this daily newspaper which, at least formally, is not yet the official voice of this Government. Let us see, for example, the tweet of the President-in-Office of the Council of 18 January last, in which he said: Get the senators out of here. If this provision is closed, Italy #changes the other way around. Now, off with the senators, it means closing the Senate, that is, repealing it. But, then, of the two l'one: or I understood nothing and this reform that we are here to discuss is that of the repeal of the Senate, just as it was made to believe the Italians and how it continues to make Italian citizens believe, or the Italians are made to believe one thing and here it is made a completely different one: "via the senators" - as the President of the Council writes - does not mean at all that the current senators, democratically elected, are replaced with other senators appointed by the parties, as provided for by this reform. If Renzi had written "via" the elected senators, ahead with those appointed" the citizens would have had a representation of the reform certainly more likely, but in that case they would certainly not have supported it. However, in its sense of eliminating the Senate, it was a hypothesis that would have found a broad and cross-cutting political convergence in this House, but not this reform that simply replaces elected senators with appointed senators, because they are more controllable. There is another misconception in the country that the savings for the state's coffers, resulting from the implementation of this reform, would be huge, represented in over a billion euros. Of course, this is a false conviction: the entire budget of the Senate, which has been mentioned several times, is known to be about 500 million euros and is largely composed of fixed costs, compared to which neither Renzi nor any representative of the Government or majority groups has ever spent a word: it is the salaries of staff, pensions and the maintenance of the buildings, to mention the most important items. So I ask myself and ask you: to whom is it agreed to spread these false beliefs, if not to those forces, obvious or occult, who want this reform at all costs? Falsity and misinformation come to this day. Let me give you another example, just one of the many, which is an exemplary article in one of the national newspapers of greatest circulation of not more than a few days ago: praising, once again, the reform of the government, has found nothing better to do than to indicate, in savings account on allowances and reimbursements to the senators, an expected savings of 500 million euros. The title was this: The Italians will no longer vote for the Senate. 500 million less allowances. Now, apart from the fact that the reimbursement of expenses will also be provided for future senators appointed by the parties, it is clear that the published figure deviates by at least 450 million euros from reality. I would ask the Presidency, if possible, to be able to attach the article to the report of the sitting, or at least its references, as a splendid example of italic disinformation. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] I would like to thank the President for his positive response to my request. [[Applause from the M5S Group.]] [[A summary of the President's agreement.]] The point is, ladies and gentlemen, that if people had been told the truth they would have understood that the mountain of this reform gave birth to a mouse. The replacement of an elective Senate with another appointed by the policy will, if it is okay, lead to savings for the community of 50 million euros. This awareness would be enough on its own to ridicule those who presented this reform as a model to save money. A saving of 50 million euros represents, to understand us, less than a fortieth of what this same majority has given to the operators of the slot machines thanks to the tomb condon of no more than a few months ago. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] Fifty million euros represent less than one hundred and fiftyth of the gift made to the banks with the decree IMU-Bankitalia [[Applause from the M5S Group]] and even represent less than a thousandth of the expenditure expected for the purchase and maintenance of the F35. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] Unfortunately, if we persist as a country in remaining at the last places in the world rankings on press freedom there will be a reason. The objective of pursuing a significant reduction in costs, in the Senate as in the other House, would be much easier and immediately reachable without having to compromise on the level of political representation and therefore more generally without having to give up a slice of democracy. It would be sufficient for all the parliamentary groups to adopt virtuous behaviors such as a 50% reduction in competences, public expenditure reporting, the waiver of electoral contributions, the waiver of the end-of-term allowance, the return of allowances paid and not used. These simple innovations would be enough to guarantee a saving for the state of about 500 million euros: this time it would be real money and not invented. It can be done and we can prove it, because we in the 5 Star Movement are already doing it. So there are two different visions of Italy in this House: Renzi's vision is to take away, wherever possible, slices of sovereignty from the citizens, as if they were the cause of the state of crisis in our country. This vision would like to strengthen the power of the parties and in particular of the secretary of the relative majority party, but also to reduce the role and prerogatives of Parliament for the benefit of the powers and prerogatives of the government. Another view, speculating, would instead want the exact opposite: it considers the parties the main ones responsible for this economic, political and social disaster; it calls for greater participation of citizens in the political life of the country; it wants to strengthen the powers and prerogatives of the main institution that represents them, namely Parliament, calling for a more incisive action of Parliament's control over the Government and, on the contrary, a substantial reduction of powers, powers and funding to parties. For this reason, many issues, such as the direct election of new members of the Senate, have raised positions that are difficult to reconcile. For this reason, a number of dissident MEPs, members of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, have been forcibly expelled by using a procedure of dubious legitimacy in order to impose the approval of this constitutional bill on the Commission. It is therefore particularly urgent to judge the legitimacy of these extromissions, which are considered to be detrimental to Article 67 of the Constitution. If the extromissions were to be declared unlawful, what fate would it have to reserve for the measures adopted in the meantime by an illegitimately constituted Commission? It has been stated that this reform aims at overcoming the perfect bicameralism and replacing the Senate of the Republic with a new Senate of the Autonomous States. This innovation would like to give greater effectiveness and efficiency to the legislative machinery. But to the precise and precise listing of the reasons why the objectives announced will not be grasped at all, the usual shredded rhetoric of reforms that are good in themselves, in a country like Italy that opposes every change has been opposed (in clear absence of arguments). Now, we all agree that our country needs major reforms. I am thinking in particular of the reforms needed to restart the economy according to the most modern standards of sustainability, I am thinking of the emergency work, of a reform of justice brought to its knees for too long by Silvio Berlusconi, I am thinking of new rules for a new ethic of politics, today increasingly absent at all levels. But the reform we are debating is profoundly wrong, based on incorrect or even false motives. It represents for Italian citizens the most classic of the passages from the pot to the fire, promoting the substitution of one elective political class with another more difficult to sanction, as it is released from the judgment of the popular vote. A underlying ethical motivation for the reform also appears to be totally unfounded: just think that the Regional Councils in which the suspects sit are currently 18 out of 20. In recent years in the Regions we have seen a rate of public morality of almost zero, I would say sadly in line with the national average! Not to mention that the new Senate would be based precisely on the principle of the accumulation of offices, offices and armchairs, while currently the incompatible senator must opt for one of his posts and then remove the cause of his incompatibility. It would be precisely the regrettable simultaneous presence in several armchairs the essential and essential element of the senator. Far from reality, it also seems to be the alleged increase in the effectiveness and efficiency of the legislative process introduced by the reform. If the current system has extended evidence of the fact that, with the existence of political will, the times of dismissal of a bill can be only a few days (as in the case of the Alfano praise, unconstitutional, but dismissed by the Parliament in record time), the new system is announced, as far as possible, even more cocky and marked by new complex relations between the two rooms, by the result easily predictable in terms of its efficiency and its effectiveness. With the new Article 70 of the Constitution, the Senate will be able to continue, for example, to intervene on all acts and laws. There is no precise demarcation of subjects, unlike what happens in other countries with a bicameral system that clearly differentiates skills, in order to avoid unnecessary overlaps. In short, the legislative process, rather than being efficient, is further slowed down. It is sufficient that one third of the members of the Senate are of different political colors than the majority present in the House so that they can request that all - and I stress all - laws be discussed in the Senate, obviously for obstructive purposes, thus slowing down the promulgation of all measures for at least two months. The lowest common denominator of this reform project is the imbalance of the powers of the state, all to the benefit of the executive power. On the contrary, the latter should withdraw, in order to allow the legislative power to regain its own space, reserved for it by the Constitution. In terms of ethics, with a view to a profound reform of the system, we should rather have assessed the introduction into our country's legal system of the recall mechanism, i.e. the possibility for voters to request the early termination of the electoral mandate of their representatives, when they had been guilty of conduct incompatible with the maintenance of their functions. Predictably, the introduction of this institute would substantially change the composition of Parliament. Whole political groups formed in disarray to the popular will manifested in the elections, such as the Autonomous and Freedom Group and also the New Right Centre itself, would never have been born or could be quickly disconfessed by the voters. If that were not the case, they would acquire, thanks to the presence of the institute of the recall, a political dignity that does not belong to them at present, not having arrived in Parliament - as political groups - by virtue of an electoral competition. Another point to make concerns the instruments of direct democracy such as the referendum without quorum, the overcoming of the repealing referendum and the bill of popular initiative. A greater participation of citizens in the democratic life of our country, thanks to these institutions, should be unanimously desired by all political forces. In this measure, however, there is a dangerous opposite trend, represented by the considerable increase in the signatures needed both for the referendum and for the filing of draft laws of popular initiative. It is therefore limited rather than encouraged the direct access of citizens to politics. Bicameralism is a guarantee of greater transparency of parliamentary work and greater thoughtfulness of choices. Let us think, for example, of the unfortunate approval of the House only a few days ago of the amendment which introduces the direct responsibility of the magistrates. Premier Renzi was quick to say that there is no need to worry and that it is not a problem, as the matter will be settled in the Senate. Of course, but did you think that, precisely as a result of your reform, the Senate's intervention will no longer be possible? Our bicameralism could easily be lightened by significantly reducing the total number of MPs, Chamber and Senate, in order to meet the sacrosanct need to reduce the costs of politics, but without jeopardising the very essence of the democratic process of forming laws in our country. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] Moreover, it is paradoxical that this very Parliament, which is called upon to make such an important choice of constitutional reform, is essentially a Parliament with limited legitimacy because of an electoral law which is known to be unconstitutional in many respects. Some final considerations. The prediction of five senators appointed by the Head of State struggles to find some logic, also in view of the many polemics raised by almost all the Groups on the occasion of the last nomination of senators for life. The reform of the Parliament under discussion was proposed and, given the tone, I would say imposed by the Government. The fact that in a first phase it had to be approved even by 25 May, that is, in time for the electoral deadline, shows the instrumental finalization of this initiative freed from any serious and coherent process and linked essentially to criteria of political opportunity. It has been said that the current Italian bicameralism is a unique that must be overcome. All right. But if we overcome it with this reform we replace a unicum with a new unicum, falling into the same mistake, this time much more difficult to justify. With this reform the so-called guillotine is constitutionalized, with blocked vote and without amendments, with vote item by article. It will be enough for the majority forces to waste a little time in order to obtain the very little flattering result of gagging oppositions and preventing the submission and voting of their amendments. I conclude with Senator Zanda's words on the occasion of the examination of constitutional reform during the Berlusconi Government in 2005, words still current, albeit under reversed political conditions: Beyond the natural differences between the political groups, it would have been necessary for the majority to have sought first of all in the work of revising the Constitution the unity of Parliament on some shared values and on the fundamental rules of public life. I believe that nothing more harms the future of the country than the breaking of the principles on which our democratic coexistence rests. There is much uncertainty, perhaps even fear, in Italian society. Citizens need to recover with politics a stronger relationship, consisting of content and not just image, ideals and not just marketing, seriousness and stability and not uncertainty and precariousness. Senator Zanda, I reminded you of your thought that it's mine, too. Let us now go, if it is consistent, to remind its Secretary of the Democratic Party! [[Applause from the M5S Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u80	Senator De Pin is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u81	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, representatives of the Government, there are many points that can be made to the draft constitutional reform proposed by the President of the Council, Mr Renzi. First of all, as Professor Massimo Villone has observed, it is based on a series of completely unfounded assumptions. It is false that bicameralism, as it has been structured, causes particular delays in legislative activity. The simple consultation of the online site of the Senate shows that the average parliamentary process of conversion into law of a decree is two weeks, without considering that the decree-laws proposed by the Government immediately enter into force. These are times that are compatible with those of the other European Parliaments. It is false that the abolition of the elective Senate leads to considerable cost savings, since the part devoted to the salaries of parliamentarians is minimal compared to other items of expenditure that cannot be compressed (property, services, staff). It is false that the new Senate can exercise more effectively the functions of control towards the Government. On the contrary, because it will be largely composed of people whose administrative career depends precisely on government funding. Any real dispute with the Executive will be very unlikely. Finally, it is false that the reform in the pipeline aligns us with the other European countries in which a high House, totally or partially elected, is the prevailing rule. If we then go into the substance of the measure, we need to note a number of inconsistencies. I'm just going to list the most obvious ones. The powers of the new Senate are contradictory: on the one hand, it cannot give confidence to the Government or intervene in a binding way on spending laws, but it will have a say in constitutional reforms and the election of the President of the Republic. The new Senate will consist of a local political class that is no less discredited than the national one. I come from a region still shocked by the story of the MOSE, which has involved mayors and regional councillors, precisely the categories destined to settle in the new organism where, moreover, they will enjoy an immunity that risks to configure itself as a gigantic sanatoria against the local rubies. With the new provisions, the balance of powers, typical of democratic regimes, will be greatly reduced. The majority (or perhaps the Prime Minister) will have the opportunity to choose the President of the Republic and five judges of the Constitutional Court, who with the five presidential nominations make two thirds of the college, thus allowing the Executive to do what it wants to do with the Constitution. The presence of a House elected without preference and a Senate of second election, combined with the increase to 800,000 signatures to request a referendum and to 250,000 for proposals of law of popular initiative, will make it even more difficult for the citizen to influence the decisions taken in the Palace. I could go on for a long time, but I will not do so either because it has been discussed a great deal in the Commission's work or because the underlying flaw in this reform proposal is not so much in this or that particular contradiction, but it is of a methodological nature. It is part of the constitutional reforms that have been promoted over the last two decades. All of them - the successful ones and the failed ones, the ones wanted by the center-right and those wanted by the center-left - united by the intention to favor the fortunes of this or that party, this or that political personality, in the absence of any vision of the long-term destinies of our country. If we look, instead, at the Constitution of 1947, we realize that it was designed to last over time. It is certainly the result of a compromise between the most influential political forces at the time (Catholics, Marxists and Actionists). The men who wrote it, however, did not aim at their re-election, but at creating the framework and the ground within which political activity was to be carried out in the following decades. Hence, I believe, the impression of rigour and seriousness which still conveys today the reading of our Constitutional Charter. Some points may be overcome, yet it is evident that the constituents were not animated by petty shop interests. Even when - unfortunately most of the time - constitutional principles have been disregarded, our Charter has maintained a very high moral value over the decades. His very presence indicates that Italy is not a country condemned to be led by inadequate and irresponsible ruling classes. At a dramatic moment like the post-war period, the Italians knew how to bring out their best virtues and expressed a serious and prepared political staff, who proved to be up to the serious tasks they had before them. Unfortunately, this seriousness and this level of awareness have completely failed in recent attempts at constitutional reform. What a difference between the passionate discussions of our constituents and the petty and interested way in which the latest reforms have been promoted! There are countless examples of this. Let's think of the sudden end of the Bicameral, when Berlusconi withdrew at the last moment from an agreement that seemed now signed. Or the introduction, in 2001, at the end of the parliamentary term, probably for electoral reasons, of the reform of Title V, which should have led to greater decentralisation and instead caused an increase in costs and bureaucracy. Or even the reforms desired by the center-right and fortunately rejected in a referendum: according to the Italian tradition for which everything is resolved to tarallucci and wine, they were announced after a lunch to the canederli of the so-called essays of the House of Freedom. Not to mention the recent introduction, almost unanimously, of the balance of the budget in the Constitution: an unholy decision, of which fathers already begin to lack and which, if really applied, will result in a fearful backwardness of the living conditions of the popular classes. All these initiatives are irremediably marked by improvisation. There is in them a hint of subterfuge, of deception, of petty and interested calculation, of play of the bussolotti from the village fair, at least how much, in the paper of 1947, there was a sense of seriousness and rigor. In 1947 the contrasts between the various political forces were harsh and harsh. However, they were determined by a different ideal view of the country's destinies. Today everything seems to be playing on the petty calculation of immediate interests. To use the terminology that Gennaro Sasso used with regard to Machiavelli and his thought, in 1947 we moved in a universe "principesco" where politics, even in the dramatic hardness of the conflict, had at heart the collective good; in recent decades, instead, we moved in the universe "mandragolesco," where what counts is only the particular profit. Is it an exception, in this context, to the reform proposed by President Renzi and which finds its strength in the abolition of the elective Senate? The answer can only be negative. It is evident that opportunism pushes for such a rush to reform. At the moment, the easiest way to get consensus is to present itself as champions of political cost cuts. The cancellation of the elective Senate allows you to get a cheap popularity. In this regard, I would like to make a few comments. I would like to point out that, as my hopes of re-election are almost the same as zero, I have no material interests at stake. When I read the book of Stella and Rizzo, "La casta" in 2008, I was very impressed and scandalized by the data and arguments of the two journalists. I have partly changed my attitude today. I am inclined to believe that, in this particular historical moment, the real danger is not so much the costs of politics, but the possibility that politics will be cancelled. This will create - and this has already happened in many respects - a post-democratic vacuum in which only demagogies and qualunquism can flourish and in which, above all, supranational financial powers will be free. Of course, the costs of politics must be limited and it is possible to do so in many ways, from the reduction of Members' salaries, to the reduction of electoral refunds, to the amalgamation of the smaller municipalities. [[Call the president back.]] Mr President, I have 20 minutes to speak. [[Senator De Petris' comments.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u82	You can speak for 20 minutes, Senator De Pin, but your group had signed you up to speak for ten minutes.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u83	[[Senator Bulgarelli's comments.]] DE PIN (Misto-ILC). Certainly the costs of politics must be limited, as I said. Many other things could be done and not because these cuts have an effective impact on the financial conditions of the country, but because it is necessary to give a signal of austerity when the Italians are forced to pull the belt. The risk, however, is that, with the excuse of the cost cuts of politics, ordinary citizens are denied the already scarce possibilities to intervene in the decision-making processes. But how negative all this is, unfortunately, you only notice at games made. Even in these last reforms, therefore, a great deal of improvisation can be recognized. The fact, however, that we move in a disorderly way, with a lack of typically Italian design, does not mean that these initiatives must be taken under the leg. They too, in fact, obey, although confused and probably unaware - hopefully - a project, which is unfortunately not only Italian. Neoliberal rationality, which is imposed in the world, subverts the systems of traditional liberalism. At the basis of neoliberal reasoning, the market is not, contrary to what is believed, a natural fact. It is a built reality that requires the direct intervention of the State. Its task is to create the conditions best suited to free competition and to ensure that all economic players respect them. In order for the State to be able to fulfil its role at its best, it itself must comply with the rules of competition, become a company governed by private law and behave like a company. The universalisation of the principle of competition thus goes beyond the confines of the State and is also imposed on individuals, who are invited to be entrepreneurs of themselves. Neoliberalism has now become the dominant ideology of our society. It transcends the strictly economic sphere and influences, in an ever more stringent way, public life, the vision of the world, the behaviour of individuals. On a purely political level, it subverts the forms of traditional liberal democracy. In fact, neoliberal rationality tends to contract the area of public law in favour of private law; to conceive public action according to the criteria of profitability and productivity (and certainly not according to those of the redistribution of income); to strengthen the executive power over the legislative one; to promote a consumer citizen who is invited to choose between different political offers, in the same way as he could choose between different goods at the supermarket. This also has constitutional consequences. The principles of the anti-fascist constitution are being called into question. Everywhere the areas of the "State of exception" tend to expand, which is now becoming the natural instrument of government of many countries, starting from the United States, where anti-terrorism legislation has abruptly put an end to a tradition of strict respect for individual rights. Everywhere absolutely natural democratic instruments can be revoked at any moment: in Greece the request for a referendum to ask the people for the consent to a very harsh economic maneuver has caused the sudden fall of the Papandreou Government and its replacement with a Ministry of Commissariat by the troika. Everywhere the executive power tends to look with insufferance at the mediations of the parliamentary dialectic. Everywhere leaders claim to impose themselves outside of any constitutional mediation. Using the terms of Roman law we could say that the auctoritas, that is, the charism of politicians who turn directly to the people, presenting themselves as bearers of saving plans, has the upper hand over the potestas, that is, the power governed by legal norms. This trend, as I said, is everywhere perceptible and in Italy assumes (though confusedly) the forms of the current constitutional reform project. The abolition of the elective Senate, combined with an electoral law in which an abnormal majority prize is associated with an equally abnormal barrier threshold, is bound to lead to a drastic fall in the levels of democracy. Let it be clear, I am not accusing President Renzi of being a dictator; I do not even want to think that this acceleration is functional to the plan to go to rapid early elections, to secure a large majority and finally, obtained full control of Parliament, to impose on the country the terrible sacrifices that are demanded by the European oligarchies. I do not want to think so, because if that were true, it would be a project that would have little precedent in national history due to cynicism and irresponsibility. What I think it is necessary to denounce is a process, not linked to a man, but of a general nature, of progressive erosion of democracy. As far as I am concerned, I think that our Constitutional Charter, like all human things, is not something untouchable. She is the daughter of a world, based on Fordism and Keynesian compromise, which is very different from ours. I have not lost hope that a future European Constitution will integrate the immortal principles of 1947 with the new problems of the contemporary world: common goods, the rights of migrants, economic democracy, issues related to the right of citizenship and the dissemination of the Network. I believe that future generations of democrats will have to fight on this front. It is up to us to preserve what our Fathers have left us, tearing away with very hard sacrifices rights that are now at risk of being erased in general indifference. And if our attempts were to be unsuccessful, it is still necessary that these measures should not be adopted by two thirds of the votes and that they should be examined by the popular referendum. In Ireland, a measure very similar to what we are discussing was unexpectedly rejected by the electorate. Even in that country the abolition of the Senate was presented as a way to reduce the costs of the institutions. The oppositions set up their referendum campaign emphasizing that in this way the spaces of democratic participation were diminished and attention was diverted from the real problems (of an economic nature) of the country. It is not excluded that something similar can happen in Italy too. In any case, it is necessary to have respect for the people (and I stress respect). Measures of this nature cannot be the result of secret agreements between people, but they must necessarily pass to the judgment of the Italians. Preventing this would be unforgivable. For these reasons, too, I now declare my vote against the abolition of the elective Senate. [[Applause from the Mixed-SEL Group and Senator Candiani]]

Title V follows the logic of government centralization of powers. There is no serious remedy to the loss of competence that the regions would suffer. The State will be the sole decision-maker of the destiny of the local order, of the cultural and landscape goods, of the general norms on the cultural activities, of the tourism, of the production, of the transport, of the national distribution of the energy, of the general norms on the government of the territory and of the strategic infrastructures, only to give some examples. The reason behind the proposal is to prevent the regions from being able to legislate on these matters, with good peace of certain guarantees that the Constitutional Court had identified precisely in favour of territorial autonomy. Just think about the energy matter. Although the constitutional reform of 2001 attributed energy to the shared competence of the State and the Region, the Constitutional Court has long argued that the State can regulate the matter in its entirety, in the presence of interests of a unitary nature, but provided that the Regions are allowed to express themselves on the energy choices made in Rome through the instrument of the understanding. What the Government actually intends to do is to weaken the role that territorial autonomy enjoys within the constitutional system of the Republic. No one seems to wonder why our Constitution intended to inform the structure of the Republic of the principle of political-institutional decentralisation. Carlo Esposito, an eminent teacher of constitutional law, pronounced these words in 1954: The coexistence in the State of these centres of territorial life does not constitute, in our Constitution, a mere legal-administrative expedient or a useful instrument of good legislation and administration. They have a profound impact on the internal structure of the State and constitute an exercise, expression, way of being, guarantee of democracy and freedom for citizens. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u88	Senator Catalfo is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u89	Mr President, in emphasizing my esteem for all my fellow Members, I would like to take a moment, before speaking, to represent my esteem for a colleague who belongs to another parliamentary group and who I have listened to very carefully today. Intellectual honesty, precision and punctuality in the political and technical analysis of the constitutional bill, integrity, oratory art and courage, which have distinguished his speech today, are typical virtues of a senator worthy of being called honorable. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] So I thank Mr Corsini. In the current crisis situation, with the emergencies that our country is going through, we do not understand the urgency of the Government to change the Constitution. Who really wants this reform? Europe? Berlin? The Colle? High finance? When, in the post-war period, the Italians decided to draw up their Constitutional Charter, citizens were given the opportunity to elect a constituent Assembly. Every citizen thus had the power to elect their representatives and were elected people of the highest ethical and cultural profile. Among the 556 Constituents were Sandro Pertini, Piero Calamandrei, Teresa Mattei, Ferruccio Parri, Alcide De Gasperi, Benedetto Croce, just to remember some of them. All people who belonged to different political traditions and experiences, but whose cultural prestige and moral uprightness could not be questioned by anyone. These people were able to write a clear and balanced text that was the basis on which our democracy was built. The first article of this text states: Italy is a democratic Republic, founded on labour. Sovereignty belongs to the people, who exercise it in the forms and limits of the Constitution. It is no coincidence, Minister Boschi, that the constituent Fathers wanted to open our Constitutional Charter with the two sentences just mentioned, which summarize three founding principles: democracy, work and popular sovereignty. [[Applause from Senator Puglia.]] These principles bring us back to a single concept, which has a well-defined word: freedom. A Parliament which (as it is preparing to do) focuses on itself all the powers that affect the freedom of the people. This has a name: it is called despotism. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] Citing Montesquieu, they are the laws that must conform to the life of the peoples and not the peoples to the laws of the people, especially if they are laws made for the use and consumption of a political, economic and financial caste. The life of peoples demands respect: respect for human and social rights, respect for human dignity, respect for the thought and will of communities. "Comunità" is the concept so much emphasized by Olivetti and that this Italy of politics and banks seems to have forgotten. But let us return to popular sovereignty. What are the elements of reform of the recent constitutional bill that would allow the people, the citizens, to exercise the supreme power? The non-electivity of the Senate? The abolition of bicameralism? The increase in the number of signatories needed to propose repealing referendums, from 500 000 to 800 000, without the elimination of quorum? No. In fact, none of the reforms envisaged will be able to facilitate citizens' participation in the affairs of the State, which will increasingly remain the prerogative of a few, closed in the Palaces of power. The Renzi Government proposes to limit universal suffrage to the Chamber of Deputies alone, while the members of the Senate would be chosen on the basis of a hybrid mechanism between the accumulation of posts (which unfortunately in our country aspires to become a real legal institution) and the restricted elections (i.e. the voters are only the holders of other important public political positions (unions and members of the Regional Councils). The abolition of universal suffrage for the Senate of the Republic is in effect a long step back in time (I would say to the nineteenth century), and precisely when the passive electorate belonged to few, reserved only to those who met certain requirements of census and power. In other words, the majority of the new Senate will be of equal line-up of the House, composed of politicians, already elected through electoral lists more or less precompiled and decided by parties where the only common denominator is obedience. The Renzi Government wants to bring about another innovation (let's call it so), the main one, that is the elimination of the perfect bicameralism. To alter the guarantee offered by equal bicameralism means to irreversibly alter Parliament's legislative capacity. As a result, all the apparatus that governs our constitutional bodies would be compromised; the operation of these bodies is in fact the result of a system of cross-checks. In fact, it is no coincidence that the Constitution guarantees itself, and its people, from the upheavals that are too rash with the mechanism of Article 138. The excuse of the current government is that by eliminating equal bicameralism there would be considerable savings for the state coffers. However, this saving, which is far from significant, would only be for the functions of the Senate and not for its costs. The other amendment concerns the number of signatories needed to propose a repealing referendum from today's 500,000 to 800,000. The truth is that even with this reform, the government is trying to drive politics further and further away from the citizens. The reform, in fact, is perfectly consistent with the fivefold increase in the number of signatories needed to propose laws of popular initiative ranging from 50,000 to 250,000. Here too the purpose is clear... I'm sorry government representatives are smiling. The citizens to whom democracy will be removed will smile less. Perhaps it is worth listening to those who represent the citizens in this House while avoiding a smile. One of the many examples, not even too recent (but we are not good at looking at the virtuous examples in Europe), is "Open Ministry" used in Finland. It is a platform where citizens discuss and write their laws and, if they collect 50,000 signatures, the laws must - I stress - be discussed in Parliament. It is a phenomenon that studies Europe, the Council of Europe, which studies the whole world, but Italy applies the opposite. [[Applause from Senator Bottici]] These are therefore the changes necessary to involve citizens again in the political life of this country. They are the same ones who agree to reform the Constitution with a condemned person in a final way. [[Applause from the M5S Group.]] Mr Boschi may not know who he is. How can we trust these people? Given this premise, what is the point of changing the Constitution? And most importantly, what does it do? Who benefits? In recent years we have been intoxicated by ever new and unexpected reform projects. First the problem was the form of government and in endless debates there were politicians and politologists engaged in describing the advantages of presidentialism, then of semi-presidency to French or of a German chancellor, then debates were introduced on hybrid systems that touched on mythology. Now the attention has shifted to Parliament and we are faced with the dilemma: bicameralism yes, bicameralism no. And the final result was this last proposal to introduce an Assembly of the Autonomouss, then reformulated as Senate of the Autonomouss, of course not elective: the people must have no voice! Already in the past some lawyers and politicians have tried to imagine in different ways the overcoming of equal bicameralism, identifying it as one of the many causes of parliamentary immobilism and the loss of centrality of our Parliament. Thus the choice for a non-elective Senate, of second level and representative of the Regions and local authorities, inspired, as they have also mentioned illustrious professors of public law, to the German model, with a function of guarantee in the process of constitutional revision and prevention of conflicts between the State and the Regions, without however clearly indicating the border and powers of this body. Moreover, those who cite German experience should remember that in Germany the Länder did not have problems comparable to the depressing spectacle offered by the members of the Councils and Regional Councils of many Italian realities. The scandals that struck the regional health care in Lazio, Lombardy, Tuscany and Campania, investigations opened by the courts of Rome, Milan, Turin, Naples, the unjustified and disproportionate electoral refunds of many advisors and above all the inefficiency and the normative chaos caused by many Regions, some of which have proved more committed to open debates on the colors of their flag and an anthem, compared to the realization of territorial development projects and services for citizens. These people have already shown that they are incapable: do we also want to reward them by creating a closed-number Senate where only they can be elected? The Senate must never be reduced to a closed club, but it must always be possible for citizens to choose their representatives directly and democratically. Reforms like this feed the idea, impressed in the minds of the Italians, that we are governed by a real caste. ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u91	Mr President, I will try to make my speech, but I do not hide that I will do so with extreme discomfort, because speaking in an almost empty Chamber and with the disinterest, to say the least, of the representatives of the Government gives a sense of the importance you give to the parliamentary debate, which is zero. Anyway, let's move on. Today, in this House, we are debating a bill that will have important repercussions on the political order and democracy in Italy. We are witnessing impotent plans to overturn our Constitution, by a Parliament explicitly delegitimized by the decision of Constitutional Court No. 1 of 2014. The responsibility of the PD is enormous, since it is allowing the implementation of the plan that was by Berlusconi and, indeed, today becomes its primary creator. The fact that it is not Berlusconi, but is the President of the Council of Ministers, President of the Council of the European Union and, not least, Secretary of the PD who takes over the witness of the authoritarian turning point is even more serious, because it neutralizes what should be the opposition opinion, of which we remain the only voice. Once again, urgent reforms, which this country needs, are sacrificed on the altar of dark interests. Once again we are voting for a reform which is certainly not aimed at improving the conditions of a country in economic and social difficulties, but at strengthening the power bodies and their self-referentiality to the detriment of the interests of its citizens. Let me remind you that this is a Parliament clearly delegitimized by the decision of the Council that deleted the Porcellum: it had to make a new electoral law quickly and then return to the vote, it certainly cannot prepare a profound revision of the Constitution, ranging from the cancellation of the Senate to the form of government. The draft law we are debating today is intended to change as many as 44 articles of the Constitution concerning both the form of state and the form of government. The secret agreements of the Nazarene signed between Renzi and Berlusconi to divide Italy are coming to the surface with this reform that has the objective of excluding the Senate from the partnership to the political address and from the trust relationship with the Government, entrusting to the Chamber of Deputies alone the revocation or the agreement of trust to the Government. Do You Remember? It was Berlusconi's drawing. A drawing that now finds its place in the inauguration of a system of investiture democracy, of man only under command, with the aim of sweeping away all forms of debate or dissent. It is increasingly clear that outside the steel pact between PD and Forza Italia the Government does not intend to touch a single comma of this text. Italians must know that the rules of the game are writing them in two, Renzi and Berlusconi. Why keep the deal a secret? When he talks to us he wants to stream and when he meets Berlusconi he does everything in secret? In the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, the 5 Star Movement presented more than 220 proposals for amendments and sub-amendments, and they were all, and I say all, rejected. However, these were changes aimed at reducing costs, cutting the number of parliamentarians and their allowances, and introducing forms of direct and participatory democracy that allowed a form of control of the citizens over politics. Democracy and participation, already: they should be the foundation of this country, as described in Article 1 of the Constitution. Among the various instruments that we have proposed there are many useful ones: from the proactive referendum to the preferential lane for popular legislative initiatives. Instead, these instruments are unwelcome to the Government, as can be seen from the text of the draft law under consideration, which in itself does not say anything or almost anything about the means of direct participation of citizens, or rather limits their prerogatives (see increase of signatures required for referendums). President Renzi, we realize that you, like your fellow reformers Berlusconi, have little tolerance for the system of guarantees and counterpowers enshrined in the Constitution, from the transition from monarchy to the Republic, from fascism to democracy, and this is not only told by us of the 5 Star Movement, the only popular force that defends the rights of citizens, they also tell you illustrious constitutionalists, the same ones that you also audit in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. The reform of the Parliament that you want to approve is aimed exclusively at the structure of the Senate, which already outlines the unbalanced approach of the establishment of the draft constitutional law under consideration. Only the House of Deputies - which remains totally unreformed in functions, competences and the numerical dimension - becomes the holder of the representation of the nation and the political address. The reversal of the bicameralism of equality and the current structure of relations between the Government and Parliament that this reform intends to bring, the transfer to the State of exclusive competence on matters so far attributed also to the Regions will re-propose the same conflict and confusion that is expressly intended to overcome in different fields and functions. In fact, this reform draws an imperfect diarchy that delivers into the hands of the governmental majority not only Palazzo Chigi, but also the two main institutions of control and guarantee: President of the Republic and Constitutional Court (and it also has repercussions for the Supreme Council of the judiciary), which I think is enough to say that we are starting a process of regime that has no comparison in the comparative panorama. After all, your goal you reveal from the beginning of the text of the bill. It is enough to see Article 1 of the reform, which amends Article 55 of the Constitution, on Parliament, according to which the House represents the nation, while the Senate does not represent the nation, nor the citizens, nor the people, but represents the territorial institutions. With this bill you intend to remove the Senate from all ties with the people, the citizens, and make it a mere representative of the institutions. The Senate will no longer have the theoretical obligation to represent the interests of the citizens, but rather that of abstract institutions which have appointed the senators themselves, like their respective regional councils. With this reform you intend to assign the entire legislative function to the Chamber alone and assign a series of illusory powers to the Senate (the so-called proposed modifications), which will always be surpassable in the last instance by the House. Combining this reduction in the powers of the Senate with the electoral law that you propose, the Italicum, which assigns to the majority in the House a prize in important seats, would be a good game to make the Senate little more than a consultative body (though defined in the text as a proposition) by taking away all counterpower to it. In the new non-elective Senate, the whole system of incompatibility, based on the prevention of conflicts of interest arising from the mixing of different positions, would be missed. Moreover, the effective power of control and the power of inquiry would be entrusted only to the Chamber, whose electoral law, together with the trust report, makes it a body strongly aligned with the Government. And the need for counterpowers is the basis of the Constitutional Charter on which our country and modern democracies are founded. You want to concentrate power in the hands of one organ and one coalition: something unthinkable for a liberal democracy, and a basis for what many have already defined as a design for an authoritarian democracy. What would happen if all the Presidents of the Region in a given election round were all an expression of the same political force? It's happened before! With this bill you violate Article 1 of the Constitution, which I take the opportunity to remind you (many of my colleagues have already done so, but perhaps it is worth repeating this over and over again in this House): Italy is a democratic Republic, founded on labour. Sovereignty belongs to the people, who exercise it in the forms and limits of the Constitution. Sovereignty belongs to the people, and therefore laws must be passed by elected representatives of the people and not by elected persons to other positions that the part-time senators do. Article 1, which proclaims popular sovereignty, also appears to be violated in the proposal to raise the minimum threshold of signatures necessary for proposals for popular initiative law, which would increase from 50,000 to 250,000. You are concerned to justify this restriction by claiming that such legislative proposals would be guaranteed times, forms and limits; which is a mirror for the lark, since it is in the meantime not intended to facilitate (as it should) but to limit the popular legislative initiative. The signatures needed for referendums requiring 800,000 signatures are also increased, with a preliminary opinion on eligibility given by the Constitutional Court after the first 400,000 signatures. The attempt to delegate to a caste which is not directly responsible to the citizens, both legislative power and executive power, along the lines of the dark decision-making mechanisms of the European elite, is becoming increasingly apparent; mechanisms which lead to results such as those we see in Greece, and which at the same time keep the direct leaders of these choices hidden. The authoritarian turning point you aim to achieve is confirmed by the simultaneous weakening of the judicial counterpower. If the objective of protecting the caste from the control of the citizens is achieved by limiting the instruments of popular initiative and eliminating the electivity of the Senate, then it remains only to preserve it from the control of the judiciary. But you in this bill also think about this. Article 6, which amends Article 68 of the Constitution on parliamentary immunities, provides that senators (also in the new composition) and Members continue to maintain the same prerogatives as before. According to the Council of Europe, Italy is among the most historically guaranteed countries: the level of protection provided by Rome is comparable to that of modern democracies such as Albania, Belarus, Georgia and Russia. The senators, in particular, will be regional councillors and mayors, and when they are appointed senators (and no longer elected, let's remember) they assume the status of "immunized," i.e. they cannot be intercepted, searched, seized or arrested without the prior permission of the Chamber. In the light of what is happening - and I am referring to the scandals that daily involve representatives of PD and FI, from MOSE to Expo, to the latest condemnations for false ideology (see the President of the Emilia-Romagna Region Errani) - do you think that this reform protects the public interest, the citizens? So you want to discreetly appoint senators who will then be immune from any investigation? The immune senators will no longer be elected by the citizens, but appointed by other politicians and the President of the Republic. I ask you again: what is the public interest protected by this whole reform? Let us turn to Article 10, which amends Article 72 of the Constitution, and let us talk about the 'gigliottina'. The Government may request from the House that a bill, indicated as essential for the implementation of the Government program, be entered with priority. This bill must be put to the vote within 60 days. If, after 60 days, the vote has not been taken, the Government may request an item-by-article vote, as proposed. With this change, the Government legalizes the famous "gigliottina" to the parliamentary debate, introducing a requirement of essentiality of the bill. For those with short memories, the "gigliottina" has recently been applied by the President of the Boldrini Chamber to give 7.5 billion public money to private banks. It introduces the institute of the "vote to certain date" aimed at guaranteeing certain times of approval of the bills considered important by the Government and then introduces the "substitution of supremacy." The latter, which can be traced back to Article 10, is the one that summarises in a crystal clear way the zeroing of the political and decision-making role of the new Senate of the Autonomouss, which, in fact, is worthless and does not count for anything. According to this clause, "on the government's initiative" the laws of the State on matters that are not the exclusive competence of the State (where therefore there should be the veto of the Regions or municipalities), can exceed the limits envisaged and prevail over the local autonomy. The vote on a certain date is aberrant, because it removes Parliament's umpteenth prerogative, which is precisely in its name, 'Parliament', which is to discuss until we reach the right and thoughtful decision on the law to be adopted. With this change, however, the discussion and comparison are totally eliminated, only to be able to execute orders and be peremptori. It will be entirely at the discretion of the Government, which will decide arbitrarily whether a bill is essential, therefore "glowable" or not. In this way we intend to prevent the parliamentary debate, which is fundamental in democracies all over the world. Once again, what is the public interest that you intend to protect with this change? Remember that every law you promulgate must be for the benefit of the citizens, who are our employers. Where, however, there is no peremptor, and this is always in contempt of the Constitution, it is about the amendment in the matter of the decree of urgency (Article 77 of the Constitution). You really hit the bottom here. In the explanatory text of this bill it is quietly explained that, in order to avoid excessive recourse to the decree of urgency, the system of voting is used on a certain date and it is therefore officially declared that the Executive up to now has made an abnormal and out-of-law use of decree-laws. Then there is an amendment that removes itself the connotation of urgency to the decree-laws establishing that, in case of referral to the Chambers by the President of the Republic of a bill of conversion of decrees-laws, the Chambers have a time limit of another 30 days for the modification. But then, if the days to convert a government bill go from 60 to 90 days, do you explain to me where the urgency is more recognizable in a provision that can take 90 days to be approved? In essence, with the vote at a certain date, the Commission is requiring Parliament to adopt a law, the law that the Commission wishes to adopt, in the time required by the Commission for the legislative process. Similarly, in the case of the measures which it itself proposes, the Commission is granting itself a longer time limit for approval. Too bad the constitutional requirement for decrees is urgency. In addition to the changes I have mentioned, which all go in the direction of what is an authoritarian design, there are substantial errors, which lead to ridicule: I take the example of Article 16, which amends Article 82 of the Constitution, on parliamentary investigations. Only the House will be able to arrange investigations on all matters of public interest, while the Senate can arrange investigations only on matters relating to territorial autonomy. How does the Senate, which according to your authoritarian plan would become made up of representatives of the territorial authorities, carry out investigations into issues that would concern the territorial authorities themselves? Not to mention the senators appointed by the President of the Republic: such senators, as it is easy to understand, will have no connection with the system of autonomy on which the bill is based. The term of this term, equal to that of the President of the Republic, is therefore released from the term of office of the other senators. Senators for life (transitional provisions) are abolished, and this is perhaps the only point on which we can agree. Obviously, in order not to displease the "Fathers of the Homeland" (or perhaps it is better to say the crisis and unemployment) like Monti, Piano or Ciampi, the current senators will remain in office for life. To them will be added, always for life, the future Presidents of the Republic, and then another Father of the Homeland as Giorgio Napolitano, assuming that one day he renounces his throne. In conclusion, then, we will have: a Senate of unelected and paperweight without a real power of protection of regional interests and control over the work of the House; a Chamber of single-colored nominations under blackmail of the Government; a Government able to impose the political agenda through guillotine and its own puppet to the Presidency of the Republic; a President of the Republic never more guarantor, because elected by a single party. It is therefore clear that this bill has the sole objective of worsening - and not least - the functioning of the already unstable parliamentary institutions: to the crisis of legitimacy of Parliament you react by transforming the Senate into an elitist Chamber; to the unconstitutionality of the Porcellum you respond with an unconstitutional electoral law. If this evil and evil bill, which aims to destabilize the political balance forever, were to pass, from that moment on in this nation will prevail only the reasons of the strongest, of those who have the most control and power; the guarantees will end for everyone, also for you and for your family members and for the generations who will descend from them. You will betray your duty to work for the common good; you will fall short of the most important reason why you are in this House, envisaged and wanted by the Constitution, regardless of political membership, which is to cultivate a far-sighted vision for our country and to ensure a future for our young people, to guarantee and preserve them a future. When I was elected, I thought I should fight here, in these classrooms, to get the right laws passed for citizens, to defend the right to work, to health. But never, believe me, I would have thought I'd be defending the Constitution today. How is our Constitution built? It has been said several times: it is built with balance. Italy, until some time ago, was in fact a modern state, but now it is no longer, and not because of the Constitution, but because of the systematic violations that have been made to the Constitution in recent years. Our Constitution takes pluralism as a polar star: pluralism of ideas, which must be able to express themselves freely. I understand that every now and then there are some innocent people who have to pay the price in front of so many culprits who will never pay him. However, our Constitution has had the merit of urging our people to be the protagonist of the construction of the community and its future; of the national community and its future; of the national and European community and of its future. Therefore: pluralism of central powers; distinction between executive, legislative and judicial very strict; plural powers between central and peripheral, with the protagonism of local autonomy. It is in Article 5 that we violate the principle of this operation. I repeat, we violate him. Article 5 states that it is national legislation which adapts to the development of self-government and the administrative decentralisation of State offices. There is the value of the protagonism of our territories, which, in this case, in the proposal is offended. It is for this reason that I do not agree, because this so-called reform project renounces to be within the channel that is marked by the Part before the Constitution, that is, the one where the constituent principles of our Republic are written in a non-modifiable way. Responsible participation, the one that calls for right-duty starts from that: every citizen has the right-duty to participate in public life. It cannot be excluded, nor limited; indeed, we must support it in this effort because it is the participation that has made this country great, it is intelligence. In Italy there was, as a main example of a living and capable economy, the small, the very small and the micro-enterprise, the one he invented, because he participated in the citizens, felt the country as his own. Now I travel the streets and we are in despair: four million unemployed; you see no one. It is a dying country, struck to death by the television broadcasts you participate in in mass, enriching those who sell fake images. This is intolerable. We're all asleep; we're not capable of reaction! There is more: where the bombs rain, where there is an unacceptable and intolerable human condition! This is a country asleep, dying, vegetable. What do we do to help him? We delete popular participation from everything; we continue to administer sleeping pills, even heavy drugs that fall asleep for the end of our people, rather than urging them to be an integral part of this effort of reconstruction and rebirth. This is the responsibility that you take. You assume the responsibility of excluding those who cannot be excluded because they are the sovereign. The sovereign is no party secretary. It may be powerful for some time, a few months or a few years, but the sovereign in a democratic state, in a society even offended by tyranny, the sovereign who is hiding, but first and then wakes up, is the people and cannot be excluded from this so-called initiative of reform of the Constitution and rewriting of the fundamental Charter of our Republic. If you do the math, you better find a way not to approve this thing with two thirds, because you have to leave the people responsible for the decision and you have to encourage the debate. You must encourage the discussion in a serious, transparent way, as it deserves a real reorganization of our regulatory system, which is needed, but in order to eliminate everything that is not needed, that is so much and that we produce in industrial quantity every day. You know this better than I do: we arrive at the Commission, we adopt regulations on 2 July and we change them on 2 September; we approve them on 2 September and we return them again on 2 October. Let's make a regulatory production crazy! We should stop for a moment, erase all the unnecessary, reorganize, understand what must be saved and give rise to our capacity to revive economic activity, to our ability to produce, to our sense of community, to our capacity for cooperation and relationship with others, which would be so useful today also because of the international crises that we face. I would just like to say these things in order to make my contribution, which will not be lacking, Mr President, even in the amendments. I have left out talking about Sardinia because I do not want to be monothematic, but it is in my thoughts and it cannot be excluded that it does so in the continuation of the debate.
