ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u98	Senator Cotti is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2014-07-14-LEG17-Senato-sed-277.ana.u99	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the constitutional bill we are examining today is not the result of a thoughtful examination of the needs of our people; it is not a response to the need to improve our legislative system; it is not a measure to rationalise or reduce the costs of politics: it is nothing more than a hasty attempt to adapt our form of Republic to the needs of a national Prime Minister. Already, national mayor: it is not a term used at random, it seems that this new institutional conformation wants to approach the management model of a municipality, concentrating as many powers on the central government, with the excuse of giving the country an accelerated not towards greater efficiency, but towards a debasement of democracy. The plan that emerges is that of a Parliament more submissive to the Government, of local authorities (in particular the Regions) deprived of legislative power and of functions transferred to a central government, with a Parliament formed by a fake Senate, made of nominations that do not disturb the maneuvers, without however reducing the costs in a meaningful way, and a surviving Chamber, still of elephant composition, without cuts of salaries or costs; with the institution referendary and that of the laws of popular initiative developed and crippled by a huge increase of the number of signatures necessary for the proposals of the citizens, always in order not to disturb those who command. But it is on the relationship between the state and the regions that I intend to focus my attention. This draft Constitutional Reform Act provides for the revision of Title V, Part II, of the Constitution, but the proposed installation in the current reform is certainly not functional to the solution of the problems caused by the 2001 reform. In substance, the reform provides for the abolition of the concurrent legislation between the State and the Regions, the one in which the State dictated the principles, with framework laws for reasons of homogeneity, and the Regions implemented them with their laws. It is clear that in this way decisions on our territories, on our lives, would be significantly removed from the people. The Declaration on Regionalism in Europe, approved by the Assembly of the Regions of Europe (ARE) on December 4, 1996, supports article 3, paragraph 1, that the division of competences between the State and the Regions is governed by the Constitution or by laws in accordance with the principles of political decentralization and subsidiarity. By virtue of these principles, duties must be carried out at the level closest to citizens. It is clear that in Italy we are going in the opposite direction. Although the Declaration of the ARE is not a document of law value, it is nevertheless a charter of principles similar to the great declarations of principle often recalled by the world community. The new framework lists the new state competences that have been removed from competing legislation. If the problem that the reform aims to solve is that of the "those who do what they do and therefore of the litigation that is created before the Constitutional Court, blocking or invalidating many laws, it is not understood how this reform would resolve it. The new definition of competences does not seem to be resolving the problem in question: what work will be considered as "infrastructure provision" (regional) and what "strategic infrastructure" (state)? And with the envisaged supremacy clause, for which, on a proposal from the Government, the law of the State may intervene in matters not reserved for exclusive legislation when the protection of the legal or economic unity of the Republic, or the protection of the national interest, requires it, it guarantees a potentially almost absolute central power. The Government will be able to use the supremacy clause to impose itself on the Regions, by discreetly bringing any law within the concept of legal or economic unity of the Republic of the Republic or of protection of the national interest. With regard to the use of this clause, it is easy to envisage another crippling dispute, without saving any work at the Constitutional Court. We come to the theme that is most important to me: that of special autonomy. Without abolishing Article 116 of the Constitutional Charter (it is proposed to abolish only paragraph 3), the new legislation provides for a substantial emptying of the special autonomy. In fact, the changes provided for in Article 117 draw a scenario in which a long series of competences is transferred to the state level, which involve the same subtraction of competences to all the Regions, be they under special statute or ordinary statute. It is not possible to do otherwise, it is expected that the new legislation will enter into force, with regard to the Regions with a special statute, only after their mandatory adjustment of the respective autonomy cards, but autonomy risks remaining only the name. Only thanks to the choral disagreement from the regions concerned, the competent Commission has fortunately introduced the understanding for the adaptation of the Statutes of the Regions and the Autonomous Provinces of Trento and Bolzano, but this is not enough. The claim to cut a myriad of competences historically attributed to these Regions, for obvious historical and geographical reasons, undermines to the roots the institutional pact of 1948, which at the same time sanctioned the indivisibility of the Republic and the autonomy of the Regions with special Statute. Without the second (autonomy) he would no longer have the right to exist the first (individibility). Here again, I would like to recall the 1996 Declaration of the ARE, which always states in Article 3, paragraph 3: "Regions must be responsible for all functions having an eminently regional dimension" and in paragraph 6 of the same article explicitly states that "State decisions or measures concerning regional competences or interests cannot be adopted without the prior agreement of the regions concerned." But this reform precedes the agreements with the Regions, imposing them immediately to those with ordinary Statute and creating a unilateral precedent also with respect to the agreements with those with Special Statute, providing an understanding on adaptation rather than on new relationships to be rewritten together. In the Special Statute of my Region in Article 5, it is provided that "saves the competence provided for in the two preceding articles" - the list of matters on which the Region may issue legislative norms - "the Region may adapt to its particular requirements the laws of the Republic, issuing norms of integration and implementation on the following subjects: " - follows a list including - "d) in other matters provided for by laws of the State". From this example we can understand how impacting the weight of the reform could be, especially for regions with special status. One of the arguments I feel, in order to support the need to standardize the Regions with a special statute to the ordinary ones is that the Autonomous Regions misuse the funds they have. True; indisputable; but if that is the problem the victims are just us citizens of these Regions with special statute, not the inhabitants of the others! The inhabitants of these regions, also not a symbol of political and administrative virtues, suffer damage from the bad government of their own Region or State, which I do not think is to be erected as a symbol of great purity in comparison with the regional bad government. If something is badly administered the solution is not to entrust the administration to other bodies distant from the territory, but to change mechanisms of law and perhaps even political class. The loss of resources to the Regions with a special Statute would not increase the availability of the other Regions, because the increased competences that would be transferred simultaneously by the Regions with a special Statute to the State would end up absorbing, perhaps more than proportional, those same resources that someone imagines to share among the Regions all. However, let us return to the Regions with a special statute and in particular to the one from which I come: Sardinia. There are numerous political documents and positions in Sardinia that have characterized the recent debate on the division of powers between State and Region, or, if we can afford, between State and Country, since, according to the most common criteria of definition of the concept of Nation, our island can safely claim to fall into this category. It seems clear to everyone, from the content of the debate in progress, that the Sardinians consider the current powers conferred on Sardinia by the laws in force, as well as by their often questionable application; for this reason we find ourselves in the paradoxical situation in which, while we are going to ask for more autonomy, someone arrives who, without so many compliments but with an exemplary arrogance, proposes to parade from under our noses also that little autonomy implemented thanks to our fundamental Charter. Even among lawyers it is discussed whether a reform of the special autonomy, which in fact could reduce them to little more than a symbolic puppet, could be approved against the will of the autonomous region. During the hearings held recently at the Bicameral Committee on Regional Affairs on the subject of regional self-government, many invited experts (mostly professors of constitutional law) stressed the need to take account of the pactic nature of the set of rules concerning regional specialties. Some say that since 1948 the reasons of the Special Statutes have failed, but I do not think that in Trentino-Alto Adige it has ceased to speak in German and Ladin. In that region the recognition of autonomy is even prior to the same Constitution (with the De Gasperi-Gruber agreement), while in Sicily (with the oldest Parliament in Europe) the Statute existed prior to the Constitution. How do you adapt them to constitutional reform, as if they were any article of the same? And Sardinia? Have the reasons for the specialty failed? I tried to look at the map. I turned it upside down, turned and turned it around, but Sardinia, I assure you, continues to remain detached from the Italian peninsula. I also tried to search from the plane to see if some land language had by chance dried up a portion of Tyrrhenian, gathering in a contiguous territory Sardinia and Italy: nothing, the sea is always there. It is difficult to find, all over the world, islands of size and with the population of Sardinia that are managed by the State of belonging without some form of autonomy to adapt the law to the condition of insularity. Cases of self-government in Europe are found in Finland, France (with Corsica and Overseas Territories), Portugal (with Madeira), Great Britain (with Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland), Spain (with Catalonia, the Basque Country and Galicia). Find me in Europe a large and populous island like our Sardinia or Sicily that do not have special forms of autonomy. Moreover, if the construction of a road in Tuscany can also be useful to Ligurians who had to go to Lazio or to the Piedmontese who wanted to go to Campania - and therefore it can make sense to make the decisions of the case at national level - what sense does it have to decide in Rome which roads should be made in Sardinia or which energy sources we should use in Sardinia, since this would not interfere with similar decisions in the Peninsula? Since the birth of civilization, Sardinia has had to undergo dominations of all kinds and only due to a chance of history has it found itself part of the same State with the Italian Republic. Despite the succession of countless foreign dominations, our land has always managed to maintain forms of local self-determination, beginning with its language, much older than the Italian language, and its culture, which is rooted in the first forms of architecture evolved in Mediterranean Europe, over 3,500 years ago. What role is there for us? Still the one of the past, which saw most of the woods of our island brutally eliminated to supply wood the Peninsula? Or that of supplying meat-man for slaughter in military activities such as the carnage of World War I, of which we remember the sad stories of Emilio Lussu in "One year on the plateau"? Or as a prey to businessmen and coast cementors or as a place of experimentation of weapons and destructive military technologies in huge military polygons? I believe that a land like Sardinia, which today as always can be called a Nation, and which by historical chance is part of the Italian State, deserves a relationship with this State with the dignity that gave it the 1948 Constitution. Sardinia is a nation with its strong peculiarities, and this is testified by our flag, much older than the Italian flag and recognized throughout Europe; a testimony of our existence and our being, which cannot be simply adapted prior to understanding identity historically and geographically far from us. "Em a bolli donai a sa chi arrapresentat su governnu, sa bandera nosta. Speru diaderus chi s'arreforma de bosaterus sperefundata siat e aici puru is crecus de unu merixeddu schinnitzosu. I translate: I want to give to those who represent the Government our flag and I hope that his and your authoritarian reform will fail miserably, like the ambitions of a capricious Premier. (Senator Cotti, waving the Sardinian flag, approaches the benches of the Government and hands over the flag to Minister Boschi). [[Applause from the M5S Group.]]
